Factors: K in {3, 6, 10}; true P(documented feeder wrong) in {0.05, 0.15, 0.30}; true P(box feeds it but does not act) in {0.05, 0.15, 0.30}; true nearness (weight of a candidate outside the documented area, relative to one inside) in {0.05, 0.25, 1.0}; true hit rates in {as assumed; degraded = every check except `override_room` has tpr - 0.15 and fpr + 0.10, floored at 0.55 and capped at 0.45}; true minutes in {as assumed; every access time x 3; `duct_trace` cheap (access 3, do 5)}. The unlisted share is 0.10 throughout. **The tool's assumptions never change**: default `Settings()` (P(wrong) 0.15, P(acts-fail) 0.15, nearness 0.25, the default menu of rates and minutes). The tool chooses its checks with its assumed numbers; the simulator draws every answer from the TRUE hit rates and charges the TRUE minutes (both habits likewise). The three cells in which every truth factor equals its default (K = 3, 6, 10) are the ASSUMPTIONS-CORRECT cells; every other cell has at least one misspecified factor. No airflow symptom is used.

**Episodes and pairing (frozen).** 500 episodes per cell. The 500 truths are drawn once per cell (seed `[19, cell]`) and shared by all five policies; outcome draws use `default_rng([19, cell, policy, episode])`. Cells are indexed in the lexicographic order of the factor levels as listed.

**Metrics.** Per policy and cell: mean TRUE minutes to stop; accuracy of the final guess (the cause with the highest belief for a tool policy, forced to that when it stops unresolved; the concluded cause for a habit), counted as correct only for the exact cause (the exact wrong box, not merely "wrong"); mean checks; mean entries (1 + checks); for tool policies also the concluded rate, the unresolved rate and the wrong-and-confident rate (concluded and incorrect). Secondary, not classified: minutes per correct diagnosis (mean minutes / accuracy).

**Frozen classification, per cell, per tool policy T against each habit H.** d_min = mean minutes(T) - mean minutes(H); r_min = d_min / mean minutes(H); d_acc = accuracy(T) - accuracy(H); 95% paired-bootstrap intervals (1000 resamples of the episode index, seed 19) for d_min and d_acc. **WINS**: r_min <= -0.10 and d_acc >= -0.02 and the interval of d_min lies below 0. **LOSES**: r_min >= +0.10 and d_acc <= +0.02 and the interval of d_min lies above 0. **NEITHER** otherwise; flagged **TRADE-OFF** when r_min <= -0.10 (faster) but d_acc < -0.02. T is judged against BOTH habits: T WINS a cell only if it WINS against habit_A and habit_B; it LOSES a cell if it LOSES against either. The 10% and 0.02 margins are judgments made before running, not tuned.

**Frozen reading of the map, per tool policy.** W = fraction of the 486 cells won, L = fraction lost. **HELPS BROADLY** if W >= 0.60 and L <= 0.10; **DOES NOT HELP** if W < 0.20 or L > 0.25; **HELPS IN PART** otherwise, in which case the conditions come from the marginal tables (W and L by level of each factor; a level is reported as a strength if W >= 0.80 there and as a weakness if L >= 0.25 there). Also reported: the same fractions for the three ASSUMPTIONS-CORRECT cells alone (the best case) and for the cells with at least one misspecified factor (the robustness read); the head-to-head of `split_half` and `entropy` in the misspecified cells; the breakdown by true cause at the default K = 6 assumptions-correct cell; input burden (mean entries) by policy. The map is descriptive: 486 cells x 2 policies will misclassify some cells by chance, and no p-value is claimed.

**Predictions written before running (each is reported only as holds / does not hold, by the direction of W across the levels of the factor; none is a threshold).** P1: the tool's advantage grows with K. P2: it shrinks as the true P(documented feeder wrong) falls (fewer layout call-outs to save on). P3: `entropy` loses more ground than `split_half` as the truth departs from the tool's assumed prior (P(wrong), P(acts-fail), nearness). P4: making access expensive (x 3) widens the tool's lead, because it is location-aware and the habits are not. P5: with degraded hit rates `split_half` wins in at least as large a fraction of cells as `entropy`.

**Gates (all evaluated before any map is read; a failed gate means NO MAP).** G1 code path: with the truth at the tool's assumptions and true minutes = assumed minutes, the study runner reproduces `sim.simulate` (`entropy`, `split_half`, `random`) and `sim.simulate_habit` (`habit_A`) exactly (same minutes, checks and guess) on the same seeds. G2 accounting: for a scripted transcript the study's minutes equal the hand-computed sum under x 3 access times, and changing the TRUE minutes never changes which check the tool chooses. G3 honesty: in the three ASSUMPTIONS-CORRECT cells, accuracy among CONCLUDED `entropy` episodes is at least 0.92 (the stopping rule is 0.95 and the belief update is exact there, so a lower value means a defect, not a finding). G4 reproducibility: one cell run twice gives identical results. G5 completeness and pairing: all 486 x 5 results exist with finite minutes, and the truth lists of the five policies in each cell are identical.

**Not used:** real data, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, Mortar, or any fitted quantity. Nothing is tuned: the tool's settings, both habits and every margin above are frozen here.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The simulated technician answers each check independently from its hit rates; real errors are correlated (a faulty box misleads several checks in the same way), so real accuracy will be lower than the simulated one. (2) Truths are drawn from the tool's own model family; only the factors above depart from it. Structural misspecification (two simultaneous faults, dependent errors, a cause outside the list, a symptom that does not fit) is not tested. (3) Both habits are my constructions; a real technician's order may be faster or slower. (4) Minutes are placeholders, so only ratios and orderings mean anything; parallel waiting (doing another check during a 30-minute room response), disruption to occupants, repeated checks and numeric readings are not modelled. (5) One connection type (`vav_zone`), 3-10 candidates, one stopping threshold (0.95). (6) Accuracy requires the exact cause, which is strict for the tool when several boxes tie. (7) A win on this map says the mechanism can save minutes under the stated assumptions, not that a technician would save any.

**What would follow.** HELPS BROADLY or IN PART: show the working model to technicians and use the interviews to place the real numbers on the map; if the strengths and weaknesses depend on a quantity technicians can state (locality of errors, check times), that quantity becomes the first interview question. DOES NOT HELP: reconsider the scope (more causes, a broader hypothesis space, or dropping the tool) before spending interview time. Either way, one further pre-registered step is needed before any real-technician claim: shadow-mode logging of real call-outs.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/study.py` (synthetic building and true-settings builders, the episode runner with TRUE accounting and answers, `habit_B`, the classification and the paired bootstrap), `scripts/62_troubleshoot_map.py` (about 486 x 500 x 5 episodes, roughly 25-40 minutes, run in the background; raw results pickled first, reporting re-runnable), `tests/test_troubleshoot_study.py` (one test per gate, plus the classification rules and `habit_B` ordering); outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot/`. `troubleshoot/model.py`, `session.py` and `sim.py` are not modified; a change to any of them after this addendum is logged here first.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-25, written after `troubleshoot/study.py`, `scripts/62_troubleshoot_map.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_study.py`, BEFORE the 486-cell run was started; none changes a level, a margin, a rule or a prediction).** (1) Bootstrap: one resample matrix per cell (1000 x 500 episode indices, seed `[19, cell index, 99]`), shared by all six comparisons of that cell. (2) The predictions are operationalised as follows, each on the labels "against BOTH habits" and each as a strict comparison of two W fractions: P1 W(entropy) at K = 10 > at K = 3; P2 W(entropy) at P(wrong) = 0.30 > at 0.05; P3 W(entropy) - W(split_half) among the cells whose truth departs from the tool's prior on all three of P(wrong), P(acts-fail) and nearness is smaller than among the cells that depart on none; P4 W(entropy) at access x 3 > at "as assumed"; P5 W(split_half) >= W(entropy) among the degraded-hit-rate cells. (3) A tool episode that stops unresolved (12 checks, or nothing informative left) is charged the minutes spent so far and counted with its highest-belief cause as its guess; ties go to the first cause in the order doc_other, doc_acts_fail, wrong boxes in candidate order, unlisted. (4) Accuracy requires the exact cause. (5) `random` draws from the same random stream inside `Session.status()` as `sim.simulate` does; the study runner keeps that call order, which G1 verifies. (6) Episodes are prefix-stable (the first n truths and the outcome seeds do not depend on n), so G4 is run as: re-run the first assumptions-correct cell with 50 episodes and require it to equal the first 50 stored episodes. G1 (30 episodes) and G2 (20 episodes) are evaluated again at run time, in addition to their unit tests. (7) G3 must hold in each of the three assumptions-correct cells, not pooled. (8) Cells run in parallel worker processes; the results do not depend on the number of workers (tested). (9) The report adds, beyond the frozen outputs, the WINS / LOSES fractions against each habit separately, the trade-off count, and mean unresolved and wrong-and-confident rates; they are descriptive. (10) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 23 study tests and 26 earlier tests pass, and six deliberately broken versions of the study code (a wrong win rule, ignoring the degraded hit rates, a wrong combination rule, a defect in the second habit, a disabled honesty gate, a wrong reading rule) were each caught. One unit test evaluated G3's own statistic (tool-only accuracy among concluded episodes, in the three assumptions-correct cells, 500 episodes each) before the run and it passed; its values were not recorded, and it compares no policy with another. A plumbing smoke run of 11 cells x 20 episodes wrote to a scratch directory and only headings and row counts were inspected; it showed that gate G5 assumed contiguous cell indices, which matters only for a subset run and was generalised (`expected_idx`) with no change for the full run. No comparison of policies has been read.

**Result of Addendum 19 (2026-09-25; `scripts/62_troubleshoot_map.py` run once as pre-registered, 306 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot/map_report.md`, per-cell `map_cells.csv`, raw `map_raw.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed (G3: accuracy among concluded `entropy` episodes 0.972 / 0.988 / 0.977 at K = 3 / 6 / 10, against the 0.92 bar). *Frozen verdict, per tool policy, against BOTH habits:* **`entropy` DOES NOT HELP (WINS 0%, LOSES 5%); `split_half` DOES NOT HELP (0%, 13%); `random` DOES NOT HELP (0%, 31%).** Against each habit alone (WINS / LOSES): `entropy` vs `habit_A` 0% / 0%, vs `habit_B` 43% / 5%; `split_half` vs `habit_A` 0% / 0%, vs `habit_B` 14% / 13%. The three assumptions-correct cells are NEITHER for `entropy` and `split_half`. No factor level is a strength (WINS >= 80%) for any policy; `split_half` has weaknesses at "hit rates as assumed" (LOSES 26%) and "`duct_trace` cheap" (28%). Head-to-head in the 483 misspecified cells: no cell where either wins; `entropy` loses where `split_half` does not in 2 cells, `split_half` loses where `entropy` does not in 44. *Predictions as operationalised:* P1-P4 "do not hold" and P5 "holds", but every one compares 0.000 with 0.000 because W is zero in every cell, so none of the five carries information.
*What the frozen verdict hides (my rule's blind spot, disclosed).* The classification has a label for "faster but less accurate" and none for "slower but more accurate". Against `habit_A` the tool is never at least 10% faster, and it is not a LOSS either because it is more accurate, so every cell is NEITHER although the two differ a great deal (means over cells, minutes / accuracy / wrong-and-confident: assumptions right (3 cells) `entropy` 32 / 0.972 / 2.1%, `habit_A` 19 / 0.853 / 14.7%, `habit_B` 38 / 0.927 / 7.3%; at least one assumption wrong (483 cells) `entropy` 46 / 0.913 / 5.6%, `habit_A` 27 / 0.719 / 28.1%, `habit_B` 47 / 0.795 / 20.5%). The verdict stands as written. *Exploratory descriptive cuts of the same runs, NOT pre-registered:* (a) `entropy` vs `habit_A` over the 486 cells: median +65% minutes and +0.177 accuracy; slower and more than 2 points more accurate in 96% of cells, faster at the same accuracy in 0%. (b) `entropy` vs `habit_B`: median -5% minutes and +0.081 accuracy; at least 10% faster at the same accuracy in 43% of cells, by cost level 52% as assumed / 77% with access x 3 / 0% with a cheap `duct_trace`, and by K 28% / 38% / 63% at K = 3 / 6 / 10 (P1's and P4's directions hold against `habit_B` alone; P2's direction is reversed: 49% / 44% / 36% at P(wrong) = 0.05 / 0.15 / 0.30). (c) The tool's confidence is honest when its assumptions are right and not when the checks are worse than it thinks: wrong-and-confident 2.4% with hit rates as assumed and 8.8% with degraded ones (3.8% / 5.3% / 7.6% at P(wrong) = 0.05 / 0.15 / 0.30). (d) By true cause in the default cell the tool takes about 20 minutes for a healthy documented box (`habit_A` 17, less accurate), 42 for a box that does not move (`habit_A` 10), and 62-106 for a wrong box (`habit_A` 26-54 at 33-74% accuracy, `habit_B` 50-150). (e) Minutes per correct diagnosis (secondary): median over cells `entropy` 47, `habit_A` 34, `habit_B` 55.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* In simulation, under placeholder minutes, the tool buys accuracy with time: against the fast habit (which stops after a cheap check and is often wrong) it costs about two-thirds more minutes for about 18 more points of accuracy; against the slow reliable habit it is about as fast or faster and more accurate, most clearly when access is expensive or there are many candidates. Whether the trade against `habit_A` is worth it depends on what a wrong diagnosis costs, which this study does not price. Its stopping threshold (0.95) is frozen, so the study cannot show whether a lower threshold reaches the fast habit's minutes at higher accuracy. Both habits are my constructions, answers are independent (real errors correlate, which would raise the tool's wrong-and-confident rate), and the truths come from the tool's own model family. *Not concluded:* that the tool saves technician time, that it is worse than trial and error, that any technician would follow it, or anything about real buildings. *Consequence as pre-registered:* "DOES NOT HELP: reconsider the scope ... before spending interview time." A single further step is warranted before acting on that, because the verdict rule cannot see the accuracy the tool buys: a pre-registered frontier over the stopping threshold (and, if the user wishes, an explicit cost for a wrong diagnosis), asking whether any threshold makes the tool at least as accurate as `habit_A` in at least 10% fewer minutes, or as fast as `habit_A` at clearly higher accuracy. Not written.

## Addendum 20 (dated 2026-09-25, written BEFORE the troubleshooting tool has been run on any real building layout): does the tool behave differently on the documented layouts of real, mostly institutional buildings? (layouts real; errors and answers simulated)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** The user wants to target well-documented buildings first (large offices, universities, hospitals, data centers) and asked for the test to be repeated on maps that carry what such buildings would have. The closest real thing in hand is the Mortar Brick models: real buildings, described in the Mortar paper as mostly large commercial buildings of a university campus plus some small non-campus buildings, each with a documented equipment list and documented room -> box and box -> air handler claims, which is all the technician-guided tool consumes. Addendum 19 used made-up layouts (one room, K boxes in two areas). This addendum replaces them with the real documented layouts; it does not use any timeseries, and the errors and the technician's answers are still simulated. It is NOT a test on real technicians or on real documentation errors, and data centers (no VAV boxes or rooms) are not covered.

**What the real layouts do and do not contribute (checked before writing).** The Brick graphs carry no location links at all (no floors, rooms-in-floors or adjacency; zero `hasLocation` statements in a 394-box building), so a layout supplies how many rooms and boxes a building has and which air handler each box hangs on, not where anything is. The candidate list for a room therefore has to be built by a rule of mine (below), and "same air handler" stands in for "nearby". Every room in every building has exactly one documented box (no room with two), so the claim structure is simple. Expect the results to resemble Addendum 19 at the matching number of candidates; the new information is the real spread of building sizes and handler groupings.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) The instrumentation census counts for the 28 Mortar buildings that document both connection types (rooms, boxes, air handlers, boxes per handler, from `mortar_census.md` and a re-count of the same Brick files; metadata only): 25 have at least 12 documented rooms (13 to 394; 1 to 11 air handlers; 2 to 86 boxes per handler); `bldg1` (8 rooms), `bldg22` and `bldg25` (2 rooms each) have fewer; 4 boxes of `bldg5` have no documented handler. (2) Addendum 19's results and exploratory cuts (frozen verdict DOES NOT HELP for all three tool policies against both habits; against `habit_A` the tool takes about 65% more minutes for about 18 points more accuracy; against `habit_B` it is at least 10% faster at the same accuracy in 43% of cells; its confident answers are wrong 8.8% of the time when checks are worse than assumed against 2.4% otherwise); the five predictions below were written with those in mind. (3) A timing-only probe (about 1.4, 4.3 and 11.3 ms per `entropy` episode at 8, 20 and 40 candidates). (4) The tool has not been run on any of these layouts.

**Maps (frozen).** The 25 Mortar buildings with at least 12 documented room -> box pairs, in numeric order of building number: `bldg2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 15, 18, 20, 27, 28, 30, 32, 34, 36, 37, 39, 40, 42, 43, 44`. Source: the Brick files in `data_cache/mortar__*.ttl` (metadata only). Equipment: air handlers, boxes (VAV, RVAV, CAV) and zones as the census classifies them; claims: each documented zone -> its documented box (`vav_zone`) and each documented box -> its air handler. Area (the nearness prior): a box's area is its documented air handler (the lowest identifier if several); a box with no documented handler has no area; a zone takes its box's area. Equipment is ordered by identifier.

**Candidate regimes (frozen).** For a room, the candidates are its documented box first, then the other boxes of the same air handler in identifier order, then the boxes of other handlers in identifier order (or, when the documented box has no area, all other boxes in identifier order, all with equal weight), capped at: **R_narrow** = 8 (the technician has narrowed the search to a handful of boxes) and **R_wide** = 40 (they have not). Nearness weight 1 for the same handler and 0.25 for another, as in the tool's default settings. Identifier order is a stand-in for physical nearness that the data cannot supply.

**Worlds (frozen; three truths, the tool's assumptions never change and stay at the default `Settings()`).** **W_default**: every truth number as the tool assumes (P(documented feeder wrong) 0.15, P(acts-fail) 0.15, nearness 0.25, hit rates and minutes as in the default menu). **W_documented**: P(documented feeder wrong) 0.05, everything else as assumed (a well-documented building whose drawings are mostly right; my guess, not a measurement). **W_stress**: P(wrong) 0.30, P(acts-fail) 0.30, degraded hit rates (every check except `override_room` has tpr - 0.15 and fpr + 0.10, floored at 0.55 and capped at 0.45), minutes as assumed.

**Cells, episodes and pairing (frozen).** A cell is (world, regime, building): 3 x 2 x 25 = 150, indexed lexicographically in that order (world outermost, building fastest). 500 episodes per cell: each draws a room uniformly from the building's documented rooms, then a cause from the TRUE prior over that room's candidates; the same (room, cause) sequence is used by all five policies (seed `[20, cell]`); outcome draws use `[20, cell, policy, episode]`. Policies, stand-in habits, accounting of TRUE minutes, metrics and the tool's stopping threshold are exactly those of Addendum 19.

**Frozen classification and reading.** The per-cell WINS / LOSES / NEITHER classification, the trade-off flag, the "against BOTH habits" rule, the 10% and 0.02 margins and the paired bootstrap are those of Addendum 19 (bootstrap seed `[20, cell, 99]`). Because Addendum 19's rule could not see accuracy bought with time, one SECONDARY label is added now, before any run: against a given habit, a cell that is not WINS or LOSES is **BUYS ACCURACY** if the tool takes at least 10% more minutes, is at least 0.05 more accurate, and the paired interval of the accuracy difference lies above 0. Reported as the fraction of cells against each habit; it carries no verdict. The map reading (HELPS BROADLY if W >= 0.60 and L <= 0.10; DOES NOT HELP if W < 0.20 or L > 0.25; HELPS IN PART otherwise) is applied per tool policy to each of the six (world, regime) groups of 25 cells and to all 150 cells. Also reported: mean minutes, accuracy, checks, wrong-and-confident and unresolved rates per policy and group; a per-building table for W_default (rooms, boxes, handlers, mean candidates, `entropy` / `habit_A` / `habit_B` minutes and accuracy, label); the relation between the number of candidates and the tool's minutes.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; reported as holds / does not hold).** P1: `entropy` WINS (against both habits) in 0% of buildings in every one of the four groups W_default and W_stress x both regimes (Addendum 19's pattern repeats). P2: against `habit_B` alone, `entropy`'s WINS fraction is higher in W_documented than in W_default and than in W_stress (pooled over both regimes, 50 cells each). P3: against `habit_B` alone, `entropy`'s WINS fraction is higher in R_wide than in R_narrow (pooled over worlds, 75 cells each). P4: `entropy`'s wrong-and-confident rate (mean over buildings) is higher in W_stress than in W_default in both regimes. P5: `entropy`'s accuracy (mean over buildings) exceeds `habit_A`'s in each of the six groups.

**Gates (all evaluated before any map is read; a failed gate means NO MAP).** G1 code path: with truth = assumptions the runners reproduce `sim.simulate` (`entropy`, `split_half`, `random`) and `sim.simulate_habit` (`habit_A`) exactly on a real-layout case. G2 accounting: true minutes are charged as hand-summed and never change which check is chosen or what is answered. G3 honesty: in W_default, accuracy among CONCLUDED `entropy` episodes (mean over the 25 buildings) is at least 0.92 in each regime. G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces the first 50 stored episodes. G5 completeness and pairing: all 150 cells x 5 policies present, finite, identical truths across policies. G6 layout fidelity: for every one of the 25 buildings, the built layout has as many rooms and distinct boxes as the census counts, every room has exactly one claim whose box exists, and every candidate list starts with the documented box, has no duplicates and has length min(cap, boxes).

**Not used:** timeseries, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, or any fitted quantity. The Mortar licence rests on the creator's email ("fairly certain" BSD-3-Clause; `data_cache/timeseries/LICENSES.md`); only aggregate statistics are written to `results/`, no layout is redistributed.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The layouts give sizes and handler grouping, not adjacency; the candidate rules and the nearness proxy are mine. (2) Errors and answers are simulated from the tool's own model family, so the results say nothing about real documentation errors or real technicians. (3) Mortar is a research corpus of mostly campus buildings; nothing here is known about hospitals, data centers or large offices, and data centers are outside the tool's connection types. (4) Placeholder minutes, assumed habits, independent answer noise, no parallel waiting and one connection type, as in Addendum 19. (5) The "well-documented" world is a guess. (6) A remote override from a control-system workstation, which such buildings may allow, is not modelled.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/realmaps.py` (Brick -> layout, eligibility, area proxy), `troubleshoot/study_real.py` (worlds, regimes, the cell runner on real layouts, secondary label, group readings, predictions, gates), `scripts/63_troubleshoot_real_maps.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_real.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_real/`. `troubleshoot/model.py`, `session.py`, `sim.py` and `study.py` are not modified.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-25, written after `troubleshoot/realmaps.py`, `study_real.py`, `scripts/63_troubleshoot_real_maps.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_real.py`, BEFORE the 150-cell run was started; none changes a building, a world, a regime, a rule, a margin or a prediction).** (1) The 25 buildings are computed by the eligibility rule (at least 12 documented rooms, numeric order) and the script stops if the result differs from the frozen list; it does not (checked). (2) The bootstrap uses one resample matrix per cell, seed `[20, cell, 99]`, 1000 x 500. (3) The predictions are operationalised as: P1 the WINS fraction of `entropy` (against both habits) is exactly 0 in each of the four groups W_default and W_stress x both regimes; P2 `entropy`'s WINS fraction against `habit_B` alone, pooled over both regimes (50 cells per world), is higher in W_documented than in each of the other two worlds; P3 the same fraction pooled over the three worlds (75 cells per regime) is higher in R_wide than in R_narrow; P4 for each regime, `entropy`'s wrong-and-confident rate averaged over the 25 buildings is strictly higher in W_stress than in W_default; P5 for each of the six groups, `entropy`'s accuracy averaged over buildings is strictly higher than `habit_A`'s. (4) G1 and G2 are evaluated at run time on the first frozen building's first room with a candidate cap of 8, and in unit tests; G4 re-runs the first cell with 50 episodes and requires it to equal the first 50 stored episodes (prefix-stable); G6 checks the layout structure first and builds the candidate lists only when it passes. (5) The candidate count reported per cell is the mean over its episodes of the size of the drawn room's candidate list. (6) A zone documented under several boxes would be claimed by its first box in identifier order; none of the 25 buildings has one. (7) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 14 new tests and the 49 earlier troubleshooting tests pass; six deliberately broken versions (a wrong secondary label, a corrupted layout, a wrong world, a disabled honesty gate, always-true predictions, a wrong regime cap) were caught, one of them at import time rather than by a specific assertion. A plumbing smoke run of 3 buildings x 20 episodes wrote to a scratch directory and only headings and row counts were inspected. No comparison of policies on these layouts has been read.

**Result of Addendum 20 (2026-09-25; `scripts/63_troubleshoot_real_maps.py` run once as pre-registered, 1085 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_real/real_report.md`, per-cell `real_cells.csv`, raw `real_raw.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (G3: accuracy among concluded `entropy` episodes 0.977 in R_narrow and 0.976 in R_wide against the 0.92 bar; G6: all 25 layouts faithful to the parsed documentation). *Frozen verdict (Addendum 19's rule, against BOTH habits):* **DOES NOT HELP for `entropy`, `split_half` and `random` in every one of the six (world, regime) groups and pooled over all 150 cells**: `entropy` WINS 0% / LOSES 0% and `split_half` 0% / 0% pooled, `random` LOSES in 82% of cells. Every one of the 50 W_default cells is NEITHER for `entropy`.
*The secondary BUYS ACCURACY label (registered before the run) is what the frozen rule could not show.* Against `habit_A`, `entropy` is BUYS ACCURACY in 100% of buildings in R_narrow in every world, 80% in W_default / R_wide, 52% in W_documented / R_wide and 100% in W_stress / R_wide; it is never WINS or LOSES. Against `habit_B` alone, `entropy` WINS (at least 10% faster at the same accuracy) in 100% of buildings in W_default / R_narrow, 72% in W_default / R_wide, 100% in W_documented in both regimes, 28% in W_stress / R_narrow and 100% in W_stress / R_wide, and LOSES in none; `split_half` does much less well against `habit_B` (0% in W_default / R_narrow, 4% in W_documented / R_narrow, 24% in W_default / R_wide, 64% BUYS ACCURACY in W_stress / R_narrow). *Means over the 25 buildings (minutes / accuracy; wrong-and-confident):* W_default R_narrow `entropy` 34 / 96%; 2%, `habit_A` 22 / 83%; 17%, `habit_B` 44 / 93%; 7%. W_default R_wide `entropy` 37 / 89%; 2%, `habit_A` 31 / 78%; 22%, `habit_B` 83 / 90%; 10%. W_documented R_narrow 27 / 98%, 20 / 86%, 36 / 93%; R_wide 28 / 96%, 25 / 84%, 56 / 92%. W_stress R_narrow 61 / 78%; 12%, 24 / 57%; 43%, 65 / 63%; 37%; R_wide 66 / 69%; 10%, 28 / 51%; 49%, 94 / 55%; 45%. `entropy`'s unresolved rate rises with the candidate list (4% in R_narrow, 11% in R_wide, 30% in W_stress / R_wide).
*Predictions as operationalised: all five hold.* P1 (0% WINS in the four groups) holds with 0.000 in each; P2 `entropy`'s WINS fraction against `habit_B` is highest in W_documented (1.000 against 0.860 in W_default and 0.640 in W_stress); P3 higher in R_wide (0.907) than in R_narrow (0.760); P4 wrong-and-confident is higher in W_stress than in W_default in both regimes (0.119 against 0.022 in R_narrow; 0.098 against 0.021 in R_wide); P5 `entropy`'s accuracy exceeds `habit_A`'s in all six groups (0.963 / 0.827, 0.895 / 0.781, 0.978 / 0.858, 0.956 / 0.843, 0.775 / 0.574, 0.690 / 0.515).
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) The real layouts change little, as expected: at matching candidate counts the numbers are close to Addendum 19's synthetic ones (W_default R_narrow `entropy` 34 minutes and 96% against 33 and 98% at K = 6 there). Within a regime, building size does not matter (bldg11 with 394 rooms and bldg42 with 13 give 34 and 33 minutes in R_narrow), because the candidate list is capped, not because the buildings are alike. (2) The one place the layouts matter is the candidate count: when the technician cannot narrow the search (R_wide, up to 40 boxes), `habit_B` slows from 44 to 83 minutes and `habit_A` from 22 to 31 while `entropy` goes from 34 to 37, at 89% accuracy against `habit_B`'s 90% and `habit_A`'s 78%; the tool's time hardly grows with the list, its accuracy falls a little and more of its episodes end unresolved (11%). (3) Against the fast habit the tool is never faster; it takes about 55% more minutes (R_narrow) or 19% more (R_wide) for about 11-13 points more accuracy, and it is the only policy that is both accurate and not slow. (4) In the stress world every policy is poor (`entropy` 69-78% accurate, and wrong when confident 10-12% of the time). (5) The Brick graphs carry no location, so "same air handler" and identifier order stand in for nearness; errors and answers are simulated from the tool's own model family; minutes are placeholders; both habits are my constructions; the "well-documented" world (P(wrong) 0.05) is a guess. *Not concluded:* anything about real technicians or real documentation errors; that the tool saves time; anything about hospitals, data centers or large offices (Mortar is mostly campus buildings); that a remote override from a control workstation, which such buildings may allow, would change the picture (not modelled).


## Addendum 21 (dated 2026-09-25, written BEFORE any run in which the tool's prior differs from its default): does telling the tool how far to trust a building's drawings make it better, and what does it cost when the technician's rating is wrong? (simulation only)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** The tool starts every complaint with a flat prior: the documented box is wrong 15% of the time in every room of every building (`Settings.p_wrong`). The user asked what a "sharper prior" would include and which levers can be built now; lever 1 of that list is a building-level rating of the drawings ("how far do you trust them?"), which costs the technician one answer. The user asked to build it and see whether it improves the tool. The information floor computed before this addendum (perfect checks, the tool's own prior) says the lever has room: about 1.7 checks at P(wrong) = 0.15 against about 1.3 at 0.05.

**What is built (before the run; additive, defaults unchanged).** `troubleshoot/model.py`: `DRAWINGS = {mostly_right: 0.05, mixed: 0.15, poor: 0.30}` and `Settings.with_drawings(level)`, which changes `p_wrong` and nothing else; `troubleshoot/cli.py`: `--drawings {mostly_right, mixed, poor}` defaulting to `mixed`. The three levels are ASSUMED placeholders, not measurements, and `mixed` is the existing default, so leaving the rating unset changes nothing (tested: equality of settings, of priors and of whole episodes). No other module of the tool is modified.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addenda 19-20: the tool always ran on its default prior. In the real-layout runs the default-prior tool took 27 minutes at 98% accuracy in W_documented (truth P(wrong) 0.05), 34 / 96% in W_default (0.15) in R_narrow; the same worlds at R_wide 28 / 96% and 37 / 89%; those numbers include the effect of easier worlds and say nothing about what a changed prior does. (2) The perfect-check floors above. (3) No run of the tool with a prior other than its default has been made. (4) Hand reasoning: when the documented box is right, one positive `override_room` on it is enough to conclude under either prior, so a sharper prior should mainly matter when the answer is NOT the documented box; the predictions below were written from that reasoning.

**What is tested.** The tool's setting is the rating r in {mostly_right (p_wrong 0.05), mixed (0.15, the default), poor (0.30)}; the strategy is `entropy` throughout. The truth is a world W with P(documented feeder wrong) in {0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.22, 0.30}; everything else in the world is as the tool assumes (P(acts-fail) 0.15, nearness 0.25, default hit rates and minutes, unlisted share 0.10), so only the prior varies. Primary worlds (on the rating grid): 0.05, 0.15, 0.30. Secondary worlds (between levels; no predictions, no verdict): 0.10, 0.22. Layouts, candidate regimes (R_narrow = 8, R_wide = 40), building list (the 25 frozen in Addendum 20), candidate rule and nearness weights are those of Addendum 20 (metadata only; no timeseries). The stand-in habits (`habit_A`, `habit_B`) are run for context and do not depend on the rating (they never read `p_wrong`; tested).

**Cells, episodes, pairing (frozen).** A cell is (world, regime, building): 5 x 2 x 25 = 250, indexed lexicographically (world outermost in the order 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.22, 0.30; then regime; building fastest). 500 episodes per cell: each draws a room uniformly from the building's documented rooms, then a cause from the TRUE prior over that room's candidates (seed `[21, cell]`). Policies per cell: `entropy` under each of the three ratings, `habit_A`, `habit_B` (five runs) on the same 500 (room, cause) draws; TRUE minutes are charged and the simulated technician answers from the TRUE hit rates, as in Addendum 19. The three ratings share one outcome stream per episode (`[21, cell, 0, episode]`); `habit_A` uses `[21, cell, 1, episode]`, `habit_B` `[21, cell, 2, episode]`. Bootstrap: one 1000 x 500 resample matrix per cell, seed `[21, cell, 99]`.

**Frozen label for a rating against the default rating in the same cell (paired episodes).** Time state: FASTER if the mean minutes are at least 5% below the default's and the paired 95% interval of the minutes difference lies below 0; SLOWER if at least 5% above and the interval lies above 0; else SAME. Accuracy state: MORE if the accuracy difference is at least +0.02 and its paired interval lies above 0; LESS if at most -0.02 and the interval lies below 0; else SAME. Label: **IMPROVES** = (FASTER and not LESS) or (MORE and not SLOWER); **WORSE** = (SLOWER and not MORE) or (LESS and not FASTER); **TRADE** = (FASTER and LESS) or (SLOWER and MORE); **NEITHER** otherwise. (The 5% bar is deliberately smaller than Addendum 19's 10%: a prior shift is a modest change and the larger bar would have called small real gains NEITHER; chosen knowing that.) Safety flag, separate: a cell has a **SAFETY REGRESSION** if the wrong-and-confident rate (concluded and wrong) is at least 0.02 above the default's with its paired interval above 0, a **SAFETY GAIN** if at least 0.02 below with the interval below 0.

**Scenarios (frozen; each is 50 cells = 25 buildings x 2 regimes, also reported per regime).** Against the default rating `mixed`: S1 matched-low: world 0.05, rating mostly_right. S2 matched-high: world 0.30, rating poor. S3 over-sure by one level: world 0.15, rating mostly_right. S4 over-sure by two levels: world 0.30, rating mostly_right. S5 too cautious by one level: world 0.15, rating poor. S6 too cautious by two levels: world 0.05, rating poor. (In world 0.15 the matched rating IS the default and is a control: every difference is exactly zero.) Secondary (reported only): worlds 0.10 and 0.22 with ratings mostly_right and poor, each against `mixed`.

**Frozen readings.** Matched scenarios (S1, S2): the map reading of Addendum 19 applied to the IMPROVES fraction (as W) and the WORSE fraction (as L): HELPS BROADLY if IMPROVES >= 0.60 and WORSE <= 0.10; DOES NOT HELP if IMPROVES < 0.20 or WORSE > 0.25; HELPS IN PART otherwise. Mis-rated scenarios (S3-S6): the cost fraction = the fraction of cells that are WORSE or TRADE or have a SAFETY REGRESSION; HARMLESS if <= 0.10, HARMFUL if > 0.25, COSTS SOMETHING otherwise.
**Break-even rating accuracy.** Model the technician's rating as correct with probability q and otherwise one of the two other levels with equal probability; the three primary worlds are equally weighted (a placeholder; nobody knows the real mix). From the scenario means (mean over the 25 buildings of the per-building mean, per regime) the expected change against always using the default is computed for minutes, accuracy and the wrong-and-confident rate at q = 1/3 (a random rating), 0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8, 0.9, 1.0, and the break-even q* is the smallest q on a 0.01 grid from 1/3 to 1 at which the change is favourable (minutes not above, accuracy not below, wrong-and-confident not above the default's), reported for each metric alone and for all three jointly ("none" if never).
**Decision (about offering the setting, never about shipping).** The setting is judged **WORTH OFFERING** (default stays `mixed`) if both S1 and S2 read at least HELPS IN PART and the joint q* is at most 0.75 in both regimes; otherwise **NOT WORTH OFFERING AS BUILT**. The 0.75 bar (the technician picks the right level three times in four) is a judgement of mine. A positive decision would mean only "worth putting in front of real technicians".
**Context, no verdict.** For each primary world and rating, `entropy` against both habits with Addendum 19's classification and Addendum 20's BUYS ACCURACY label, so that the effect on the earlier picture ("DOES NOT HELP against the fast habit") can be seen.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; reported as holds / does not hold).** P1: in S1, mean minutes (mean over the 25 buildings) are lower than under the default in BOTH regimes and mean accuracy is not more than 0.01 lower. P2: in S2, the mean wrong-and-confident rate is lower than under the default in both regimes and mean accuracy is not lower. P3: in S4, the mean wrong-and-confident rate is at least 0.02 higher than under the default in both regimes. P4: in S6, mean minutes are higher than under the default in both regimes. P5: at q = 0.70 the expected change is favourable on all three metrics jointly in both regimes.

**Gates (all evaluated before any reading; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 default-off: `Settings().with_drawings("mixed") == Settings()` and 30 episodes on a real-layout case are identical (guess, checks, minutes) under the two, plus Addendum 20's G1 (the runners reproduce `sim.simulate`). G2 accounting: Addendum 20's G2. G3 honesty: with the MATCHED rating in each primary world, accuracy among CONCLUDED `entropy` episodes (mean over the 25 buildings) is at least 0.92 in each of the six (world, regime) groups (a lower value would mean a defect, not a finding, as in Addendum 19). G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces the first 50 stored episodes of all five policies. G5 completeness and pairing: all 250 cells x 5 policies present, finite, identical (room, cause) draws across policies, and the two habits' results identical whatever rating the tool holds. G6 layout fidelity: as Addendum 20 for the 25 buildings.

**Not used:** timeseries, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, or any fitted quantity. Mortar licence as in Addendum 20: only aggregates are written to `results/`.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The three ratings equal the primary world levels, so "matched" is the best case: it assumes a technician's word means the same error rate to every technician; the two between-level worlds test only part of that. (2) Errors and answers come from the tool's own model family: a prior that is true helps mechanically, so what is informative is the SIZE of the gain, the cost when the rating is wrong and the break-even accuracy, not that a gain exists. (3) The rating error model (uniform over the other two levels) is a placeholder; real errors are probably ordered (adjacent more likely) and correlated with what the technician has already seen. (4) Equal world weights are a placeholder. (5) Nothing here is evidence about real technicians, real drawing reliability or whether technicians can rate drawings at all. (6) Lever 1 of eight; `entropy` only; `vav_zone` only; the acts-fail prior is fixed, so a rating of the drawings says nothing about actuators. (7) Everything from Addendum 20's limitations (no location data in Brick, identifier order as nearness, placeholder minutes, both habits my constructions).

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/study_rating.py` (worlds, cells, runner, labels, scenarios, break-even, predictions, gates), `scripts/64_troubleshoot_rating.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_rating.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_rating/`.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-25, written after `troubleshoot/study_rating.py`, `scripts/64_troubleshoot_rating.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_rating.py`, BEFORE the 250-cell run was started; none changes a world, a rating level, a label margin, a reading threshold, the decision bar or a prediction).** (1) The readings and the decision use S1 and S2 pooled over both regimes (50 cells each); per-regime shares are shown alongside but decide nothing. (2) The break-even grid is q = 1/3, then 0.34, 0.35, ..., 1.00. Because the expected change is linear in q and three conditions are combined, the favourable set need not reach q = 1; the report therefore gives, next to the smallest favourable q (the frozen q*), the largest favourable q and whether q = 1 is favourable, and the decision additionally requires q = 1 to be favourable (a perfect rating must not itself be unfavourable). The bar q* <= 0.75 is unchanged. (3) The predictions are operationalised as: P1 mean over the 25 buildings of the paired minutes difference (S1 rating minus default) is below 0 and of the accuracy difference is at least -0.01, in each regime; P2 the wrong-and-confident difference in S2 is below 0 and the accuracy difference at least 0, in each regime; P3 the wrong-and-confident difference in S4 is at least +0.02, in each regime; P4 the minutes difference in S6 is above 0, in each regime; P5 at q = 0.70 the expected change is favourable on all three metrics in each regime. (4) The per-cell "mean checks" difference is reported but enters no label. (5) Habits run once per cell on the default-rating case (their outcome streams are 1 and 2; the three ratings share stream 0); the run checks that the habits give identical episodes under every rating. (6) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 2 new tests for `Settings.with_drawings` and the CLI option and 40 for the study pass, the full doc_mismatch suite passes (405 tests); twelve deliberately broken versions of the study code (a swapped label branch, a flipped time-state sign, a wrong rating-error weighting in the break-even, a wrong matched-rating map, a flipped favourability sign, the wrong baseline rating, a flipped safety-flag sign, a prediction reading the wrong scenario, ratings not sharing random draws, a moved harm threshold, a cost that ignores safety regressions, a decision that ignores q = 1) were each caught by at least one test; the test for shared random draws was written before that check, because I saw that no existing test would catch a version in which the three ratings do not share random draws. A plumbing smoke run of 3 buildings x 20 episodes wrote to a scratch directory and only headings and row counts were inspected. No comparison of ratings on these layouts has been read.

**Result of Addendum 21 (2026-09-25; `scripts/64_troubleshoot_rating.py` run once as pre-registered, 527 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_rating/rating_report.md`, per-cell `rating_cells.csv`, raw `rating_raw.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (G3, matched rating, accuracy among concluded episodes 0.978-0.990 in the six groups against the 0.92 bar). *Frozen readings and decision:* S1 (world 5% wrong, rating mostly_right) **HELPS BROADLY** (IMPROVES in 100% of the 50 cells); S2 (world 30% wrong, rating poor) **DOES NOT HELP** (WORSE 56%, TRADE 44%, IMPROVES 0%); mis-rated: S3 HARMLESS, S4 COSTS SOMETHING, S5 HARMFUL, S6 HARMFUL. Break-even: **no q is favourable in either regime** (at q = 1 the expected change is +2.85 minutes in R_narrow and +3.20 in R_wide, accuracy +0.007 / +0.003, wrong-and-confident -0.008 / -0.009), so the decision is **NOT WORTH OFFERING AS BUILT**; the default stays `mixed`.
*What the numbers say (pooled over both regimes; deltas are rating minus default on the same episodes).* The two directions of the rating behave very differently. **mostly_right** saves 2.4 minutes (9%) in the 5%-wrong world, 2.7 (8%) in the 15% world and 3.3 (7%) in the 30% world, with 0.17-0.26 fewer checks; accuracy changes by -0.001, -0.003 and -0.008 and the wrong-and-confident rate by +0.002, +0.006 and +0.013; a safety regression (>= 0.02 with a clear interval) appears in 0%, 0% and 10% of cells. **poor** costs 11.5 minutes (+24%) in the 30% world where it is the right rating, 11.9 (+34%) in the 15% world and 12.3 (+45%) in the 5% world, with 0.8 more checks in each; it buys +0.016, +0.008 and +0.003 accuracy and -0.027, -0.014 and -0.005 wrong-and-confident (a safety gain in 86%, 22% and 0% of cells). Between-level worlds (10%, 22%) behave the same way. Against the habits (context, no verdict): the matched rating changes little in the earlier picture; the one visible change is 5% wrong / R_wide with mostly_right, where the tool ties `habit_A` on minutes (26 against 26) at 96% against 83% accuracy; with `poor` the tool loses most of its speed edge over `habit_B` in the 30% world (WINS in 20% of buildings in R_narrow against 92% under the default rating).
*Predictions as operationalised: P1, P2 and P4 hold; P3 and P5 do not.* P1 holds (-2.32 and -2.44 minutes, accuracy -0.002 and -0.000); P2 holds (wrong-and-confident -0.026 and -0.028, accuracy +0.023 and +0.010); P4 holds (+12.3 and +12.4 minutes); **P3 does not hold** (over-sure by two levels raised wrong-and-confident by 0.013 in both regimes, not the 0.02 predicted: the over-sure rating is less harmful than I expected); **P5 does not hold**, because even a perfect rating is slower on average once the slow 'poor' worlds are counted.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) The frozen decision is NOT WORTH OFFERING AS BUILT, and it is driven by minutes: the rule treats a 24% slowdown that buys 2.7 points fewer confidently wrong answers as WORSE/TRADE. Whether that trade is worth making is a judgement about the cost of a wrong repair that the simulation cannot supply. (2) The gain from the sharper prior is real but small: about 8% of the minutes and 0.17-0.26 checks, against an information-floor headroom of about 0.4 checks (1.7 to 1.3) computed before the run. (3) The asymmetry is the finding: telling the tool 'the drawings are mostly right' is nearly free even when the drawings are somewhat worse (S3, S4), while telling it 'the drawings are poor' makes it slower everywhere because it spends about 0.8 more checks before it trusts the documented box (interpretation; not separately tested). (4) Post-hoc, NOT registered and NOT tested: a one-way setting (only the 'mostly right' direction) would keep the gain and drop the cost, and would need its own pre-registered study before any claim. (5) All of it is simulation inside the tool's own model family, with rating levels equal to the world levels (the best case), an equal-weight mix of worlds and a uniform rating-error model, all placeholders; nothing is known about whether real technicians can rate drawings or about real error rates. *Not concluded:* that ratings help real technicians; anything about the other seven levers.


## Addendum 22 (dated 2026-09-25, written BEFORE any run of the one-way switch or of any stress world with a non-default prior): is a one-way "the drawings are mostly right" switch worth offering, and does it stay safe when the actuators or the checks are worse than assumed? (simulation only)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** Addendum 21 tested a three-level rating of the drawings. Its frozen decision was NOT WORTH OFFERING AS BUILT, driven by the `poor` level, which cost 24-45% more minutes in every world. The `mostly_right` level behaved differently: about 7-9% fewer minutes at almost no accuracy cost, even in worlds where the drawings were worse than rated. The user asked to pre-register a study of the one-way switch that keeps only that direction. **This study is post-hoc in origin**: it exists because of what Addendum 21 showed. It therefore cannot be independent confirmation of Addendum 21's `mostly_right` result (same layouts, same tool family, same simulator; fresh seeds only guard against a lucky seed). What it can add is what Addendum 21 held fixed: worse actuators, worse checks, a mild switch level, and how many wrong switch-ons the setting tolerates.

**The switch.** State **off** = the default prior (P(documented feeder wrong) 0.15, unchanged). State **on** = P(wrong) 0.05, exactly the `mostly_right` level of `model.DRAWINGS`; its level is NOT re-tuned. Secondary arm **on_mild** = 0.10, reported only (no decision). The technician's only act is to flip the switch on; nothing else in the tool changes.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) All of Addendum 21, including per-world results of `mostly_right` against `mixed` at 5%, 10%, 15%, 22% and 30% wrong drawings with everything else as assumed (pooled: -2.4, -2.7 and -3.3 minutes; accuracy -0.001, -0.003, -0.008; wrong-and-confident +0.002, +0.006, +0.013 at 5%, 15%, 30%; safety regression in 0%, 0%, 10% of cells; at 10% and 22% the same picture). (2) Addenda 19-20: the default-prior tool in the harsh world (30% wrong, 30% acts-fail, degraded checks) took 61-66 minutes at 69-78% accuracy with 10-12% wrong-and-confident. (3) No run of a non-default prior in any world with a different acts-fail rate or degraded checks has been made. The predictions below were written with (1) and (2) in mind.

**Worlds (frozen).** Truth = P(documented feeder wrong) in {0.05, 0.15, 0.30} crossed with four stress conditions: **X0** as the tool assumes (P(acts-fail) 0.15, default hit rates: the world set of Addendum 21); **X1** actuators fail more (P(acts-fail) 0.30, default hit rates); **X2** checks worse (P(acts-fail) 0.15, hit rates degraded as in Addenda 19-20: every check except `override_room` has tpr - 0.15 and fpr + 0.10, floored at 0.55 and capped at 0.45); **X3** both. Nearness 0.25 and the default minutes in all worlds. The tool's own assumptions (acts-fail 0.15, default hit rates, tau 0.95, `entropy`) never change; only its P(wrong) does. Layouts, candidate regimes (R_narrow = 8, R_wide = 40), the 25 buildings, the candidate rule and TRUE accounting are those of Addendum 20 (metadata only).

**Cells, episodes, pairing (frozen).** A cell is (condition, P(wrong), regime, building): 4 x 3 x 2 x 25 = 600, indexed lexicographically in that order (condition outermost, building fastest). 500 episodes per cell, seed `[22, cell]` for the (room, cause) draws; policies `entropy` under off, on and on_mild (sharing outcome stream `[22, cell, 0, episode]`), `habit_A` (stream 1), `habit_B` (stream 2), on the same draws; bootstrap `[22, cell, 99]`, 1000 x 500 per cell. The habits do not read the prior and run on the default case.

**Labels and readings (frozen; those of Addendum 21).** For `on` (and, secondary, `on_mild`) against `off` on the same episodes: time state FASTER / SLOWER at 5% with the paired interval clear of 0; accuracy state MORE / LESS at 0.02 with the interval clear of 0; labels IMPROVES / WORSE / TRADE / NEITHER; SAFETY REGRESSION when wrong-and-confident is at least 0.02 higher with the interval above 0. A **group** is a (condition, P(wrong)) pair: 50 cells (25 buildings x 2 regimes), also shown per regime. In the 5%-wrong world (the switch is right) the group reads by the map reading (HELPS BROADLY if IMPROVES >= 0.60 and WORSE <= 0.10; DOES NOT HELP if IMPROVES < 0.20 or WORSE > 0.25; HELPS IN PART otherwise); in the 15% and 30% worlds (the switch is wrongly on) by the harm reading on the COST share (WORSE, TRADE or a safety regression): HARMLESS <= 0.10, HARMFUL > 0.25, COSTS SOMETHING otherwise.
**How many wrong switch-ons can it take?** The technician flips the switch on with probability s when the drawings are 5% wrong and f when they are 15% or 30% wrong (independent of anything else); worlds are mixed with weights pi. Two mixes, both placeholders: **equal** (1/3, 1/3, 1/3) and **tilted toward well-documented buildings** (0.6, 0.3, 0.1) for (5%, 15%, 30%). From the group means of the paired differences (on minus off; mean over buildings, per regime and condition) the expected change against never switching on is sum over worlds of pi_w x P(on | w) x delta_w, for minutes, accuracy and wrong-and-confident. It is **favourable** if expected minutes are not above 0, expected accuracy is not more than 0.01 below and expected wrong-and-confident is not more than 0.01 above the default's (the two 0.01 budgets are judgements of mine). The break-even false-alarm rate **f*** is the largest f on the grid 0, 0.01, ..., 1.00 for which the change is favourable at every grid value up to f ("none" if not favourable at f = 0); reported for s in {0.5, 1.0}, both mixes, both regimes, each condition.
**Decision (about offering the switch, never about shipping it; frozen).** For a condition define **C** = no 15%- or 30%-world group of that condition reads HARMFUL AND f* >= 0.50 at s = 1.0 for both mixes and both regimes. Let **Q** = the X0 5%-world group reads at least HELPS IN PART. **WORTH OFFERING** if Q holds and C holds in all four conditions. **CONDITIONAL ON RELIABLE CHECKS** if Q holds, C holds in X0 and X1, and C fails in X2 or X3. **NOT WORTH OFFERING** otherwise. The 0.50 bar (the switch may be wrongly on half the time in buildings that are not mostly right) is a judgement of mine. A positive decision would mean only "worth putting in front of real technicians"; if it is WORTH OFFERING or CONDITIONAL, restricting the tool's `--drawings` option to {mixed, mostly_right} (and, for CONDITIONAL, saying when) would be a later, separate change.
**Context, no verdict.** `entropy` in each state against the two habits (Addendum 19's classification and Addendum 20's BUYS ACCURACY label) in the primary worlds; the X0 group means placed next to the corresponding Addendum 21 values (`mostly_right` and `mixed`) to show how much a fresh seed moves them.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; reported as holds / does not hold).** P1 (replication): in X0 and the 5%-wrong world, `on` has lower mean minutes than `off` in both regimes and mean accuracy not more than 0.01 lower. P2: in the 30%-wrong world, the mean wrong-and-confident difference (on minus off) is larger in X2 than in X0 in both regimes (a sharper prior costs more when the checks are worse than assumed). P3: in every one of the 12 (condition, P(wrong)) groups, in both regimes, `on` has lower mean minutes than `off` (the saving is robust). P4: `on_mild` is between `off` and `on`: in every condition and both regimes, its minutes difference in the 5%-wrong world is above `on`'s (a smaller saving) and its wrong-and-confident difference in the 30%-wrong world is below `on`'s. P5: the frozen decision is **CONDITIONAL ON RELIABLE CHECKS**.

**Gates (all evaluated before any reading; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 default-off and code path: `off` is exactly the default `Settings()`, `on` equals `Settings().with_drawings("mostly_right")`, 30 episodes under `off` are identical to the tool as it was, and the runners reproduce `sim.simulate` (Addendum 20's G1). G2 accounting: Addendum 20's G2. G3 honesty: in X0, accuracy among CONCLUDED `entropy` episodes (mean over the 25 buildings) is at least 0.92 for `off` in the 15%-wrong world and for `on` in the 5%-wrong world, in each regime (a lower value would mean a defect, not a finding; no honesty gate in the stressed conditions, where overconfidence is the thing being measured). G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces the first 50 stored episodes of all five policies. G5 completeness and pairing: all 600 cells x 5 policies present, finite, identical (room, cause) draws across policies, habits identical whatever prior the tool holds. G6 layout fidelity: as Addendum 20.

**Not used:** timeseries, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, or any fitted quantity. Mortar licence as in Addendum 20: only aggregates are written to `results/`.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) Post-hoc in origin (above): a replication on the same layouts and simulator, not new evidence about the world. (2) Errors and answers come from the tool's own model family and a prior that is right helps mechanically; the informative numbers are the saving, the cost when the switch is wrongly on, and the tolerated false-alarm rate. (3) Nobody knows how well a technician can tell that drawings are mostly right: s and f are unknowns that the study only bounds. (4) The degraded hit rates, the stress levels, the two mixes, the two 0.01 budgets and the 0.50 bar are placeholders or judgements. (5) `poor` is not re-tested; nothing here says a two-way rating is worth having. (6) One lever of eight, `entropy` only, `vav_zone` only. (7) Everything from Addendum 20's limitations (no location data in Brick, identifier order as nearness, placeholder minutes, both habits my constructions).

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/study_switch.py` (stress worlds, cells, runner, expected-change and f*, decision, predictions, gates; reusing the labels and readings of `study_rating.py`), `scripts/65_troubleshoot_switch.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_switch.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_switch/`. `model.py`, `session.py`, `sim.py`, `study.py`, `study_real.py`, `study_rating.py` and `realmaps.py` are not modified. Expected run time about 20 minutes on 8 workers.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-25, written after `troubleshoot/study_switch.py`, `scripts/65_troubleshoot_switch.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_switch.py`, BEFORE the 600-cell run was started; none changes a world, a condition, a switch level, a label margin, a reading threshold, a budget, the 0.50 bar, the decision rule or a prediction).** (1) The group readings (5%-wrong: map reading; 15%- and 30%-wrong: harm reading) use the 50 pooled cells of a (condition, world) group; f* is computed per regime from the per-regime group means. (2) The break-even grid is f = 0, 0.01, ..., 1.00, s in {0.5, 1.0}, both mixes; the favourability test allows a floating-point tolerance of 1e-12; C requires f* >= 0.50 (0.50 itself passes) at s = 1.0 for both mixes in both regimes. (3) The predictions are operationalised as: P1 mean over the 25 buildings of the paired minutes difference (on minus off) in X0 and the 5%-wrong world is below 0 and of the accuracy difference at least -0.01, in each regime; P2 the mean wrong-and-confident difference in the 30%-wrong world is strictly larger in X2 than in X0, in each regime; P3 the mean minutes difference is strictly below 0 in each of the 24 (condition, world, regime) groups; P4 in each of the 8 (condition, regime) pairs, `on_mild`'s minutes difference in the 5%-wrong world is strictly above `on`'s and its wrong-and-confident difference in the 30%-wrong world is strictly below `on`'s; P5 the decision string equals CONDITIONAL ON RELIABLE CHECKS. (4) The replication table in the report reads Addendum 21's stored analysis (`results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_rating/rating_analysis.pkl`) and is context only. (5) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 20 new tests for the study pass and the full doc_mismatch suite passes (425 tests); twenty-two deliberately broken versions of the study code (the wrong baseline state, right and wrong worlds swapped in the false-alarm model, a flipped accuracy budget, a flipped wrong-and-confident budget, a break-even that is not contiguous from f = 0, a decision using `or` instead of `and`, a decision needing only X0, a strict instead of inclusive 0.50 bar, the wrong group for Q, the harm reading applied where the switch is right, states that do not share random draws, a stress condition that is not degraded, a wrong `on` level, a changed cell order, a swapped tilted mix, a dropped safety flag, an `off` delta that is not zero, a habit-independence gate that checks one state, an honesty gate reading the wrong state, P2 reversed, P3 accepting zero, P5 with a different target) were each caught by at least one test; I tightened the honesty-gate test before that check, because its stub gave every policy the same accuracy and so could not tell which policy the gate read. A plumbing smoke run of 3 buildings x 20 episodes wrote to a scratch directory; I listed the report's headings and row counts, and one heading (the decision section's) contains the smoke run's decision string, so I saw that one string (it equals the P5 prediction, which was written before). The smoke numbers are meaningless (20 episodes, 3 buildings) and no other number in them was read. No comparison of switch states on the full 600 cells has been read.

**Result of Addendum 22 (2026-09-25; `scripts/65_troubleshoot_switch.py` run once as pre-registered, 1446 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_switch/switch_report.md`, per-cell `switch_cells.csv`, raw `switch_raw.pkl`; the run was launched twice: the first launch was killed by me while still computing, before anything had been written or displayed, over a worry about a tool time limit, and the second is the reported run).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (G3: accuracy among concluded 0.977 and 0.978 for `off` at 15% wrong, 0.983 and 0.986 for `on` at 5% wrong, against the 0.92 bar). *Frozen decision:* **CONDITIONAL ON RELIABLE CHECKS**. Q holds (the X0 5%-wrong group reads HELPS BROADLY, IMPROVES in 100% of the 50 cells); C holds in X0 (as assumed) and X1 (actuators fail more) and fails in X2 (checks worse) and X3 (both). Wrongly-on readings: X0 15% HARMLESS, 30% COSTS SOMETHING; X1 HARMLESS and HARMLESS; **X2 and X3 HARMFUL at both 15% and 30% wrong**. Break-even false-alarm rate f* at s = 1.0: 1.00 in X0 and X1 for both mixes and both regimes (the switch could be left on in every building and stay within the budgets); X2 0.31 / 0.36 (R_narrow, equal / tilted) and 0.24 / 0.17 (R_wide); X3 0.42 / 0.67 and 0.28 / 0.30, so the 0.50 bar fails in every X2 combination and in three of four in X3.
*What the numbers say (pooled over both regimes; `on` minus `off` on the same episodes).* X0 replicates Addendum 21 within about a minute and a point: minutes -2.5, -2.7, -3.3 (-9%, -8%, -7%) at 5%, 15%, 30% wrong drawings; accuracy -0.001, -0.003, -0.007; wrong-and-confident +0.002, +0.006, +0.012 (a safety regression in 14% of the 30%-wrong cells, 28% in R_wide). X1 (30% acts-fail): the saving is larger, -4.8, -5.0, -5.5 minutes (-16%, -13%, -11%), with the same small cost (wrong-and-confident +0.003, +0.007, +0.012). X2 (worse checks): -5.5, -6.9, -8.4 minutes (-14%) but wrong-and-confident +0.012, +0.025, +0.042 (a safety regression in 12%, 74%, 98% of cells; the confident-wrong rate goes from 8% to 11% at 15% wrong and from 11% to 15% at 30% in R_narrow) with accuracy about unchanged (+0.005, -0.002, -0.011). X3 (both): -8.4, -10.0, -12.0 minutes (-19%); wrong-and-confident +0.008, +0.021, +0.041 (safety regression in 4%, 54%, 100%). The switch saves more time, not less, when things are worse than assumed, and it is the safety cost that grows. **`on_mild` (10%) did nothing**: against `off` every difference is within 0.1 minutes, 0.002 accuracy and 0.001 wrong-and-confident and every cell is NEITHER, so the tool behaves the same at a prior of 0.10 as at 0.15 and the effect appears only at the sharper level (5%); the reason (a stopping-threshold effect) is my interpretation and was not tested. Against the habits (context, no verdict): `entropy` never WINS against both habits in any state (one cell in 72 LOSES against `habit_A`, X1 30% wrong R_wide with `on`); with the switch on in a 5%-wrong building with a wide search the tool's minutes tie `habit_A`'s (25 against 25 in X0) at 95% against 84% accuracy; in X2 and X3 both habits collapse (accuracy 48-67%, wrong-and-confident 33-52%) while the tool stays at 69-89% accuracy and 6-16% wrong-and-confident whichever state the switch is in.
*Predictions as operationalised: all five hold.* P1 (-2.44 and -2.55 minutes, accuracy -0.002 and -0.000); P2 (wrong-and-confident +0.040 against +0.010 in R_narrow, +0.043 against +0.015 in R_wide); P3 (`on` is faster in all 24 groups; the smallest saving is -2.44); P4 holds, but only because `on_mild` is indistinguishable from `off`, so it is not evidence of a smooth in-between; P5 holds (I had seen the smoke run's decision string in a heading listing before the run, disclosed above).
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) The one-way switch is safe and modestly useful only if the tool's checks are about as reliable as it assumes: then it saves 7-9% of the minutes (11-16% if actuators fail more often), costs at most 1.2 points of confident wrong answers even when left on in every building, and tolerates any false-alarm rate. (2) If real checks are worse than assumed it saves more time but adds 1-4 points of confident wrong answers, mostly where the drawings really are unreliable, and it tolerates only about a fifth to two thirds of wrong switch-ons. (3) Whether the switch is safe therefore turns on a number nobody has: how often a real technician's check is wrong. That is what interviews or field trials could measure; the simulation cannot. (4) Everything is simulation inside the tool's own model family, the degraded hit rates and stress levels are placeholders, the technician-behaviour model is (s, f) with placeholder mixes, and the study is post-hoc in origin, so X0 is a replication and X1-X3 are stress tests, not confirmation on real data. (5) Restricting the tool's `--drawings` option to {mixed, mostly_right}, with a warning about check reliability, would be a later, separate change; it has not been made. *Not concluded:* that technicians can tell when drawings are mostly right; anything about real check reliability; anything about the other seven levers.


## Addendum 23 (dated 2026-09-25, written BEFORE any run of the tool without air-handler grouping or with a true feeder that may lie outside its candidate list): how does the tool do with only the minimum data it needs, and how does it compare with the two stand-in habits there? (quick, simulation only)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** The user asked how well the tool works with just the minimum data it needs to run, how that compares with a technician, and then for a quick, cheap test of the minimum. The tool needs an equipment list (rooms and the boxes that could feed them) and the drawings' claim for the complaining room; nothing else (`building.py`). Every study so far gave it more: Addenda 19-22 also gave it a nearness prior ("same air handler" counts more than another) and drew the true feeder from the candidate list it was handed, apart from a 10% "unlisted" share. So they could not show what happens when the tool has no idea where to look and the real feeder may not be on its list.

**What "minimum" means here (frozen).** Level **min**: the equipment list and the claims only. Every box has no area, so the tool's candidate list for a room is the documented box followed by the other boxes in equipment-identifier order, capped at K, all with equal weight (K stands in for a technician who narrows the search to K boxes without location information; identifier order is arbitrary). Level **grouped** (for comparison; this is Addendum 20's setting): each box's area is its documented air handler; candidates are the documented box, then the other boxes of the same handler, then boxes of other handlers, capped at K, weights 1 and 0.25. The tool's own assumptions (P(wrong) 0.15, P(acts-fail) 0.15, unlisted share 0.10, default hit rates and minutes, tau 0.95, `entropy`) are the defaults in both levels.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addenda 19-22 in full, including Addendum 20's wide-search result with grouping (37 minutes, 89% accuracy, 11% unresolved at up to 40 candidates in the default world). (2) A hand calculation, not a study: with perfect checks tested one box at a time and a flat prior, the expected number of checks is about 1.285 + 0.0825 x K (about 1.9 at K = 8, 4.6 at 40, 9.5 at 100, 34 at 394), so the visits with a wrong box drive the growth. (3) Code reading: with all areas None the candidate weights are all 1 and the order is equipment order. (4) The tool has not been run without grouping and has not been run against a true feeder outside its list except through the 10% unlisted share.

**Worlds and coverage (frozen).** For a room, the true cause is drawn from the TRUE prior over ALL boxes of the building (documented right and acting 0.70; documented box does not act 0.15; documented feeder wrong 0.135 split over the other boxes; equipment not in the building's list 0.015). Two worlds differ only in where a wrong feeder lies: **local** (same air handler weight 1, another handler 0.25: the truth of Addenda 19-22) and **anywhere** (all other boxes equally likely, no locality). A wrong feeder that is not on the tool's list is, from the tool's side, the cause "unlisted" (every check on a listed box answers exactly as for an unlisted cause), so the truth is mapped to "unlisted" for that policy; the share of episodes in which this happens is the **off-list share**. Layouts (the 25 buildings of Addendum 20, Brick metadata only) and TRUE accounting are Addendum 20's. Caps: **K8** and **K40**.

**Cells, episodes, pairing (frozen).** A cell is (world, cap, building): 2 x 2 x 25 = 100, indexed lexicographically (world outermost: local, anywhere; then cap: 8, 40; building fastest). 300 episodes per cell, seed `[23, cell]` for the (room, cause) draws. Six policies: `entropy`, `habit_A`, `habit_B`, each at level min and level grouped, on the same draws; the two levels of a policy share their outcome stream (`[23, cell, 0|1|2, episode]` for entropy, habit_A, habit_B); bootstrap `[23, cell, 99]`, 1000 x 300. The tool stops after 12 checks (unresolved) as before; the habits run to exhaustion as before.

**Metrics.** Minutes (TRUE), accuracy (the answer equals the mapped truth; a correct "unlisted" counts), **actionable** (correct and the answer names a cause, i.e. is not "unlisted": the technician knows what to do), wrong-and-confident, unresolved, checks, off-list share.

**Comparisons and labels (frozen; descriptive, no verdict rule).** (a) Within a level, `entropy` against each habit with Addendum 19's classification (10% minutes, 0.02 accuracy, paired intervals) and Addendum 20's BUYS ACCURACY label, and against both habits (Addendum 19's `combine`). (b) For the tool, level grouped against level min with Addendum 21's labels (5% minutes, 0.02 accuracy; IMPROVES / WORSE / TRADE / NEITHER, IMPROVES meaning grouping is better), and the paired differences in minutes, accuracy, actionable, wrong-and-confident and off-list share. A **group** is a (world, cap) pair (25 buildings); everything is reported per group and per building for anywhere / K40.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; reported as holds / does not hold; means over the 25 buildings of a group).** P1 (grouping helps when errors are local): in the local world, for each cap, `entropy@min` has a higher off-list share and higher minutes than `entropy@grouped`. P2 (grouping is worth nothing when errors are anywhere): in the anywhere world, for each cap, the minutes of `entropy@min` are within 10% of those of `entropy@grouped` and the off-list shares differ by at most 0.02. P3 (coverage): in the anywhere world the off-list share of `entropy@min` is at least 0.08 at K8 and lower at K40 than at K8. P4 (accuracy edge persists): `entropy@min` has higher accuracy than `habit_A@min` in all four groups. P5 (speed edge over the slow habit persists): `entropy@min` takes fewer minutes than `habit_B@min` in all four groups.

**Gates (all evaluated before any reading; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 code path: on a layout in which every box hangs on one air handler, `min` and `grouped` give identical episodes, and on a real-layout case with the truth inside the list the runners reproduce `sim.simulate` (Addendum 20's G1). G2 accounting: Addendum 20's G2. G3 coverage bookkeeping: in every cell and level every mapped truth lies in the tool's cause list, the off-list share equals a direct count of the truths that are wrong feeders not on the list, and it is exactly 0 in every cell whose building has at most K boxes. G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces the first 50 stored episodes of all six policies. G5 completeness and pairing: all 100 cells x 6 policies present and finite, one shared set of (room, cause) draws per cell. G6 layout fidelity: as Addendum 20.

**Not used:** timeseries, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, or any fitted quantity. Mortar licence as in Addendum 20: only aggregates are written to `results/`.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The identifier order is arbitrary; in real buildings ids often follow location (VAV-2-04 next to VAV-2-05), so min-level lists may cover the true feeder better than here (optimistic real-world factor, not modelled), while "narrowing to K boxes" by a technician who has looked at the building would also do better than an arbitrary list (also not modelled). (2) The local world with min-level lists is the worst case for a tool that cannot use locality; real errors lie between local and anywhere. (3) No "trace the duct and read the tag" check and no use of `add_candidate`; both would change the wrong-feeder visits. (4) Errors and answers come from the tool's own model family; minutes and hit rates are placeholders; both habits are my constructions and simpler than real technicians. (5) The tool's prior still thinks only 1.5% of visits have the feeder off its list; that mismatch is part of what is being measured. (6) One connection type (`vav_zone`), default world otherwise (15% wrong, 15% acts-fail, assumed checks), 300 episodes.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/study_minimum.py`, `scripts/66_troubleshoot_minimum.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_minimum.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minimum/`. No other module of the tool or of the earlier studies is modified. Expected run time a few minutes on 8 workers.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-25, written after `troubleshoot/study_minimum.py`, `scripts/66_troubleshoot_minimum.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_minimum.py`, BEFORE the 100-cell run was started; none changes a level, a world, a cap, a metric, a label, a prediction or a gate).** (1) The predictions are operationalised as: P1 in the local world, for each cap, the mean off-list share and the mean minutes of `entropy@min` are each strictly above those of `entropy@grouped`; P2 in the anywhere world, for each cap, |minutes(min) - minutes(grouped)| <= 0.10 x minutes(grouped) and |off-list share(min) - off-list share(grouped)| <= 0.02; P3 in the anywhere world the mean off-list share of `entropy@min` is >= 0.08 at K8 and strictly lower at K40; P4 the mean accuracy of `entropy@min` is strictly above `habit_A@min`'s in each of the four groups; P5 the mean minutes of `entropy@min` are strictly below `habit_B@min`'s in each of the four groups. (2) The stored `truth_idx` of a policy is the index of the (mapped) truth in that policy's own cause list; pairing across policies rests on the shared (room, cause) draws, which G3 recomputes from the seeds. (3) The candidate count per episode reported by the script is that of the grouped level.
