(9) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 168 new tests (63 + 59 + 38 + 8 for the data model, session and command line, study code and documentation) pass; the full doc_mismatch suite passes (670 tests: the 502 that existed before v1 and the 168 new ones), so no earlier test changed. **Deliberately broken versions, run with bytecode caching disabled and `__pycache__` removed before every run and the mutated file restored and hash-checked after:** 150 of the v1 code and the study code (causes, catalogue, data tiers, engine, simulator, command line, study, script) were each caught by at least one test (re-run once more on the final code: 150 of 150), after the first pass left 16 alive and tests were added or strengthened for each of them (an unvalidated attribute value, zone data taken from the box, asking the last missing fact instead of the first, a skipped check moving the technician, the tie in the most-likely answer, replay ignoring the question asked, the auto tier, belief not shown after an answer, the answer key `a`, the bootstrap seed, the boundary of P6, the replay gate's scope, branch accuracy, the exit code on a failed gate, the clipping of true probabilities, and the wording of a rejected declaration); none had to be written off as equivalent. 7 deliberately broken versions of v0's own files (a hit rate, a prior, the accounting, the demo wording, the nearness weights, the tie-break, the simulator) were used to check that the v0 regression guard works: five were caught by the golden transcripts alone, one by v0's own tests, and one (the tie-break, which no test had pinned) survived until a test for it was added, after which it was caught; v0's files are byte-identical to their state before v1 (hashes compared). 8 deliberately broken versions of the table generator (which builds the tables of `TROUBLESHOOT_V1.md`, itself compared against the generator by a test) were caught, one after a test was added. The study code was run only inside tests (tiny cells of 6-20 episodes, structure and gates asserted on synthetic and tiny inputs) and once as a plumbing smoke run written to a scratch directory (disclosure 4); **no comparison of arms at the frozen size has been computed or read.**

**Status: RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-26), after the user asked me to "run back the cheap test", which I read as the approval requested above.**

**Result of Addendum 27 (2026-09-26; `scripts/71_troubleshoot_tiers.py`, 29 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tiers/tiers_report.md`, per-cell `tiers_cells.csv`, raw `tiers_raw.pkl` (synthetic episodes only; no Mortar or other real data is involved)).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (G2: 400 degraded-reliability episodes replay identically from their answers alone; G3: accuracy among concluded episodes 0.952-0.995 with 74-214 conclusions per arm against the 0.90 bar, the tightest being half coverage / T2 at 0.952). *Frozen readings of the four adjacent steps over the 12 cells:* T1 against T0 **HURTS** (IMPROVES 0%, WORSE in 5 of 12 cells, no TRADE); T2 against T1 **MIXED** (IMPROVES in 4 of 12, WORSE in 0); T2+access against T2 **MIXED** (IMPROVES 4, WORSE 2); T3 against T2+access **HELPS** (IMPROVES in all 12). *Primary cell (R0 / assumed / full, hardware-cause episodes, n = 438), minutes / accuracy / mean checks:* T0 88 / 72% / 8.3; T1 91 / 71% / 8.6; T2 85 / 77% / 8.6; T2+access 85 / 84% / 9.1; T3 48 / 82% / 9.2. Sessions that reached 0.95: 18%, 21%, 23%, 43%, 49%; that stopped because nothing left was worth its time: 68%, 60%, 50%, 24%, 11%; that hit the 12-check limit: 13%, 19%, 27%, 33%, 40%. Wrong-and-confident at most 0.5% in every arm here. *Predictions as operationalised: P1-P7 and P9 hold, P8 does not.* P1 T3 48.3 minutes against the 63.8 bound; P2 48.3 against 76.6; P3 T2 within 7.0% of T1's minutes and 5.7 points more accurate; P4 T1 91.5 against T0 88.4; P5 the wrong-and-confident rate is higher at R2 than in the primary cell for all five arms; P6 the R2 increase is 13.2 points at T3 against 6.8 at T1; P7 the T3 against T2+access gap is 15.8 minutes at half coverage against 36.8 at full; P9 T1 locates the wrong claim in 82% of those episodes. **P8 does not hold:** accuracy under the shifted mix is lower than in the primary cell for T0, T2, T2+access and T3 (by 3.7, 1.7, 4.7 and 3.4 points) but higher for T1 (73.4% against 71.2%).
*What the numbers say (primary cell unless stated).* (1) **The point map is where the minutes are.** T3 is 36.8 minutes (43%) faster than T2+access at about the same accuracy (-1.4 points) and IMPROVES in every cell (-33 to -38 minutes at full coverage, -16 to -19 at half). Control-system access without a map changed minutes by 0.0 (about the same number of reads, at 10 minutes each) but bought accuracy: +6.8 points over T2, and 43% of sessions reaching 0.95 against 23%. (2) Attributes and design values (T2 against T1): -6.4 minutes (-7%) and +5.7 points accuracy. (3) The drawings (T1 against T0) cost 3.1 minutes and 0.9 points on hardware-cause episodes, the price of testing a documentation hypothesis that is usually false, and buy the ability to find a wrong claim (82% of those episodes; T3 finds them less well, 74%, having spent checks on reads). (4) **The number of checks does not fall with more data; it rises slightly**: 8.3, 8.6, 8.6, 9.1, 9.2 in the primary cell, and 8.30, 8.85, 8.93, 9.53, 9.80 averaged over all 12 cells (exploratory, not registered). Richer tiers spend about the same budget on more decisive checks: the share of sessions that reach 0.95 rises from 18% to 49%, and what falls is minutes (T3), questions asked (3.0 to 0.6) and entries typed (12.2 to 10.8). (5) **Stress:** when the checks are worse than the tool believes, accuracy falls to 44-56% (R1) and 26-35% (R2) in every arm, and the wrong-and-confident rate rises from 0-2% (R0) to 1-9% (R1) and 5-18% (R2), most in the richer arms (R2 / assumed / full: T0 5%, T1 7%, T2 12%, T2+access 15%, T3 14%); a safety regression is flagged in 8 of 12 cells for T3 against T0, 3 of 12 for T3 against T2 and for T2+access against T2. The minutes T3 saves survive the stress; its accuracy edge does not. (6) With half of the boxes covered by attributes and points, T3's edge over T2+access shrinks from 37 to 16 minutes.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (a) Everything is simulation inside the tool's own model family with independent answers and placeholder minutes; the wrong-and-confident figures under stress show sensitivity to check reliability, not a real frequency, and real errors are correlated. (b) Even with checks exactly as reliable as assumed, most sessions at T0-T2 end without reaching 0.95 (18-23% do) and the leading cause is right 71-77% of the time: without the control system the tool is not decisive, and part of that is my placeholder stopping rules (0.95, 12 checks, 0.003 bits per minute), which this study did not vary, so it cannot say whether a different rule would cut the number of checks at the same accuracy (that would need its own registration). (c) v0 used about three to five checks, but it answered a much smaller question (four lumped causes); the numbers are not comparable, and no comparison with v0 or with a technician was made. (d) T1 against T0 is HURTS by the frozen rule because the T1 arm pays for documentation hypotheses on the episodes where the drawings are right; the reading says nothing about buildings whose drawings are suspect. (e) The catalogue and defaults were shaped by development episodes (disclosure 2), so predictions that held are weaker evidence than they would be from a fresh design. *Consequence as pre-registered ("What would follow"):* the point map, more than access, is what to ask a facilities contact for first, and access on its own buys accuracy rather than minutes; the R1 / R2 safety regressions in the richer arms justify a separate pre-registered study of a stricter stopping threshold for those tiers, which is not written. *Not concluded:* that any real technician or building would see these minutes; that fewer tests is impossible; anything about real check reliability or real cause frequencies.


## Addendum 28 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE any stand-in technician has been run on a v1 episode): how does the v1 tool compare with fast trial-and-error stand-ins at each data tier, and what would a wrong diagnosis have to cost for the slower tool to pay? (simulation only)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** After Addendum 27 (what each data tier buys the v1 tool) the user pasted the table of Addendum 26 (the id-weighted v0 tool against `habit_A`, the invented "fast technician": 34-40 against 22-24 minutes, 91-97% against 77-83% right, 2% against 17-23% confidently wrong), asked what it was, and then asked to **compare v1 to the fast technician stand-in**. The two studies are not comparable as they stand: Addendum 26 asked v0's narrow question (which box feeds the room, four lumped causes, about 70% of episodes being "the box is fine, the cause is elsewhere"), whereas v1 must name one of 14 hardware causes; and `habit_A` knows only v0's four checks. This addendum runs the same complaints, the same data tiers and the same simulated technician answers as Addendum 27 through fast stand-ins, so that the comparison is on the same footing. **Every stand-in is my construction, not a real technician.**

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addenda 19-27 in full, including Addendum 27's numbers for the tool at every tier and cell (primary cell, minutes / accuracy / checks: T0 88 / 72% / 8.3, T1 91 / 71% / 8.6, T2 85 / 77% / 8.6, T2+access 85 / 84% / 9.1, T3 48 / 82% / 9.2; sessions reaching 0.95 18-49%; wrong-and-confident 0-2% at R0, up to 18% at R2) and Addendum 26's pattern (tool slower than the fast stand-in, more accurate, far less often confidently wrong). The predictions below were written with those in mind. (2) No stand-in has been run on a v1 episode, and no stand-in code beyond v0's `habit_A` exists. (3) The v1 catalogue and the study world are those of Addendum 27, unchanged.

**The stand-ins (frozen; all three answer from the same TRUE responses as the tool and are charged the same minutes by the same accounting, including fallback costs; none asks a question or reads a belief; each is told the world's facts (gauge, occupant, reheat, control-system access) for free, which favours it).** *`habit_A`* (v0's fast stand-in, logic unchanged): confirm that the documented box acts (`confirm_acts`: no concludes "the box does not act"); otherwise run the cheaper of `override_room` and `override_diffuser` (by the minutes from where the technician is) on the documented box and then on each alternative in candidate order; the first yes concludes "the documented box feeds it and acts, cause elsewhere" (documented box) or "fed by box j"; all no concludes "unlisted". It uses only v0's four checks, so it can answer only at v0's level of detail (four lumps); at T0 its only candidate is "the box serving the room". *`fast_list`* (new): the technician works down a checklist of the reading checks that are available at the arm and each name **one** cause, cheapest first (the checklist is every reading check with exactly one cause whose P(abnormal) is at least 0.75 in the catalogue; its cause is that cause; the order is ascending total minutes from scratch, including any fallback, ties by name), doing each check with location-aware minutes, and **stops at the first abnormal reading and concludes that check's cause**. If the whole list is normal and the arm has the drawings' claims (T1 and up), it walks the alternative boxes as `habit_A` does (the cheaper act-and-watch check on each alternative in candidate order, first yes concludes "fed by box j"); if nothing is found it concludes "other" (at T0: "other" straight away). *`fast_confirm`* (new; a careful fast technician, to guard against a strawman): as `fast_list`, but a first abnormal reading is confirmed by the cheapest other available reading check that sees the same cause (P(abnormal) at least 0.5 for it), which is also charged; if the confirmation is abnormal the cause is concluded, if it is normal the finding is discarded and the checklist continues; if no such check exists the reading stands. No check is done twice. `fast_list` and `fast_confirm` cannot name a cause that has no single-cause check (actuator failure, low duct pressure, capacity at T0-T1, and "other" except by exhaustion): they can only miss it. That is a property of a checklist that names one cause per check; a real technician's judgement is not modelled.

**The world, arms, cells, episodes (frozen; exactly Addendum 27's).** The same synthetic building, draws (`[27, cell, 0, episode]`), true causes, gauge and occupant draws, five arms (T0, T1, T2, T2+access, T3) and 12 cells (reliability R0 / R1 / R2 x mix assumed / shifted x cover full / half), 500 episodes per cell and arm. The tool's results are Addendum 27's, reproduced exactly (same seeds). The stand-ins' outcome draws use `[28, cell, policy, i]` with policy 0 = `habit_A`, 1 = `fast_list`, 2 = `fast_confirm`, independent of the arm (so a stand-in that does not use the arm's data behaves identically across arms; tested), and the true reliability is degraded exactly as in Addendum 27 (R0 a = 0, R1 a = 0.25, R2 a = 0.5). Two episode subsets: **hardware-cause** episodes (true cause one of the 14; run at every arm) and **documentation-wrong** episodes (the drawings are wrong; T1 and up).

**Levels of detail and metrics.** *Exact cause* (a leaf, "fed by box j", "unlisted", "other"): the tool, `fast_list`, `fast_confirm`. *v0 level* (four lumps: fed by box j / unlisted / the box does not act (actuator, damper, power, signal, controller communications) / the box acts and the cause is elsewhere (every other hardware cause)): all four policies, by collapsing each cause to its lump (`habit_A` can only be scored here). Per policy, arm, subset and level: mean minutes, accuracy, wrong-and-confident rate (the tool: concluded and wrong; a stand-in always concludes, so all its wrong answers), mean checks, mean entries typed, minutes per correct diagnosis (secondary, unclassified), and, at v0 level, the accuracy of the constant guess "the box acts, cause elsewhere" (the base-rate baseline, so that a lump-level accuracy is not read without it).

**Frozen comparisons and labels.** Per arm, subset and level, the tool minus each stand-in, on the same episodes: Addendum 21's labels unmodified (time FASTER / SLOWER at 5% with the paired 95% interval clear of 0; accuracy MORE / LESS at 0.02 likewise; IMPROVES / WORSE / TRADE / NEITHER; safety REGRESSION / GAIN at 0.02 in the wrong-and-confident rate), TRADE in its two kinds. Bootstrap: one 1000 x n resample matrix per cell and subset, seeds `[28, cell, 99, 0]` (hardware) and `[28, cell, 99, 1]` (documentation). Reported as shares of the 12 cells and as the primary-cell deltas (R0 / assumed / full); **no verdict rule** (as in Addendum 26). **Break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis** (descriptive, per arm and stand-in, on the means of the primary cell, hardware, exact level; v0 level for `habit_A`): with tool minutes m_t and accuracy a_t and stand-in minutes m_s and accuracy a_s: DOMINATES if the tool is no slower and no less accurate; DOMINATED if it is no faster and no more accurate; if it is slower and more accurate, W* = (m_t - m_s) / (a_t - a_s), the number of extra minutes a wrong diagnosis must cost (return visit, rework, discomfort) before the tool's extra time pays; if it is faster and less accurate, W* = (m_s - m_t) / (a_s - a_t), the cost below which the tool is ahead. No cost of a wrong diagnosis is assumed.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; reported as holds / does not hold; primary cell, hardware-cause episodes, exact level unless stated).** P1: `fast_list` takes fewer mean minutes than the tool at T0, T1, T2 and T2+access. P2: the tool's accuracy is above `fast_list`'s at all five arms. P3: the tool's wrong-and-confident rate is below `fast_list`'s at all five arms. P4: `fast_confirm` takes more minutes than `fast_list` and is at least as accurate at all five arms. P5: the tool's accuracy is above `fast_confirm`'s at all five arms. P6: at v0 level the tool's accuracy is above `habit_A`'s at T1, T2, T2+access and T3. P7: for each arm, the tool's accuracy advantage over `fast_list` at (R2, assumed, full) is smaller than in the primary cell. P8: the ratio of the tool's minutes to `fast_list`'s is smaller at T3 than at T0. P9: on the primary cell's documentation-wrong episodes the tool's accuracy is above `fast_list`'s at T1, T2, T2+access and T3.

**Gates (all evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 code path: (a) on the v0-equivalent tree of v1, `habit_A` reproduces v0's `sim.simulate_habit` exactly (guess, minutes, checks) on the study's building; (b) if Addendum 27's raw file (`results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tiers/tiers_raw.pkl`) is present, the tool's arrays in every cell equal it exactly (the tool has not drifted since Addendum 27; if the file is absent this part is reported as skipped). G2 accounting: hand-summed minutes of scripted stand-in transcripts (at T2: design_load 6, controller lights 6 + 3, 24 VAC at the box 4 = 19 minutes when the third reading is abnormal; at T3: fan status 2 then, with confirmation, static pressure 2 = 4), and the first 10 valid episodes of every stand-in and arm in every R1 and R2 cell replay identically from their recorded answers alone (a stand-in's choices depend on nothing about the world's reliability). G3 availability: every check a stand-in performed was available at its arm given the world's true facts, and none was done twice. G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces the first 50 stored episodes of every policy. G5 completeness and pairing: all 12 cells, arms and policies present, finite, with the tool's and the stand-ins' true causes identical episode by episode, `habit_A` identical at T1, T2, T2+access and T3, and every valid episode with a guess.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, Mortar, any fitted quantity, any download. The stand-ins, the labels and the predictions are frozen here.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) All three stand-ins are my constructions, simpler than a real technician: no symptoms, no history, no knowledge of the building, and each stops at (or nearly at) the first abnormal reading. (2) `fast_list` and `fast_confirm` cannot conclude causes that have no single-cause check, so part of the tool's accuracy edge is the design of the checklist, not what a real fast technician knows; and `habit_A` was built for v0's question and cannot say which hardware cause it is. (3) The stand-ins get the world's facts for free (no questions, no entries typed), which favours them; the tool's entries include its questions. (4) The tool stops after 12 checks and at 0.003 bits per minute; the stand-ins run to the end of their list (up to 17 checks), which flatters the tool on minutes when nothing is found. (5) A stand-in always concludes, so its wrong-and-confident rate is its whole error rate; the tool's is only among the sessions that reach 0.95, so the two are not the same kind of number and the tool's fraction of unresolved sessions must be read beside it. (6) Answers are independent given the cause, causes come from the tool's own model family, minutes and probabilities are placeholders, and everything from Addendum 27's limitations applies. (7) At v0 level the accuracy of a constant guess is high, because most hardware causes fall in the lump "the box acts, cause elsewhere"; the v0-level comparison is informative mainly on the acts-fail and documentation episodes. (8) Nothing here is evidence about real technicians; W* is a break-even, not a finding that any technician's wrong diagnosis costs that much.

**What would follow.** If the tool is more accurate and less often confidently wrong than every stand-in at a cost in minutes that W* says a plausible wrong diagnosis would repay, the argument for the tool rests on the cost of a wrong diagnosis, which the interviews can ask about (how long does a return visit take, how often do they happen); if a stand-in is as accurate as the tool at fewer minutes at some tier, the tool's design (its stopping rules, its breadth) is not earning its time there and should be reconsidered before any technician sees it. Whatever the result, no claim about real technicians follows; the next step remains shadow-mode logging of real call-outs.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v1/study_fast_v1.py` (the three stand-ins, the runner that reuses Addendum 27's runner for the tool, levels, summaries, labels, break-even, predictions, gates), `scripts/72_troubleshoot_v1_fast.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_v1_fast.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_v1_fast/` (aggregate statistics; synthetic episodes only). No existing module is modified.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-26, written after `troubleshoot/v1/study_fast_v1.py`, `scripts/72_troubleshoot_v1_fast.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_v1_fast.py`, BEFORE the study was run; none changes a stand-in's rule, a level, a label, a margin, a prediction or a gate as written above).** (1) The checklist of `fast_list` and `fast_confirm` has **16** checks, not "up to 17" as the limitations say: `bas_ahu_fan`, `bas_controller`, `bas_zone`, `bas_reheat`, `power_24vac`, `control_signal`, `manual_damper_test`, `damper_linkage`, `controller_leds`, `sensing_ports`, `flow_vs_gauge`, `reheat_coil`, `ahu_visit`, `diffuser_duct`, `thermostat`, `design_load` (each the only cause with P(abnormal) of at least 0.75), of which only those available at an arm and its facts are used; the checks that see several causes at 0.75 or more (`bas_static`, `bas_airflow`, `bas_damper`, `static_at_box`, `flow_hood`, `ahu_gauge`) or none (`k_factor`, `max_airflow`, `bas_hunting`, `ask_occupant`) are not on it. (2) In gate G2 the hand-summed T3 confirmation example names the wrong confirming check: with equal costs the tie-break is by name and `bas_damper` and `bas_airflow` also see a fan fault (0.5 and 0.92), so the check that confirms a fan finding is `bas_airflow` (2 minutes, total 4, as registered), not static pressure; the minutes are unchanged. The frozen rule (the cheapest other available reading check with P(abnormal) of at least 0.5 for the cause, ties by name) is what is coded. (3) A stand-in's minutes are the tool's own accounting: a `Session` is built for the arm with every fact of the world declared and is used only as an accountant (`report` charges access, doing, waiting and fallback and moves the technician); the stand-ins read no belief from it. (4) The stand-ins' answers draw from `p_true` (the TRUE response, clipped as the tool's is) with the stream `[28, cell, policy, episode]`; `habit_A` at T1, T2, T2+access and T3 is identical by construction because it uses none of the arm's data (checked by G5). (5) The "documented box" a stand-in works on at T0 is the generic "box serving the room"; `habit_A` there has only that box as a candidate. (6) The reading, labels and break-even are computed per cell and subset; the hardware-cause comparisons pair the same episodes as Addendum 27's; the documentation-wrong comparisons exist only where a cell has such episodes. (7) The break-even cost is reported on the primary cell's means and carries no verdict. (8) *Code-path evidence and disclosures before the run:* 41 new tests pass and the full suite passes (711 tests: the 670 that existed before this study and the 41 new ones); the tool's arrays are compared with Addendum 27's stored raw file in gate G1, so the tool cannot have drifted; `habit_A` reproduces v0's `sim.simulate_habit` exactly on the study's building (20 episodes). **Sixty-five deliberately broken versions** of the new code and script (checklist threshold and boundary, ordering and tie-break, fallback cost, availability, the walk, the habit's first check and conclusions, confirmation and its boundary, repeated checks, positive read as negative, the world's facts, answers not drawn from the true response, replay strictness, stream seeds, T0's skipped episodes, the concluded flag of a stand-in, the v0 level, wrong-and-confident, break-even, comparison signs, the bootstrap seed, baseline, the comparisons' set, each of P1-P9's boundary or scope, each gate, and the script's exit code and report) were run with bytecode caching disabled and `__pycache__` removed before every run; **four survived the first pass** (the strictness of the replay's answer source, the baseline of an empty subset, an isolated G5 check, and the script's use of Addendum 27's raw file) and tests were added for each, after which all 65 were caught (re-run once more on the final tests). A plumbing smoke run of the script (2 cells x 20 episodes, written to a scratch directory, gates not enforced) was made; only its gates table was read. **No comparison of a stand-in with the tool at any size has been computed or read.**


**Result of Addendum 28 (2026-09-26; `scripts/72_troubleshoot_v1_fast.py` run once as pre-registered, 32 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_v1_fast/fast_report.md`, per-cell `fast_cells.csv`, raw `fast_raw.pkl` (synthetic episodes only)).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed (habit_A reproduces v0's `simulate_habit`; the tool's episodes equal Addendum 27's raw file in all 12 cells; G2: 1200 degraded-reliability stand-in episodes replay identically from their answers alone). *Primary cell (R0 / assumed / full, hardware-cause episodes, n = 438), exact cause, minutes / accuracy:* T0 tool 88 / 72%, `fast_list` 66 / 47%, `fast_confirm` 82 / 41%; T1 91 / 71%, 79 / 44%, 101 / 38%; T2 85 / 77%, 79 / 46%, 105 / 40%; T2+access 85 / 84%, 91 / 43%, 135 / 45%; T3 48 / 82%, 56 / 45%, 95 / 42%. A stand-in always concludes, so its wrong-and-confident rate is its whole error rate (53-62%); the tool's is 0-0.5% among the sessions that reach 0.95 but only 18-49% of its sessions do (the rest end unresolved with a leading cause that is right or wrong), so accuracy, not the confident-wrong rate, is the like-for-like number. *Predictions as operationalised: P2, P3, P5, P6, P7, P8 and P9 hold; P1 and P4 do not.* **P1 does not hold:** `fast_list` is faster than the tool at T0, T1 and T2 (66 against 88, 79 against 91, 79 against 85 minutes) but not at T2+access (91 against 85). **P4 does not hold:** `fast_confirm` is slower than `fast_list` at every arm, as predicted, but it is NOT at least as accurate: it is less accurate at T0, T1, T2 and T3 (41.3 against 46.6, 37.9 against 44.3, 40.2 against 45.7, 42.5 against 44.5) and more accurate only at T2+access (45.2 against 43.4). P2: the tool is more accurate than `fast_list` at all five arms (by 25.6, 26.9, 31.3, 40.4 and 37.9 points); P3 wrong-and-confident 0.2-0.5% against 53-57%; P5 the tool is more accurate than `fast_confirm` at all five (by 30.8 to 40.0 points); P6 at v0's level the tool is 96.6-97.5% against `habit_A`'s 72.4% at T1-T3; P7 the tool's accuracy advantage over `fast_list` at R2 is smaller than at R0 at every arm (18.5, 11.5, 17.6, 15.7, 20.9 points against 25.6, 26.9, 31.3, 40.4, 37.9); P8 the tool-to-`fast_list` minutes ratio is 0.87 at T3 against 1.35 at T0; P9 on the 62 documentation-wrong episodes the tool is 74-82% against `fast_list`'s 37-56%.
*What the numbers say (primary cell unless stated).* (1) **Against the checklist stand-in the tool buys accuracy with time at low data and wins on both with the control system.** Tool minus `fast_list`: +23 minutes (+35%) at T0, +12 (+16%) at T1, +6 (+8%) at T2, -5 (-6%) at T2+access, -7 (-13%) at T3, with +26 to +40 points of accuracy at every arm. By Addendum 21's labels the tool is TRADE (slower, more accurate) in 100%, 100%, 92%, 75% and 67% of the 12 cells at the five arms and IMPROVES in the rest of the cells at T2 and above (8%, 25%, 33%); it is never WORSE. Break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis against `fast_list`: 89 minutes at T0, 46 at T1, 21 at T2 (the tool's extra time pays if a wrong diagnosis costs more than that), and none at T2+access and T3 (the tool is ahead on both). (2) **Against the careful stand-in the tool is ahead from T1 up:** `fast_confirm` takes 101-135 minutes at T1-T3 (95 at T3), so the tool is 9% to 49% faster and 33 to 40 points more accurate there (IMPROVES in 33%, 33%, 83% and 75% of cells at T1, T2, T2+access and T3); at T0 it is 8% slower, 31 points more accurate, break-even 21 minutes. (3) **Against v0's `habit_A`, at v0's level** (the earlier question, four lumps): `habit_A` takes 13 minutes at T0 and 27 at T1 and up and is right 72% of the time, the tool takes 48-91 minutes and is right 97-98%; the tool is +21 (T3) to +75 (T0) minutes slower, and the wrong-diagnosis cost at which it pays is 87 minutes at T3 and 239-297 at the other arms. **The base rate matters:** always guessing "the box acts, cause elsewhere" is right 82% of the time on these episodes, so `habit_A` (72%) and both checklists at T1 and up (63-74%) are WORSE than a constant guess, and only the tool (97%) is clearly above it. (4) **On the 62 documentation-wrong episodes** (n = 62; the tool at T1-T3 is 74-82% right against `fast_list`'s 37-56%, `fast_confirm`'s 55-61% and `habit_A`'s 63% at v0's level) the tool is also faster than both checklists (-16% to -34% against `fast_list`, -24% to -49% against `fast_confirm`), because they exhaust their 16-check list before they look at the other boxes; against `habit_A` it is 41-57 minutes slower with a break-even of 283-361 minutes. (5) *Exploratory, not registered, same run:* averaged over all 12 cells and five arms the tool's exact accuracy is 75.7%, 49.6% and 29.5% at R0, R1 and R2 against `fast_list`'s 47.9%, 25.2% and 15.0% and `fast_confirm`'s 45.1%, 28.3% and 18.3%: the tool's lead shrinks in points as the checks get worse but it stays ahead in every tier and every cell mean; averaged over all cells `fast_list` takes 36-53 minutes and the tool 63-92.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (a) All three stand-ins are my constructions. The checklists cannot name several causes at all (actuator failure, low duct pressure, capacity below T2, "other" except by exhaustion), so a large part of the +26 to +40 points is the design of a one-cause-per-check checklist, not what a real fast technician knows, and a real technician who reasons across readings would do better than these; `habit_A` was built for another question. (b) The confirming stand-in was LESS accurate than the plain one at four of five arms. That is a property of my confirmation rule (a finding that its confirming check misses is discarded and the list moves on, ending in a walk over the boxes that raises false alarms), not evidence that double-checking hurts real technicians; the rule is not a fair model of a careful technician and P4's failure says so. (c) The stand-ins get the world's facts for free and run to the end of their list (up to 16 checks, then a walk), while the tool stops at 12 checks or 0.003 bits per minute; that flatters the tool on minutes when nothing is found, and the tool's own accuracy counts the leading cause of the 51-82% of sessions that end unresolved. (d) Answers are independent given the cause, causes come from the tool's own model family, minutes are placeholders. (e) The break-even is a threshold, not a finding that any technician's wrong diagnosis costs that much; the interviews can ask what a return visit costs. (f) Predictions P2, P3, P5-P9 were partly informed by Addenda 26 and 27 (disclosure 1) and P1 and P4 were wrong. *Not concluded:* that v1 beats, or would beat, a real technician on speed or accuracy; that double-checking does not help; anything about real check reliability or real cause frequencies. *Consequence as pre-registered ("What would follow"):* with the control system (T2+access and T3) the tool is ahead of the fast checklist on both minutes and accuracy in this model, and below that its extra minutes are repaid only if a wrong diagnosis costs more than roughly 20-90 minutes; the interviews' first question about the cost of a wrong diagnosis is therefore the one that decides whether the tool's caution is worth its time at low data.


## Addendum 29 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE the tool as it was before v1 has been run on a v1 episode): how do the minutes, checks and accuracy of the v1 tool compare with those of the v0 tool on the same complaints? (simulation only)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** After Addendum 28 the user asked whether speed got better, and then clarified that they meant speed compared to before the model change (v0 to v1). No like-for-like number exists. v0's "about 38 minutes and 3-5 checks" (Addenda 19, 23, 25, 26) come from worlds in which about 70% of episodes are "the documented box is fine and acts, the cause is elsewhere", scored on v0's four lumps; v1's 85-91 minutes (Addendum 27, T1 to T2) come from hardware-cause episodes scored on the exact cause. The two were never run on the same complaints. This addendum runs **the tool as it was before** on Addendum 27's episodes, and compares.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addenda 19-28 in full, including v0's minutes and accuracy in its own worlds and v1's numbers on Addendum 27's episodes (T1: 91 minutes, 8.6 checks, 71% exact, 97% at v0's level, wrong-and-confident 0.5%). (2) The v0-equivalent tree of v1 (`compat_v0=True`) reproduces v0's sessions and simulated episodes exactly (tests since Addendum 27), so "the tool as it was before" is that tree; it has been run on the v1 example building for a scripted demonstration only and on nothing else. (3) No v0-tool statistic on v1 episodes has been computed.

**Policies (frozen).** `v0`: the v0-equivalent tree (`compat_v0=True`: the causes "the documented box feeds it and acts, cause elsewhere", "the box does not act", "fed by box j", "unlisted"; the four v0 checks; v0's default `Settings`; `entropy` selection; tau 0.95; at most 12 checks; no bits-per-minute stop, which is v1-only). It has the same data as T1 (the equipment list and the drawings' claims) and cannot use anything more. `v1`: Addendum 27's tool at T1, T2, T2+access and T3 (T0 has no claims, which v0 needs, so T0 is not compared). Both work the same episodes.

**The world, cells and episodes (frozen; exactly Addendum 27's).** The same synthetic building, draws (`[27, cell, 0, episode]`), true causes (14 hardware causes and "the documentation is wrong"), gauge and occupant draws, 12 cells (reliability R0 / R1 / R2 x mix assumed / shifted x cover full / half), 500 episodes per cell and arm. The simulated technician answers each check from the same TRUE response as in Addendum 27 (the tool's own at R0, degraded at R1 and R2). The four v0 checks answer under a hardware cause by their refined rows (for instance a box override does not make the room respond with the air handler off), which the v0 tool does not know: that is the point of running it on v1's world. The v1 tool's results are Addendum 27's, reproduced. The v0 tool's outcome draws use `[29, cell, 0, i]`. Two subsets: hardware-cause and documentation-wrong episodes.

**Level of detail and metrics.** v0 can answer only in its four lumps, so the comparison is at **v0's level** (each true cause and each guess collapsed to its lump: fed by box j, unlisted, the box does not act (actuator, damper, power, signal, controller communications), the box acts and the cause is elsewhere). v1's exact-cause accuracy is shown for context only (v0 has none). Per policy, arm and subset: mean minutes, mean checks, mean entries typed (1 + checks, plus declared facts for v1), the share of sessions that reach 0.95 (unresolved = the rest), accuracy at v0's level (the leading cause at the end, whether or not concluded), wrong-and-confident rate at v0's level (concluded and wrong), and the accuracy of the constant guess "the box acts, cause elsewhere" (the base rate).

**Frozen comparisons and labels.** For each v1 arm and subset, v1 minus v0 on the same episodes at v0's level, with Addendum 21's labels unmodified (time FASTER / SLOWER at 5% with the paired 95% interval clear of 0; accuracy MORE / LESS at 0.02 likewise; IMPROVES / WORSE / TRADE / NEITHER; safety at 0.02 in the wrong-and-confident rate), TRADE in its two kinds; bootstrap seeds `[29, cell, 99, subset]` (0 hardware, 1 documentation). Reported as shares of the 12 cells and primary-cell (R0 / assumed / full) deltas; no verdict rule. The **break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis** of Addendum 28 (v1 as the tool, v0 as the comparison, primary cell, hardware, v0's level), descriptive.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; primary cell, hardware-cause episodes, v0's level unless stated).** P1: every v1 arm (T1, T2, T2+access, T3) takes more mean minutes than v0. P2: v1 at T1 takes more checks than v0. P3: v1 at T1 is at least 0.05 more accurate than v0. P4: v0's wrong-and-confident rate is higher than v1's at T1. P5: on the documentation-wrong episodes v1 at T1 is at least as accurate as v0 minus 0.02 (v1 has not lost v0's own competence). P6: v1 at T1 takes more minutes than v0 in every one of the 12 cells. P7: the accuracy advantage of v1 at T1 over v0 at (R2, assumed, full) is smaller than in the primary cell.

**Gates (all evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 code path: on the study's building the v0 policy reproduces v0's `sim.simulate` exactly (guess, minutes, checks) on v0's own causes for `entropy` (12 episodes); and if Addendum 27's raw file exists the v1 arms equal it exactly in all 12 cells (skipped and said so if it does not). G2 accounting: a scripted v0 transcript costs the hand-summed minutes (`confirm_acts` 6 + 3 = 9, then the diffuser override on the documented box 0 + 2 + 3 = 5, both yes, concluding "the box acts, cause elsewhere": 14 minutes); and the first 10 valid episodes of the v0 policy in every R1 and R2 cell replay identically from their recorded answers alone. G3 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 50 episodes reproduces its first 50 stored episodes. G4 completeness and pairing: all 12 cells present, every arm valid on every episode, minutes finite, and a guess for every episode.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, Mortar, any fitted quantity, any download.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The comparison is at v0's level, the only one v0 can answer, and the world is v1's (14 hardware causes, refined check rows), not v0's: it shows what the old tool does on the new complaints, not how good it was on its own. (2) v1 asks the technician questions and makes them enter facts; v0 needs none (entries are reported). (3) v1's stopping rules include the 0.003 bits-per-minute stop that v0 does not have; v0 stops only at tau or after 12 checks. (4) At v0's level the constant guess is right about 82% of the time on hardware episodes, so v0-level accuracies must be read against it. (5) Everything from Addenda 27 and 28's limitations applies: independent answers, the tool's own model family, placeholder minutes; nothing here is evidence about real technicians.

**What would follow.** The number the user asked for: how many more (or fewer) minutes and checks the model change costs on the same complaints, and what it buys at v0's level; the exact-cause capability that v1 adds is not measured against anything, because v0 has none.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v1/study_v0_vs_v1.py`, `scripts/73_troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1/` (aggregate statistics; synthetic episodes only). No existing module is modified.
**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-26, written after `troubleshoot/v1/study_v0_vs_v1.py` and `scripts/73_troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1.py`, BEFORE the study was run; none changes a policy, a level, a label, a prediction or a gate).** (1) **No new test file and no broken-versions check were written for this study, at the user's instruction** ("the testing already happened for how it was before"): the components it reuses were tested when they were built (the v0-equivalent tree reproduces v0's sessions exactly; Addendum 27's runner, `study_fast_v1`'s summaries and break-even, and Addendum 21's labels are tested), but its own new code (the v0 runner on v1 episodes, the comparison, the seven predictions, the gates, the report) is checked only by the four run-time gates above and one plumbing smoke run. (2) The v0 policy is run by a `Session` on the v0-equivalent case with its answers drawn by the same function as the stand-ins' (`F.ask_from_world`, the TRUE response under the real cause, clipped as the tool's is) from `[29, cell, 0, i]`; it does not call `check_world`, because a v0-equivalent case has only v0's causes while the episode's true cause is a v1 leaf. (3) Two yes answers do conclude at 0.976 after 14 minutes on the study's building (checked before writing the gate). (4) A plumbing smoke run (2 cells x 20 episodes, written to a scratch directory, gates not enforced) was made; only its gates table was read. **No comparison of v0 and v1 has been computed or read.**
**Status: RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-26), after the user asked for the comparison and said no tests were needed.**

**Result of Addendum 29 (2026-09-26; `scripts/73_troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1.py`, 28 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_v0_vs_v1/v0_vs_v1_report.md`, per-cell `v0_vs_v1_cells.csv`, raw `v0_vs_v1_raw.pkl` (synthetic episodes only)).** *Gates:* G1-G4 all passed (the v0 policy reproduces v0's `sim.simulate`; the v1 arms equal Addendum 27's raw file in all 12 cells; 80 degraded-reliability v0 episodes replay identically). *Primary cell (R0 / assumed / full), hardware-cause episodes (n = 438), at v0's level:* **the tool as it was before takes 49 minutes and 4.3 checks (5.3 entries typed), reaches 0.95 in 82% of sessions, and is right 80% of the time, confidently wrong 3%**; v1 at T1 (the same data) takes 91 minutes and 8.6 checks (12.5 entries), reaches 0.95 in 21% and is right 97%, confidently wrong 0%; v1 at T2 85 minutes / 8.6 checks / 97%; at T2+access 85 / 9.1 / 97%; at T3 48 / 9.2 / 97%. v1's exact-cause accuracy (which v0 cannot give) is 71%, 77%, 84% and 82% at those four arms. The constant guess "the box acts, cause elsewhere" is right 82% of the time, so **v0 on these complaints is below a constant guess** and v1 is not. *Predictions as operationalised: P2-P7 hold, P1 does not.* P1: v1 takes more minutes than v0 at T1, T2 and T2+access but **not at T3** (48.3 against 49.2). P2 checks 8.6 against 4.3; P3 accuracy 97.5% against 79.9% (+17.6 points); P4 wrong-and-confident 2.5% against 0.0%; P5 on the 62 documentation-wrong episodes v1 at T1 is 82.3% against v0's 80.6%; P6 v1 at T1 takes more minutes than v0 in all 12 cells; P7 the accuracy advantage is 12.9 points at R2 against 17.6 in the primary cell.
*What the numbers say.* (1) **At the same data (T1) v1 is slower than the tool before it: +42 minutes (+86%) and 4.4 more checks in the primary cell**, and by Addendum 21's labels it is TRADE (slower, more accurate) in 83% of the 12 cells and WORSE (slower, not more accurate) in 17%; over the cells v0 takes 49-65 minutes and 4.3-5.4 checks against v1 at T1's 87-96 minutes and 8.5-9.2 checks. At T2 and T2+access the gap is +36 minutes (+73%). (2) **v1 is no slower than v0 only at T3 (control-system access and a point map): -1 minute (-2%) in the primary cell, IMPROVES in 6 of 12 cells and TRADE in the other 6;** with half of the boxes covered T3 takes 70-78 minutes against v0's 51-65. (3) What the extra time buys at v0's level: accuracy 80% to 97% and a confident-wrong rate 3% to 0% in the primary cell, and v1's advantage holds in every cell (v0 falls to 55-65% at R2, v1 at T1 to 67-72%, at T3 69-78%). Break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis: 240, 215 and 212 minutes at T1, T2 and T2+access (v1's extra time pays if a wrong diagnosis costs more than that); at T3 v1 is ahead on both. (4) v1 also does something v0 cannot (name the exact cause, 71-84%), which this comparison does not price. (5) On the 62 documentation-wrong episodes (the question v0 was built for) v0 takes 82 minutes and is right 81% of the time, confidently wrong 13%; v1 takes 97 (T1), 100 (T2), 100 (T2+access) and 83 (T3) minutes and is right 82%, 81%, 82% and 74%, confidently wrong 0-3%: v1 is 15-18 minutes slower for the same accuracy at T1-T2+access (WORSE in 58% of cells by the frozen labels) and 6.5 points LESS accurate at T3 (WORSE in 17%, IMPROVES in 25%), but far less often confidently wrong.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (a) v0 is run on v1's world, in which the four v0 checks respond by the refined rows (a box override does not move the room with the air handler off); that is why it falls below the constant guess here, and it says nothing about v0 on its own world (about 38 minutes and 3-5 checks in Addenda 19, 23, 25, 26). (b) v1 asks the technician questions and takes about twice the entries (12.5 against 5.3); v0 asks none. (c) v0 reaches 0.95 in 82% of sessions because its question is coarse; v1's 21-49% is for the exact-cause question, so the shares are not comparable. (d) v1's bits-per-minute stop and v0's absence of one both affect minutes. (e) Simulation only, in the tool's own model family, placeholder minutes, independent answers. (f) **No test file or broken-versions check was written for this study, at the user's instruction;** it is covered by its four run-time gates and the tests of the components it reuses. *Not concluded:* that v1 is slower or faster for a real technician; anything about v0 on its own world; the value of the exact cause.


## Addendum 30 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE any joint-session code has produced a comparison): does investigating several complaints together (shared upstream causes, shared readings) save technician time over working each complaint separately, and over a sensible routine that reuses readings? (simulation only; a well-documented building with some map faults; NOT YET RUN)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** The user proposed, and then said "let's build this", a *system-wide adaptive physical test optimization*: the software plans the remaining investigation across interconnected equipment, finds tests that resolve several complaints at once (shared root causes), accounts for reliability, instrument availability, setup time and travel, and recomputes after every measurement. Earlier in the discussion (a) I judged that look-ahead over many test sequences adds little for the diagnosis problems built so far (one hidden cause is found about as well by ordering tests by probability per minute, which one-step selection already does), (b) that the most plausible new value is shared-root-cause reasoning (one reading at a shared parent, such as an air handler, explains several complaints and also checks every documented child link), and (c) that a cheap version, asking whether neighbouring rooms on the same air handler also complain, captures much of it. The user set the scope aside and asked to assume a well-documented building in which some faults in the map are possible. This addendum builds the smallest thing that can test the claim, `troubleshoot/v2/` (v0 and v1 are not modified), and asks how much the joint machinery adds beyond (b) and (c).

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addenda 19-29 in full, including v1's tier results (T2 85 minutes and 77% exact, T3 48 minutes and 82% exact in Addendum 27's primary cell) and the fast stand-ins' (Addendum 28). (2) An exploratory split of Addendum 26's stored episodes by true cause (not registered): with documented boxes right in 70% of call-outs the tool and the fast stand-in take the same time (20 and 19 minutes); the tool's extra minutes come from wrong-box (13% of call-outs, 41% of its minutes) and box-does-not-act (15%) call-outs. (3) My own reasoning in the discussion (a)-(c) above: predictions P2, P3, P7 and P9 below are not independent of it. (4) No statistic of the joint model, of the ticket policies on multi-complaint events, or of the new stand-in has been computed; the v2 code does not exist yet.

**The world (frozen).** A synthetic well-documented building: 3 air handlers (AHU-0..2), 8 boxes and 8 rooms each (24 rooms; Room-i is documented to VAV-i, VAV-i to AHU-(i div 8); the area of a room or box is its air handler's), documented attributes (reheat on boxes with i mod 3 != 0, K-factor, design airflow, inlet size, each AHU's static setpoint) and control-system point names for every box and air handler, all documented correctly. Candidates for a wrong claim are the room's 7 other boxes on the same air handler (cap 8, v1's rule). **An event** is one visit with `n >= 1` complaints, drawn from a generative model: each air handler independently is `ok` (1 - f), `ahu_fan_off` (f x 5/17) or `low_static` (f x 12/17), f being the world's upstream-fault rate (factor `shared`); each room independently has a local fault with probability rho = 0.03, its cause drawn from the v1 prior over the causes other than the two upstream ones (the 12 remaining hardware causes, "the documented box is wrong: which of the candidates" and "not on the list") under the world's P(documented box wrong) (factor `map`); a room whose true air handler is `ahu_fan_off` (`low_static`) is affected with probability 0.9 (0.5); a room complains if it has a local fault or is affected; its first-order cause is the upstream one if it is affected, else its local one; events with no complaint are redrawn. The true air handler of a box is its documented one, or with probability q_ahu (factor `map`) another one drawn uniformly (redrawn per event). Complaints arrive in a random order. The simulated technician answers a check on a room or box from the TRUE response of that check under the complaint's first-order cause (the tool's catalogue, degraded toward 0.5 by a in {0, 0.25} for factor `rel`), and a check on an air handler (the four checks whose subject is the air handler: `bas_ahu_fan`, `bas_static`, `ahu_visit`, `ahu_gauge`) from the TRUE response under that air handler's state (`ok`: the false-alarm rate). **Data and context:** two tiers on the same events, T2 (equipment list, claims, attributes: no control system) and T3 (T2 plus control-system access and a point map); the technician has an instrument and no occupant is available at either; nothing is asked. Cells: `shared` f in {0.01 (rare), 0.03 (assumed), 0.09 (common)} x `map` in {M0: q_ahu 0 and P(wrong) 0.05; M1: q_ahu 0.10 and P(wrong) 0.05; M2: q_ahu 0.10 and P(wrong) 0.15} x `rel` in {R0, R1}: 18 cells, 300 events per cell, both tiers on the same events, event draws from `[30, cell, 0, episode]`, the outcome draws of policy p at tier t from `[30, cell, 100 + 10 t + p, episode]` (t = 0 for T2, 1 for T3; p indexes the policies below in their listed order). **The tool's assumptions never change:** v1's default `Settings` with the rating `mostly_right` (P(documented box wrong) 0.05), `entropy` selection, tau 0.95, min_rate 0.003 bits per minute, at most 12 checks per complaint (12 n per event for the joint tool), and, for the joint model, f = 0.03, rho = 0.03, affected probabilities 0.9 and 0.5, and the belief that the documented air handler of every box is right.

**The joint model (frozen).** The complaints of an event are grouped by their documented air handler. Per group, the shared state u is `ok`, `ahu_fan_off` or `low_static` with prior (1 - f, f x 5/17, f x 12/17). Given u the complaints are independent: for a complaint with local-cause prior L over its causes (v1's prior with the two upstream causes removed and renormalised), P(complaint, cause c | u) is rho x L(c) under `ok`; and under an upstream state with affect probability r, r for the upstream cause and (1 - r) x rho x L(c) for a local cause c. The documented rooms of the group that did NOT complain contribute the factor (1 - P(complaint | u)) each. Observations of an air handler check multiply the weight of u by that check's P(observed | u) (the catalogue's detection level for the state, the false-alarm rate for `ok`); observations of a room or box check multiply that complaint's cause likelihood as in v1. The posterior over u and each complaint's marginal cause distribution are exact (three states per group, so no approximation) and are the belief the tool shows. **Selection:** for every check that could be done (v1's availability, minutes and location rules: an air handler check is one option per air handler, informing every complaint of its group; room, box and act-and-watch checks are options per complaint and target, never repeated), the expected reduction in the SUM over the group's complaints of the entropy of their marginals, per minute; the best option whose gain per minute is at least `min_rate` times the number of its unconcluded complainants (one for a room or box check, so the rule per complaint is v1's) is recommended; a complaint is concluded when its leading marginal is at least tau. The technician's location carries over between checks as in v1. **Optional two-step look-ahead** (`lookahead=2`): the top six options by one-step score are scored by (their gain plus, for each of their two outcomes weighted by its probability, the best follow-up's gain) over (their minutes plus the same weighted follow-up minutes).

**Policies (frozen; the order fixes the stream index p).** (0) `tool_ticket`: v1's tool, each complaint its own independent session in arrival order, v1's prior, nothing shared (what exists today). (1) `tool_ticket_nb`: as (0), but each session's prior over causes is the joint model's marginal at time zero for that complaint (the neighbour question: how many rooms on its air handler complain and how many do not), and nothing else is shared. (2) `tool_ticket_memo`: as (1), and a reading of an air handler taken in one ticket is entered, at no cost, into every later ticket on that air handler (what a sensible person using the tool would do). (3) `tool_joint`: the joint session above. (4) `tool_joint_la2`: (3) with two-step look-ahead, run only in the three cells with map M0 and rel R0. (5) `fast_ticket`: v1's `fast_list` stand-in (Addendum 28), each complaint on its own. (6) `fast_parent`: my construction of an experienced technician's obvious rule: for an air handler with two or more complaints, first the cheapest available fan check and, if normal, the cheapest available static reading; the first abnormal one names the cause (fan off; low static) for EVERY complaint of that air handler and ends their work; otherwise (or with one complaint) each complaint is worked by `fast_list`, skipping the air handler checks already done. Stand-ins are charged the tool's minutes by v1's accounting, get the facts for free, and always conclude. In the ticket policies and the stand-ins the technician's location carries over from one ticket to the next (which favours them relative to a location reset), and minutes are the sum over the event (no parallel work, no return visits).

**Metrics.** Per event and policy: total minutes, total checks, the number of complaints diagnosed correctly (exact cause; the tool's answer is its leading cause at the end, whether or not concluded), the number concluded and wrong (wrong-and-confident; a stand-in always concludes). Per event the accuracy is correct / n and the wrong-and-confident rate is (concluded and wrong) / n; comparisons average these over events (a complaint-weighted accuracy is reported alongside). Subsets: all events; `shared` (at least two complaints whose first-order cause is upstream on the same air handler); `multi` (n >= 2, not shared); `single` (n = 1).

**Frozen comparisons and labels.** Policy A minus policy B on the same events and tier, with Addendum 21's labels unmodified (time FASTER / SLOWER at 5% with the paired 95% interval clear of 0; accuracy MORE / LESS at 0.02 likewise; IMPROVES / WORSE / TRADE / NEITHER; safety at 0.02 in the wrong-and-confident rate), bootstrap `[30, cell, 99, subset]` (subsets in the order all, shared, multi, single): (A) joint - ticket; (B) joint - ticket_memo (the primary comparison); (C) ticket_memo - ticket; (D) ticket_nb - ticket; (E) joint - fast_parent; (F) joint - fast_ticket; (G) joint_la2 - joint. Reported as shares of the 18 cells (3 for G), primary-cell deltas (shared = assumed, map = M0, rel = R0) and the break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis of Addendum 28 for E and F.

**Decision (frozen; primary cell, `shared` events, both tiers read, T2 decides).** *JOINT MACHINERY ADDS VALUE* if comparison (B) is IMPROVES at T2 and not WORSE at T3. *THE ROUTINE CAPTURES IT* if (B) is not IMPROVES at T2 but (C) is IMPROVES at T2. *NO GAIN FROM SHARING* otherwise. A qualifier is appended, *UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS*, if in the four cells (M1, M2) x (T2, T3) at shared = assumed and rel = R0 the joint policy's wrong-and-confident rate exceeds ticket_memo's by at least 0.02 in at least two of them.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; primary cell unless stated).** P1: at T2 on `shared` events, `tool_joint` takes at least 15% fewer minutes than `tool_ticket` and is not LESS accurate. P2: at T2 on `shared` events, `tool_joint` takes less than 10% fewer minutes than `tool_ticket_memo` (the routine captures most of the gain). P3: at T2 on all events, `tool_ticket_nb` takes fewer minutes than `tool_ticket`. P4: the mean minutes `tool_joint` saves against `tool_ticket` on `shared` events are smaller at T3 than at T2. P5: at T2 with rel R0 and shared = assumed, `tool_joint`'s wrong-and-confident rate in M1 exceeds its rate in M0 by at least 0.01, and in M1 is at least `tool_ticket_memo`'s. P6: at T2 on all events `fast_parent` takes at least 5% fewer minutes than `tool_joint` and `tool_joint` is at least 0.10 more accurate. P7: in each of the three look-ahead cells at each tier, `tool_joint_la2` and `tool_joint` differ in minutes by less than 3% and in accuracy by less than 0.02. P8: on `single` events `tool_joint` is within 5% of `tool_ticket_nb` in minutes at each tier. P9: at shared = rare (map M0, rel R0) the advantage of `tool_joint` over `tool_ticket_memo` on all events is under 3% of minutes at each tier.

**Gates (all evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 exactness: on 200 random groups (1-6 complaints, random observations) the joint model's posterior over u and every marginal equal an independent brute-force enumeration over (u, c_1..c_k) to 1e-9, and at time zero a single complaint's marginal equals `tool_ticket_nb`'s prior. G2 code path: on `single` events whose air handler state is consistent with the complaint's cause, `tool_ticket` reproduces v1's `simulate_v1` exactly (guess, minutes, checks) from the same answer stream (25 episodes per tier); and a scripted joint transcript costs the hand-summed minutes. G3 replay: the first 10 valid events of `tool_joint`, `tool_ticket_memo` and `fast_parent` in every R1 cell replay identically from their recorded answers alone (the choices depend on the answers and on nothing about the world). G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 30 events reproduces its first 30 stored events. G5 completeness and pairing: all 18 cells present with every policy at both tiers (look-ahead in 3 cells), identical event draws (complaints, order, states) across policies and tiers, finite outputs. G6 world sanity: the share of `shared` events rises strictly with f (rare < assumed < common) within each (map, rel), and the tool's settings are identical in every cell.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, Mortar, any fitted quantity, any download.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) Simulation in the tool's own model family: the complaints are generated by the joint model's own structure (in the as-assumed cells the world equals the tool's belief), so this shows what the machinery can do when its structure is right; only the `shared`, `map` and `rel` factors stress it. (2) The rates f, rho, 0.9 and 0.5, every minute and every reading response are placeholders. (3) One kind of shared cause (an air handler); no plant, electrical or control-panel layers; one first-order cause per complaint; box and room checks are answered from the complaint's cause, not from the air handler's state. (4) All complaints arrive together and sessions run one after another: no calendar time, no return visits, no parallel work; the cost of an unshared reading is what a same-day dispatch of separate tickets would pay. (5) The stand-ins are my constructions, not real technicians; `fast_parent` in particular is my guess at an obvious rule. (6) The joint tool's objective is the sum of marginal entropies, one of several reasonable choices. (7) Instrument placement, combined measurements with shared setup and hardware costs in dollars are NOT built: the instrument is a fact declared once. (8) Nothing here is evidence about real technicians, buildings or the frequency of shared causes, which is what a real ticket log would show.

**What would follow.** The number that matters is comparison (B): whether the joint machinery earns anything beyond a routine of the neighbour question plus reusing readings, and (E): how it stands against an obvious human rule. If (B) is not an IMPROVES the proposal reduces, in this model, to that routine, which is cheap; if it is, the size of the saving over the routine is the size of the prize for planning. The frequency of shared causes and of repeat visits in real tickets remains the number that decides whether any of it matters, and it is not measured here.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v2/` (`joint.py`: the joint model and session; `world2.py`: the event world and the simulated technician; `policies.py`: the ticket policies and the two stand-ins; `study_joint.py`: cells, runner, analysis, predictions, gates), `scripts/74_troubleshoot_joint.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_v2.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_joint_study.py`, `TROUBLESHOOT_V2.md`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint/` (aggregate statistics; synthetic events only). No existing module is modified.

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-26, written after `troubleshoot/v2/*`, `scripts/74`, the tests and the broken-versions check, BEFORE the study was run at its frozen size; none changes the world, a policy's definition beyond what is stated here, a label, a prediction or a gate as written above).** (1) **Events per cell raised from 300 to 1000** (`study_joint.N_EPISODES`): the plumbing run showed the study is cheap, and at 300 events the `shared` subset of the rare cells (about 5% of visits) would have had about 15 events. The 1000 events are a prefix-compatible extension (event `i` of a cell is the same at any size, tested). (2) **A diagnosis needs an observation.** When the demonstration command line was first run on four complaints of one air handler it printed "Every complaint has a likely cause" after zero checks: with a complaint pattern that low static explains, the joint prior alone is above tau. A tool that names a cause before any reading is not what was meant, so a complaint now counts as **concluded only if its leading marginal is at least tau AND a real observation has informed it** (a check on it, or on its air handler; an `unsure` is not an observation), and complaints that are not yet informed stay in the option list whatever their marginals. The same rule is applied to the ticket sessions that use the joint marginal as their prior (`tool_ticket_nb`, `tool_ticket_memo`; for `tool_ticket` v1's prior never reaches tau, so it changes nothing there, and gate G2 confirms it), through `PriorSession`; a reading carried over by `tool_ticket_memo` counts as an observation. This departs from the literal wording "a complaint is concluded when its leading marginal is at least tau"; it was found from the demonstration, before any comparison of policies had been computed, and it is applied to every tool policy alike. (3) **`fast_parent` skips a whole question, not one check:** after it has read the fan (by the control-system read or by a visit) or the static pressure (read or gauge) for an air handler, none of the checks that ask the same question are repeated by its per-complaint checklists (the first version repeated a fan visit for every complaint, which an experienced technician would not). (4) **Gate G1's brute force covers groups of 1-3 complaints** (22^3 joint states), by an explicit joint table built with outer products and marginalised; larger groups are covered by the tests on symmetry and by the equality of the vectorised expected gain with full recomputation. (5) The two-step look-ahead is exposed as `JointSession.two_step_value` (the option's gain and minutes plus the outcome-weighted best follow-up), which the tests pin. (6) The complaint count of an event is unbounded (a fan-off air handler gives about 7 complaints); the tool's check limit is 12 per complaint. (7) **What was seen before the run (disclosure):** the plumbing smoke run of `scripts/74` on cells 0 and 1 with 20 events written to a scratch directory (gates read, numbers not compared); an earlier six-event smoke in one unregistered cell (f = 0.09) in which I saw that every policy runs and their minutes and correct counts for those six events, and computed no comparison; the demonstration outputs (fan off with seven complaints, low static with four). (8) **Tests and broken versions:** 99 new tests (69 for the model, world, policies and command line, 30 for the study code) pass and the earlier suites are untouched; **140 deliberately broken versions** (59 of the joint model, 25 of the world, 21 of the policies, 27 of the study code and its gates, 8 of the command line), each run with bytecode caching off in a fresh copy, are all caught, after the first pass left 8 alive and the second 3 (each fixed by a new test or a refactor that exposed the quantity; none had to be written off as equivalent). (9) A command line (`python -m docmismatch.troubleshoot.v2`, `troubleshoot/v2/cli.py`) and an example building (`examples/troubleshoot_example/building_v2.json`, generated from the study's building) were added; they do not enter the study. (10) Wrong-and-confident is defined per complaint (concluded and wrong); a stand-in always concludes, so its rate is its error rate.
**Status: RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-26), after the user said "let's build this".**

**Result of Addendum 30 (2026-09-26; `scripts/74_troubleshoot_joint.py`, 352 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint/joint_report.md`, per-cell `joint_cells.csv`, raw `joint_raw.pkl` (synthetic visits only)).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (the joint model equals brute-force enumeration to 5e-15 on 200 random groups; `tool_ticket` reproduces v1's `simulate_v1` on 50 single-complaint events; the scripted transcript costs 37 minutes; 540 replayed events identical; reproducibility; completeness; the share of shared-cause visits is 5% / 15% / 37% for f = rare / assumed / common). *The visits (primary cell):* 2.11 complaints per visit on average; 57% one complaint, 26% several without a shared cause, 17% a shared upstream cause. **Frozen decision: THE ROUTINE CAPTURES IT** (no UNSAFE-UNDER-MAP-FAULTS qualifier). P1, P2, P3, P6, P8 and P9 held; **P4, P5 and P7 did not.**
*Numbers (primary cell, all visits, minutes per visit / share of complaints right):* T2: `tool_ticket` 151 / 78%, `tool_ticket_nb` 120 / 80%, `tool_ticket_memo` 114 / 78%, `tool_joint` 124 / 80%, `tool_joint_la2` 115 / 79%, `fast_ticket` 190 / 46%, `fast_parent` 104 / 57%; T3: 93 / 77%, 68 / 83%, 64 / 83%, 71 / 85%, 71 / 84%, 129 / 44%, 72 / 54%. On shared-cause visits: T2 `tool_ticket` 296 / 87%, `tool_ticket_memo` 97 / 93%, `tool_joint` 128 / 97%, `fast_parent` 113 / 80%; T3 225 / 86%, 43 / 95%, 55 / 96%, 52 / 76%.
*What the numbers say.* (1) **The value is in using what the other complaints say, and a routine gets it.** `tool_ticket_memo` against `tool_ticket` (C): IMPROVES in 100% (T2) and 94% (T3) of the 18 cells; on shared-cause visits it takes 67% (T2) and 81% (T3) fewer minutes (primary: -199 and -182 minutes) at equal or higher accuracy; the neighbour question alone (D) already gives -56% and -72% on shared-cause visits. (2) **The joint machinery did not beat that routine** (B, the primary comparison): IMPROVES in 0% of cells on all visits at both tiers, WORSE in 83%; on shared-cause visits +31 minutes (+32%) at T2 and +12 (+29%) at T3 for +3.6 and +1.3 points of accuracy; on visits with several unrelated complaints it is slower than the routine (+11% and +16%) for +1.6 and +3.3 points. Against `tool_ticket` (A) it is faster on average (T2 -18%, T3 -23%; on shared-cause visits -57% and -76%) but slower on visits with several unrelated complaints (+8%, +20%), the cost of looking for an upstream cause that is not there. (3) **Against the stand-ins (my constructions, not technicians):** `tool_joint` against `fast_parent`, primary cell, all visits: T2 +20 minutes (+19%) and +22.7 points of accuracy, wrong-and-confident 0.8% against 42.9% (break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis 89 minutes); T3 -0.3 minutes and +31 points. The stand-ins' accuracy is low because a checklist names one cause per reading (the caveat of Addendum 28). Against `fast_ticket` the joint tool is 35% (T2) and 45% (T3) faster. (4) **Look-ahead (P7 failed):** two-step against one-step differ by 0.7-7.1% in minutes and up to 0.041 in accuracy across the six (cell, tier) pairs; at T2 in the primary cell `tool_joint_la2` takes 115 minutes against 124 at the same accuracy (IMPROVES in 1 of 3 cells, NEITHER in 2), at T3 it is a wash (WORSE in 1 of 3). It is not shown to be nothing and not shown to be worth its cost; three cells only. (5) **Map faults and worse checks:** with 10% of the air-handler claims wrong (M1) `tool_joint`'s wrong-and-confident rate rose from 0.8% to 1.7%, the same as `tool_ticket_memo`'s (P5's first half missed by 0.001: +0.009 against +0.01); with checks 25% toward a coin flip every tool policy falls to 47-59% accuracy and 4-9% confidently wrong, and no policy is specially brittle. (6) **P4 failed:** the minutes saved against `tool_ticket` on shared-cause visits are the same at T3 (170) as at T2 (168): even with 2-minute control-system reads the ticket policy spends about 37 checks on such a visit, so the saving comes from not working through causes ticket by ticket, not from the price of reading an air handler.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (a) **My own conclusion rule (clarification 2) probably handicaps the joint tool, and the routine as well.** A reading at one box that bears on the air handler (the inlet static pressure) informs the shared state and so the other complaints' beliefs, but the rule marks only that complaint as informed, so each complaint still needs a reading of its own before it can conclude. In an exploratory look at the three stored shared-cause visits of the primary cell (not registered, after the result), both tool policies read the inlet static at each of four boxes (48 minutes) in a low-static visit where one gauge reading at the air handler (17 minutes) informs all four. The tool's entropy objective does not know about the rule, so it does not plan for it. This has not been tested; a rule under which a reading that discriminates the air handler's state informs the whole group would probably help both policies, and by how much is unknown. (b) The world is generated by the joint model's own structure and the numbers f, rho and the affect probabilities are placeholders, so this shows what the machinery can do when its structure is right. (c) Minutes are one technician's checks added up: no return visits, calendar time or parallel work; instrument placement, combined measurements and dollar costs are not built. (d) The stand-ins are my constructions. (e) Nothing here says how often real call-outs have a shared cause or a repeat visit, which is what decides whether any of this matters. *Not concluded:* that joint planning beats, or loses to, a real technician; that look-ahead is worthless; anything about real buildings.
*What would follow.* In this model the shared-cause value is real and cheap: ask how many other rooms on the same air handler are affected, and do not read the same air handler twice; that routine takes about two-thirds to four-fifths of the minutes off shared-cause visits. The full joint machinery added 1-4 points of accuracy for 10-30% more minutes there and cost time where there is no shared cause. Before deciding whether the machinery earns anything, a follow-up (not started; it would be its own addendum) should let a reading that bears on the air handler count for the whole group, in both the routine and the joint tool.


## Addendum 31 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE any policy with the group-informing rule has been run): does letting a reading that bears on the air handler count for the whole group change the verdict of Addendum 30? (simulation only; NOT YET RUN)

**Motivation (discussed with the user before writing this).** Addendum 30's frozen decision was THE ROUTINE CAPTURES IT. In the result paragraph I recorded a flaw in my own conclusion rule (clarification 2 of Addendum 30): a complaint is concluded only after a real reading has informed it, and the rule marks as informed only the complaint that was read, or (for a reading of the air handler) its whole group. A reading of the inlet static pressure at ONE box does bear on the air handler, and therefore on the other complaints of its group, but under the rule it informs only its own complaint; in an exploratory look at three stored low-static visits both tool policies read the inlet static at each of four boxes (48 minutes) where one air-handler gauge reading (17 minutes) or one box reading that counted for the group would have done. The user asked for this follow-up. It tests whether the rule handicapped the joint tool (and the routine) and whether the verdict changes when the rule is repaired for both.

**What has been seen before writing this (disclosure).** (1) Addendum 30's report in full, all cells, and its raw file; (2) the exploratory look at three stored shared-cause visits (above); (3) the predictions below are informed by both: I expect the repair to help both policies at T2, where the air-handler gauge costs 17 minutes and a box reading 12, and to matter little at T3, and I expect the joint tool to close most of its gap to the routine without clearly beating it; P4, P5 and P9 are not independent of that expectation. (4) No policy with the new rule has been run on any event, in any test beyond tiny scripted checks whose numbers are not read.

**The repair (frozen).** *The rule.* A check on a room or box **bears on the air handler** if it is a reading check whose probability of coming back abnormal is at least 0.5 under BOTH upstream causes (`ahu_fan_off`, `low_static`) and at most 0.5 under every other cause and as a false alarm. In v1's catalogue that is exactly one check, `static_at_box` (0.92, 0.92 and a 0.04 false-alarm rate); a flow hood, the airflow read and the damper read respond to many local causes and do not qualify. **With the rule on:** (a) an answered (not `unsure`) reading of such a check on any complaint informs EVERY complaint of its air handler's group (the joint model already updates all of their beliefs through the shared state; only the informed flag changes); (b) its audience for the rate rule is the number of unconcluded complaints of the group, as for an air-handler reading (the option's gain is already summed over the group). Nothing else in the joint model, its selection, its stopping or its look-ahead changes. *The routine's counterpart* (a sensible person's rule, `tool_ticket_memo2`): as `tool_ticket_memo`, and an ABNORMAL `static_at_box` reading taken in one ticket is entered, at no cost, as an abnormal `static_at_box` reading into every later ticket on the same air handler (low static in the duct is a property of the air handler; a normal reading at one box says little about the others and is not carried). With the rule off everything is Addendum 30's, byte for byte (gate G1).

**Policies (frozen; the order fixes the stream index p after Addendum 30's seven).** (7) `tool_ticket_memo2`; (8) `tool_joint2`: the joint session with the rule on; (9) `tool_joint2_la2`: the same with two-step look-ahead, run only in the three cells with map M0 and rel R0. Addendum 30's policies are not re-run except in gate G1: their stored results are the comparison partners (same events, different answer draws).

**The world, cells and events (frozen; exactly Addendum 30's).** The same 18 cells, the same event draws (stream `[30, cell, 0, event]`, 1000 events per cell), both tiers. The outcome draws of the new policies use `[31, cell, 100 + 10 t + p, event]` (t = 0 for T2, 1 for T3; p = 7, 8, 9). The tool's assumptions never change (Addendum 30's).

**Metrics, subsets, labels.** As in Addendum 30 (per-event minutes, accuracy, wrong-and-confident; subsets all, shared, multi, single; Addendum 21's labels; bootstrap `[31, cell, 99, subset]`).

**Frozen comparisons.** (H) joint2 - ticket_memo2 (**primary**: with both repaired, does the machinery add anything to the routine); (I) joint2 - joint (what the repair did for the joint tool); (J) ticket_memo2 - ticket_memo (what it did for the routine); (K) joint2 - fast_parent; (L) joint2 - ticket; (M) joint2_la2 - joint2. Reported as shares of the 18 cells (3 for M), primary-cell deltas (shared = assumed, map = M0, rel = R0), and the break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis for K.

**Decision (frozen; primary cell, `shared` events).** *JOINT MACHINERY NOW ADDS VALUE* if (H) is IMPROVES at T2 and not WORSE at T3. *THE ROUTINE STILL WINS* if it is not that and (H) is WORSE at T2 or at T3. *NO EDGE EITHER WAY* otherwise. The qualifier *UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS* is appended if in the four cells (M1, M2) x (T2, T3) at shared = assumed and rel = R0 the wrong-and-confident rate of `tool_joint2` exceeds `tool_ticket_memo2`'s by at least 0.02 in at least two of them.

**Predictions written before the run (each strict; primary cell, `shared` events, T2, unless stated).** P1: (I) minutes at least 15% fewer (r_min <= -0.15). P2: (J) minutes at least 10% fewer (r_min <= -0.10). P3: at T3 (I) changes minutes by less than 10% in either direction (|r_min| < 0.10). P4: (H) is not IMPROVES at T2. P5: |r_min| of (H) is under 0.15 at T2 (the two end up within 15%). P6: on ALL events, at each tier, `tool_joint2`'s wrong-and-confident rate is at most `tool_joint`'s plus 0.01. P7: on ALL events, at each tier, `tool_joint2`'s accuracy is at least `tool_joint`'s minus 0.02. P8: at T2 with rel R0 and shared = assumed, in each of M1 and M2, `tool_joint2`'s wrong-and-confident rate (all events) exceeds `tool_ticket_memo2`'s by less than 0.02. P9: in at least two of the three look-ahead cells at each tier (M) is not IMPROVES. P10: on `single` events, at each tier, `tool_joint2` is within 5% of `tool_joint` in minutes (the rule cannot matter with one complaint).
