**Gates (all evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 regression: in all 18 cells the events (complaint counts and kinds) equal Addendum 30's stored ones, and Addendum 30's `tool_joint` and `tool_ticket_memo` re-run on the first 20 events of every cell at both tiers with Addendum 30's streams reproduce its stored minutes and correct counts exactly (the rule off changes nothing). G2 the rule: on 100 random groups of 2-4 complaints with random observations the joint model's beliefs with the rule on equal brute-force enumeration and equal the beliefs with the rule off (the rule changes only who is informed and the rate rule's audience); a `static_at_box` answer (either way) informs the whole group with the rule on and only its complaint with it off; every other room or box reading informs only its complaint; an `unsure` informs nobody; and exactly one catalogue check bears on the air handler. G3 replay: the first 10 events of `tool_joint2` and `tool_ticket_memo2` in every R1 cell, fed only their recorded answers, ask for the same checks in the same order and end with the same guesses. G4 reproducibility: the first cell re-run with 30 events reproduces its first 30 stored events. G5 completeness and pairing: all 18 cells, every new policy at both tiers (look-ahead in 3 cells), finite outputs, 1000 events. G6 the tool's settings equal Addendum 30's in every cell.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, T1-T6 suites, Mortar, any fitted quantity, any download.

**Limitations named up front.** Those of Addendum 30 apply in full (the world is the joint model's own; placeholder numbers; one technician's minutes added up; stand-ins are mine; nothing about real buildings or the frequency of shared causes). In addition: (1) the rule is a repair I designed after seeing the result it repairs, so this follow-up cannot be independent of Addendum 30; it can only show whether the verdict was an artefact of the rule; (2) only one catalogue check bears on the air handler, so the repair is narrow by construction; (3) carrying an abnormal box reading to other boxes of the air handler is my construction of what a sensible person does, and treating a low reading at one box as the same reading at another is an assumption (in the world every box answers from its own complaint's cause); (4) the joint tool's tendency to keep confirming after an upstream cause is likely (the rate rule scales with the audience) is not changed here and may still cost it minutes.

**What would follow.** If (H) at T2 is IMPROVES, the earlier verdict was partly an artefact of the rule and the joint machinery earns something over the routine (and by how much is the number to read). If (H) is NO EDGE, the two are equivalent in this model and the simpler routine is the one to build. If (H) is WORSE, the joint machinery costs time even with a fair rule and the confirmation behaviour is the next thing to look at. The size of the repair's own effect on each policy, (I) and (J), is reported whatever (H) says.

**Implementation to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v2/joint.py` (`bears_on_ahu`, an off-by-default `group_informing` switch), `policies.py` (`tool_ticket_memo2`, `tool_joint2`, `tool_joint2_la2`), `study_joint2.py`, `scripts/75_troubleshoot_joint2.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_joint2.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint2/` (aggregate statistics; synthetic events only). Addendum 30's code paths and results are not modified (the switch defaults to off; gate G1).

**Clarifications made while implementing (2026-09-26, written after `troubleshoot/v2/joint.py` (the switches), `policies.py`, `study_joint2.py`, `scripts/75` and the tests, BEFORE the study was run; two of them change the registered design and are marked as such).** (1) **CHANGE: the rate threshold of a bearing room or box reading is NOT scaled by the group** (part (b) of the rule as registered is dropped; its audience is still shown as the number of unconcluded complaints of the group, but its threshold stays `min_rate`, as for every room or box check in Addendum 30). Reason, found in a scripted test before any run: with the scaled threshold, in a visit whose complaint pattern already makes low static 96% likely, no option clears the scaled rate threshold and the repaired tool stops after zero checks with every complaint unconcluded, which would have made the effect of the informing rule uninterpretable. (2) **ADDITION: a secondary policy, `tool_joint3` (index 10)**, the joint session with the informing rule on AND the air-handler reading's rate threshold unscaled (`min_rate` instead of `min_rate` x its audience). Reason, found in the same test: in Addendum 30 an air-handler option had to clear 4 x 0.003 bits per minute for four complaints while a box reading had to clear 0.003, which is why the Addendum 30 joint tool read the inlet static at four boxes (48 minutes) where the air-handler gauge (17 minutes) informs all four: the scaling handicapped the air-handler option relative to the box options and relative to the ticket policies, and I had not registered it as a lever. Comparisons N (joint3 - ticket_memo2) and O (joint3 - joint2) and prediction **P11** (at T2 on shared-cause visits `tool_joint3` takes at least 10% fewer minutes than `tool_joint2`; r_min <= -0.10 for O) are SECONDARY: the decision stays on (H); N's label is reported next to it, descriptively. The switch is `JointModel(scale_audience=False)`; the default (True) is Addendum 30's (gate G1). (3) P9 is evaluated on `shared` events (the registration's default for predictions without a stated subset). (4) Gate G2's brute force covers groups of 2-4 complaints (22^k joint states; explicit tables built with outer products), and its informing checks are on a real group of three; the rule-off beliefs are compared with the rule-on beliefs after the same random observations. (5) **What was seen before the run (disclosure):** the plumbing smoke run of `scripts/75` on cells 0 and 1 with 20 events (scratch directory; gates read, numbers not compared) BEFORE change (1); a scripted four-complaint low-static event in which, with the joint tool's Addendum 30 rules, four inlet static readings (48 minutes) concluded the group, and with the registered scaled threshold the repaired tool did nothing; no statistic of any new policy on any study event. (6) **Tests and broken versions:** 35 new tests (`tests/test_troubleshoot_joint2.py`) pass together with the 99 of Addendum 30 (134 in all); **69 deliberately broken versions of the new code** (the rule and its switches, the new policies, the study code and its gates), run in fresh copies with bytecode caching off and Addendum 30's raw file present, are all caught but one, which is equivalent (a set union that already contains the element); Addendum 30's 140 broken versions are all still caught with the changed code, and the full suite passed before this addendum's code was added (810 tests). (7) Addendum 30's code paths are unchanged with the switches at their defaults, which gate G1 checks against its stored results.
**Status: RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-26), after the user asked for the follow-up.**

**Result of Addendum 31 (2026-09-26; `scripts/75_troubleshoot_joint2.py`, 315 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint2/joint2_report.md`, per-cell `joint2_cells.csv`, raw `joint2_raw.pkl` (synthetic visits only)).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (1440 re-run Addendum 30 episodes identical; the rule changes only who is informed, beliefs equal brute force to 5e-14 and equal the rule-off beliefs; 360 replays identical; reproducibility; completeness; settings). **Frozen decision: THE ROUTINE STILL WINS** (no UNSAFE qualifier). P1, P2, P3, P4, P6, P7, P8, P9 and P10 held; **P5 and P11 did not.**
*Numbers (primary cell, shared-cause visits, minutes per visit / share of complaints right / wrong-and-confident):* T2: `tool_ticket` 296 / 87% / 0.1%, `tool_ticket_memo` 97 / 93% / 1.7%, **`tool_ticket_memo2` 82 / 93% / 2.2%**, `tool_joint` 128 / 97% / 0.5%, **`tool_joint2` 97 / 95% / 2.4%**, `tool_joint2_la2` 100 / 96% / 2.1%, `tool_joint3` 92 / 96% / 2.1%, `fast_parent` 113 / 80% / 19.9%. T3: 225 / 86% / 2.0%, 43 / 95% / 2.4%, **41 / 95% / 2.3%**, 55 / 96% / 2.8%, **53 / 95% / 3.1%**, 64 / 97% / 2.2%, 49 / 96% / 2.5%, 52 / 76% / 24.0%. All visits, primary cell: T2 memo 114, memo2 112, joint 124, joint2 119, joint3 118; T3 64, 65, 71, 71, 72.
*What the numbers say.* (1) **The flaw was real, and it explains about half of the machinery's deficit without a control system.** The repair took 25% (31.5 minutes) off the joint tool's shared-cause visits at T2 (128 to 97; IMPROVES in 39% of the 18 cells, NEITHER in the rest) and 4% at T3 (P1 and P3 held); it also took 15% off the routine's at T2 (97 to 82; IMPROVES in 72% of cells; P2 held) and 4% at T3. Accuracy on shared-cause visits fell 1.3 points for the joint tool (97% to 95%) and its confident-wrong rate rose from 0.5% to 2.4% (safety regression flagged in 11% of cells at T2), the same level as the routine's; over all visits neither moved (P6 and P7 held). (2) **The routine still wins** (H, the primary comparison): on shared-cause visits the repaired joint tool is still slower than the repaired routine, +14 minutes (+17%) at T2 (WORSE in 56% of cells, NEITHER in 39%) and +12 minutes (+29%) at T3 (WORSE in 67%, TRADE in 33%), for +2.2 and +0.4 points of accuracy; on all visits +6% and +10% (WORSE in 83% of cells at both tiers). Before the repair the gap was +32% (T2) and +29% (T3): the repair closed about half of it at T2 and none at T3. P5 failed (the two are not within 15% at T2). (3) **Against the fast stand-in (mine):** on shared-cause visits `tool_joint2` is 14% (16 minutes) faster than `fast_parent` at T2 and equal at T3, with 15 and 19 points more accuracy; on all visits it is 14% slower at T2 (+23 points of accuracy; break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis 64 minutes) and equal at T3 (+31 points). (4) **The secondary arm** (`tool_joint3`: the air-handler reading's rate threshold unscaled) changed little: 5% (T2) and 7% (T3) fewer minutes than `tool_joint2` on shared-cause visits (P11 failed: not 10%), and it is still slower than the routine (+12% and +21%). (5) Look-ahead on top of the repair is not clearly better (NEITHER in all three cells at T2, one WORSE at T3; P9 held). (6) One complaint, the rule cannot matter and does not (P10: within 1.1% and 2.1%).
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (a) **This does not change the verdict of Addendum 30; it sharpens it.** With a fair conclusion rule the shared-cause saving belongs to the cheap routine (neighbour question, one reading per air handler, and a low inlet static carried to the other tickets), and the joint machinery costs 12-29% more minutes on such visits for 0.4-2.2 points more accuracy and slower still where complaints are unrelated. (b) The joint tool's remaining excess (about 0.6 to 0.9 more checks and 12-15 more minutes on a shared-cause visit) is not explained by anything tested here: the candidates are its entropy-sum objective favouring dear checks that inform several complaints, and its continuing to confirm after an upstream cause is likely; neither has been isolated. (c) **The repair was designed after seeing Addendum 30's result, so this is not an independent test;** it shows only that the earlier verdict was not merely an artefact of the rule. (d) The simulated worlds are the joint model's own, the numbers are placeholders, minutes are one technician's checks added up, the stand-in is mine; nothing here says how often real call-outs have a shared cause, which is what decides whether any of it matters. *Not concluded:* that joint planning could not beat a routine with a different objective or stopping rule; anything about real technicians or buildings.
*What would follow.* In this model the routine is the thing to build and to test in the field: it takes two-thirds to four-fifths of the minutes off shared-cause visits (Addendum 30) and, with the low-static carry, another 15% at T2. The joint machinery has not earned its complexity here. What decides whether any of it is worth doing is real data: how often a call-out is one of several complaints with a shared cause, and what a repeat visit costs.

## Addendum 32 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE any work-order text has been read and BEFORE any language model has been run on any record): does evidence that a language model pulls out of historical work orders change the routine's next physical check in a way that pays, beyond what a conventional search of the same records already gives? (retrospective study on real records; PROTOCOL ONLY; NOT YET RUN: blocked on data and on an expert)

**Origin.** The user's fourth proposal (an LLM assistant beside the engine) and their design for testing it (2026-09-26): compare (A) the routine alone, (B) the routine with conventional work-order search, (C) the routine with LLM-extracted evidence, on real maintenance records; a domain expert verifies extracted information before it can influence a decision; measure how often the LLM correctly changes the recommended next physical test and whether those changes could cut technician labour compared with ordinary search; do NOT implement the LLM first. This addendum turns that into a protocol. Changes I made to the user's design are marked **CHANGE**.

**What the study needs, and what exists.** Needed: (1) dated orders with a room or equipment identifier; (2) the text written when an order was opened kept apart from the text written when it was closed; (3) a recorded cause an expert can check; (4) the building's equipment map, so the engine can be run; (5) a domain expert. The repository holds no work-order text of any kind. The one public corpus I found, the Facility Management Unified Classification Database (FMUCD; Mendeley Data DOI 10.17632/cb8d2nsjss.1, CC BY 4.0; 3,693,867 orders from 12 North American universities, 2002-2021; per its paper 38 columns: university, building, system, subsystem, component, a free-text `WODescription`, start and end dates, cost, labour hours, weather), has NO room or asset-ID column and no separate closure note as far as the paper describes. It can support Stage 1 (does the text carry usable evidence at all) but not Stage 2 (the three-arm replay): without a room or equipment ID the routine cannot be run on a visit and history cannot be linked to the right equipment. Stage 2 needs an export from a real building or campus; nothing public that I found provides one. Nothing has been downloaded; file sizes on the Mendeley page could not be retrieved.

**Stage 1: does the text carry evidence? (no engine, no minutes; needs the user's permission to download.)** Sample 600 unplanned-maintenance orders classed as HVAC by the corpus's own codes (frame chosen from the code columns alone, before any description is read), random with seed 32, stratified by university. An expert labels four kinds of statement in each description: (a) a room or equipment reference; (b) a prior-repair or recurrence statement; (c) a documentation, tag or location mismatch; (d) a mention of shared equipment ("whole wing", "AHU-3"). Two extractors run on the same 600: a keyword search (list frozen in the script before any description is read) and a language model (model and date named; prompt frozen before any description is read; temperature 0; raw outputs stored). Reported per kind: the share of orders carrying it (expert-verified) and each extractor's recall and precision against the expert labels; and the LLM-ONLY share = orders with a verified (b), (c) or (d) statement that the keyword search does not surface. **Frozen stop rule: STOP (the idea cannot reach the Stage 2 bar on this kind of text) if the LLM-only share is below 10% of orders, or if fewer than 70% of the language model's extracted statements are confirmed by the expert.** Why 10%: the Stage 2 bar (below) is 5 minutes per visit averaged over ALL visits; the saving on a visit whose history names the fix is unlikely to exceed about 40 minutes (my assumption: two to three skipped checks at v1 catalogue minutes), so an edge over search needs about 5/40 = 12.5% of visits affected, and I stop a little below that. A Stage 1 pass is NOT a go for the tool: it says nothing about whether history is linked to the right equipment.

**CHANGE (labels).** No domain expert exists in this session. If the labels are mine (second pass by a differently prompted agent, disagreements listed), the result is reported as NOT expert-verified; the stop rule still applies, but no positive statement rests on such labels.

**Stage 2: the three arms (only with a suitable export).** Same visits, same engine (v1 with the shared-cause routine and the conclusion rule of Addendum 31), same declared data tier, same equipment map; only the source of history differs. The candidate set of a visit = orders opened and closed before it, within 24 months, tied to its room, its box, its documented air handler, or another room on that air handler. (A) no history. (B) conventional search: keyword filter (Stage 1's frozen list) over the candidate set, the 5 most recent hits shown; an expert reads them and writes facts (the same reading step as C, so B is not handicapped by an unread record); sensitivity: B with no cap. (C) the language model reads the whole candidate set and proposes facts. **Every fact, from B or C, is verified by the expert, blind to the arm (facts pooled and shuffled), before the engine sees it; unverified facts are dropped.** A fact = (type, equipment, cause in the v1 vocabulary, source order ID and span, date); facts that cannot be put in v1's vocabulary are logged and dropped (their share is reported).

**Facts to engine (frozen mapping; placeholders, not fitted).** A verified prior repair or recurrence of cause X at the complaint's equipment within 12 months multiplies that cause's prior odds by m (m = 5; 2 and 10 reported as a sweep). A verified mismatch statement for the room's documented box sets the "documented box is wrong" prior for that room to 0.5. A verified shared-equipment mention of another room on the same air handler within 7 days counts as a neighbour complaint for the routine. Nothing else moves the engine.

**Leakage rules.** (1) The engine and both arms see only the complaint text as first written and orders closed before the visit's order opened; never the visit's own closing text or any later order. (2) The cause used for replay is the expert-verified cause from the closing text; visits with no verifiable cause are excluded and their share is reported (the selection leans toward well-documented orders). (3) Stage 2 records must not be public (the model may have seen public text). (4) The language model's prompt and the mapping are frozen before any Stage 2 record is opened.

**Replay and outcomes (frozen).** Per labelled visit and arm the engine runs against a scripted oracle built from the verified cause (the mechanism of Addendum 30) with v1 catalogue minutes, giving minutes, checks, first recommended check, accuracy. This measures the ENGINE under placeholder numbers, not a technician's labour; the study cannot measure labour. Per visit and arm: CHANGE = the first recommended check differs from A's (information-equivalent ties count as no change); CORRECT change = replayed minutes strictly lower than A's; HARMFUL = strictly higher. **Primary: net minutes per visit, C minus B and B minus A, averaged over ALL labelled visits (not just visits with evidence), where net includes 2 minutes of expert verification per fact (measured if the expert logs time; 1 and 5 as a sweep).** Secondary: change, correct and harmful rates; fact precision; the LLM-ONLY share; the share of visits whose verified evidence pointed away from the true cause.

**Decision (frozen).** *LLM EDGE OVER SEARCH:* C minus B is at least 5 minutes per visit net, its paired-bootstrap 95% lower bound is above 2 minutes, harmful changes are at most half of correct changes, and the edge is still at least 2 minutes at m = 2. *SEARCH SUFFICES:* not that, and B minus A is at least 2 minutes. *HISTORY DOES NOT HELP THE ROUTINE:* B minus A and C minus A both under 2 minutes. *UNDERPOWERED:* fewer than 400 labelled visits (rough: a 95% interval half-width of 2 minutes needs about 140 visits if the spread of per-visit differences is what a 10% share saving 40 minutes gives (sd about 12), and about 380 at sd 20; the real spread is unknown, so I take the larger).

**Not tested here.** Whether the assistant asks technicians better questions (a retrospective cannot answer a question); whether any of it saves real labour (needs a paired field trial with technicians using the same instruments); dispatch triage before the visit.

**Limitations named up front.** Retrospective: only logged orders, hindsight labels, expert time; the conventional-search baseline is my construction (a stronger one reads every hit); the mapping multipliers are placeholders; the replay is the engine's own model; the 40-minute and 10% figures are my assumptions; FMUCD cannot link history to equipment; one model, one prompt; nothing transfers to other buildings without their own records. The language model is a measurement device here, not part of the tool.

**What would follow.** Stage 1 STOP: drop the LLM for history evidence on this kind of text; the routine stays as it is. Stage 1 pass with no Stage 2 data: nothing further can be concluded without an export of one real building's orders with room and equipment IDs, its equipment tree, and an expert. Stage 2 LLM EDGE: a candidate for a paired field trial, not a result. SEARCH SUFFICES: use conventional search, no LLM.

**Implementation, to be written after the user's go-ahead and BEFORE any run, then frozen:** `scripts/76_workorder_screen.py` (Stage 1: sampling, keyword list, LLM prompt, scoring) with tests on synthetic text; nothing in the tool changes and no LLM code enters `troubleshoot/`. Nothing downloaded, nothing run.

**Clarifications made after the user approved the download and after pass 1, BEFORE any description was read or any language model run (2026-09-26).** (1) **Pass 1** (`scripts/76_workorder_screen.py codes`, code columns and emptiness of the description only): the file was streamed (never stored) and hashed; SHA-256 and size equal the published ones (4464648252c4bdca...9f006a; 1,439,574,384 bytes); 3,731,442 rows, 0 malformed; the paper's 3,693,867 classified orders plus 37,575 rows with no classification make up every row. Python's own HTTP client got a 403 from the site (nothing was fetched); the stream comes from `curl` instead, which is what `scripts/76` documents. Only about 30 KB of counts are on disk. (2) **The frame, fixed from the code columns alone:** unplanned maintenance (`PPM/UPM` = UPM), system code D30 (HVAC), subsystem code D3040 (distribution), D3050 (terminal and package units) or D3060 (controls and instrumentation) by CODE, whatever the description column of the subsystem says (some rows say only "HVAC"), and a non-empty work-order description: the parts of HVAC a room complaint can reach; plant (D3020, D3030), testing and balancing, "other" and "general" are out. About 278,000 orders in 12 universities are eligible (from pass 1: UPM D30 in those three subsystems). (3) **The sample:** 50 per university x 12 universities = 600, uniform random within a university (algorithm R, generator seeded by "32|university", so the draw depends only on the file); if a university has fewer than 50 eligible orders the run stops (no rule for that case). (4) **Headline shares are averages over universities with equal weight** (each university is one source of records, which is what a building is in Stage 2); volume-weighted shares (weights = eligible orders per university, counted in pass 2) and the range over universities are also reported; the stop rule is applied to the equal-weight figures, and a different verdict under volume weights is flagged. Order volume is dominated by two universities. (5) **The LLM-ONLY share is defined strictly:** an order counts if the language model extracted a (B), (C) or (D) statement AND the labels confirm it AND the keyword search does not fire for that kind on that order. (This makes the frozen 10% rule harder to pass than the looser wording above.) (6) **Extractor and labellers, run as separate agents that never see each other's output or the keyword hits:** the extractor is one Claude model (`sonnet`, claude-sonnet-5) that sees only the order id and the description; two independent labellers with differently worded prompts (`opus`, claude-opus-5-5, and `fable`, claude-fable-5-1) see the same; each writes its file and returns only a line count, so nobody reads outputs before they are all written. **DEVIATION: temperature cannot be set on these agents, so "temperature 0" is not achievable; each agent is run once per chunk and raw outputs are stored.** Disagreements between the two labellers are adjudicated by me from the words alone, listed with their ids, and the adjudicated labels are the reference. All three are Claude models, so their errors are correlated: the labels are NOT expert-verified and are reported that way. (7) **Frozen before any description was read:** the frame, seed, per-university size, kept columns, keyword search (`workorders.KEYWORDS`), the extractor prompt and the two labeller prompts, fingerprinted together (`workorders.prompts_sha256()`, recorded with the sample); tests `tests/test_workorders.py`. (8) Descriptions are treated as data: agents are told to ignore any instruction that appears inside one.

**Further clarifications, written after the sample was drawn and the 12 extractor and labeller runs were launched, BEFORE any of their output or any description was opened (2026-09-26).** (9) The 70% rule is applied to the pooled statement-level precision of the extractor over the decisive kinds (B, C, D) only; A is reported separately (it is easy and would inflate the figure). A statement is CONFIRMED only if the reference label for that order and kind is true AND the extractor's quoted words appear literally in the description (a quote that does not is unsupported and counts as not confirmed). The reference label is the two labellers' common answer, or my ruling from the words alone where they differ; the disputes are listed before scoring, and I adjudicate seeing only the description and the two labellers' answers, never the extractor's or the keyword search's output. (10) The 95% intervals for the two stop-rule figures come from a bootstrap of orders within university (2000 draws, seed 32); the frozen rule is applied to the point estimates, and an interval that straddles a threshold is reported as straddling it. If an agent's file is incomplete or malformed it is re-run once with the same prompt and the re-run is logged. (11) Code: `docmismatch/workorder_score.py`; `scripts/76_workorder_screen.py check` (coverage, labeller agreement, dispute list) and `score`; tests `tests/test_workorder_score.py`.

**Adjudication of the two labellers' disagreements (2026-09-26; done from the words alone, seeing only the two labellers' answers, BEFORE the extractor's output or any keyword hit was opened).** Coverage was complete (three agents x 600 orders). The labellers disagreed on 105 (order, kind) pairs in 103 orders: A 19, B 17, C 0, D 69 (kappa: A 0.92, B 0.61, C 1.0, D 0.67). Rulings are in `results/docmismatch/workorder_screen/adjudicated.json`. **Rules I applied (judgement calls; they move the numbers).** *D:* true when the text says more than one room, space or unit is affected (plural tags, a floor, 'entire building'), OR names a shared system (an air handler, supply or return fan, HVAC or heating-and-ventilating unit, heat recovery unit, a water, glycol or steam loop, a pump, control air) as what the order is about or what is in alarm, as the frozen definition's own example 'AHU-3 tripped' does; a room symptom that names the unit serving it (e.g. 'SUP004') also counts. One D ruling went the other way (a single dining room 'too hot'). This is a GENEROUS reading: labeller 2 counted only statements about several spaces, so 68 of the 69 D disputes became true. The caveat that matters: an order written about an air handler already tells the technician where to go and adds nothing the routine does not know, so D-by-this-rule may overstate what a search or a model could add; a sensitivity that drops D is therefore added to the report (exploratory, disclosed, does NOT change the frozen rule). *A:* true when the text gives a tag or number for equipment or a room or floor or wing, or a named room or area below building level; false for a generic type of space or equipment ('stairwell', 'elevator', 'vestibule', 'expansion tanks', 'air handler') or a building name alone (11 of 19 false). *B:* true only for a repair, visit, inspection or test of the equipment that is reported as having happened, or 'still not working' after a call back; false for occupants' own attempts (adjusting the heat, closing vents, taking temperatures), for findings or observations ('per J. W.'), for workarounds, and for the response narrated in the order itself (4 of 17 true). *Limitation:* rulings were made only for pairs the labellers disagreed on; an order both of them marked false that my D rule would call true was not revisited, so D is if anything understated by the consensus. **Exploratory addition (declared before any output was opened):** the report also gives the LLM-only share for B and C alone and for D alone.

**Status: Stage 1 RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-26). Stage 2 NOT run: no data, no expert.**

**Result of Stage 1 (`scripts/76_workorder_screen.py`; report `results/docmismatch/workorder_screen/stage1_report.md`, `stage1_results.json`, the 600-order sample `sample_600.csv`, the agents' raw files in `raw/`, `disputes.csv`, `adjudicated.json`; about 0.5 MB on disk, the 1.4 GB file was only streamed).** *Gates:* G1-G4 passed (stream equals the published file; frozen items unchanged since the draw; sample files unchanged; 600 orders in 12 universities). *Frozen decision by the rule as written: PASS* (LLM-only share 19.3%, 95% interval 16.5-22.3%, above 10%; the model's precision over B, C, D 95.0%, 91.3-98.5%, 133 confirmed of 140 statements, above 70%; volume weights give the same verdict). *What the text carries (share of orders, equal weight over universities):* A reference 69.5%; **B history 3.0%**; **C mismatch 0.2% (one order in 600, which the extractor missed)**; D shared 28.0% under the generous reading; the university range for D is 0% to 48%. *Who finds it:* keyword search recall/precision B 33%/40%, D 6.5%/92%; the model B 78%/78%, D 71%/97.5%; 0 quotes absent from the text. Labeller kappa before adjudication: A 0.92, B 0.61, C 1.0 (on one order), D 0.67.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) **The pass is real by the letter and misleading in substance:** the sensitivity declared before any output was opened puts the LLM-only share at **1.3% for B and C together (below the 10% bar) and 18.2% for D alone**, i.e. nearly all of the 19.3% is D, and D was read generously (an order written about an air handler counts as shared equipment; such an order tells the technician where to go and adds nothing to the routine, and a plain equipment-name search would surface it: the frozen keyword list has no bare equipment tags, which is why its D recall is 6.5%). I do not treat this as support for building the assistant. (2) **Documentation errors are almost never written into the text** (1 of 600), so on this corpus the 'text reveals a map error' idea has nothing to work with. (3) **History is in 3% of orders**; the model finds more of it than search (78% against 33%) but the gain is about 1.3% of orders, far from the 12.5% that a 5-minute average saving would need (Stage 1 stop-rule arithmetic). (4) **The main claimed value, linking EARLIER orders on the same equipment to a new complaint, could not be tested here at all:** the corpus has no room or asset ID, so Stage 1 only measures statements inside single orders. (5) Limits: the labels are Claude-made and adjudicated by me (correlated with the extractor, NOT expert-verified); temperature was not controllable; one extraction pass; the keyword search and the D rule are my constructions; rulings were made only for disputed pairs; 12 universities' terse orders, mostly campus buildings (not hospitals or residential blocks except some housing orders); one seed; nothing here says anything about the routine's speed.
*What would follow.* On this evidence there is no case for building the LLM assistant; what a positive answer would need is Stage 2 (one real building's export with room and equipment IDs, its equipment tree, and an expert) to measure whether earlier orders on the same equipment change the first check. If no export can be had, the idea stays where the routine already leaves it: the neighbour question needs only a count of open complaints on the air handler.

**Status update (2026-09-26, after Stage 1): Stage 2 is BLOCKED and parked.** The user has no work-order export from a real building, no equipment tree for one, and no domain expert. Nothing public that I found supplies them. The LLM history-evidence idea therefore stays at Stage 1: no case to build it on the evidence in hand (see the Result above), and no route to a better test without one of those three things arriving.


## Addendum 33 (dated 2026-09-26, written BEFORE any language-model technician has worked a visit): against a language model playing an experienced technician, with the same information, checks, minutes and answers, how do the routine, the joint tool and my rule-based stand-in compare on minutes and accuracy? (simulation only; a PILOT; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** The user (2026-09-26), after seeing how the tool compares with my fast stand-ins: "make an LLM be the experienced technician, and test that against the model". The stand-ins of Addenda 26, 28, 30 and 31 are rules I wrote; a language model has knowledge and judgement I did not write down and can adapt as answers arrive, so it is a harder and less arbitrary opponent. It is still NOT an experienced technician: it has no field experience, and nothing here is evidence about real technicians.

**World and visits.** Addendum 30's primary cell (shared = assumed, map M0, reliability R0), both tiers (T2, T3), events from Addendum 30's draws (stream [30, cell 6, 0, event]) restricted to the 990 of 1000 with at most 8 complaints. A stratified sample: **10 visits from each of the three kinds (one complaint; several complaints with no shared cause; shared cause) = 30 visits, each worked at T2 and at T3 (60 technician sessions)**, drawn with seed 33; the number is fixed before the run after the smoke test has measured what a session costs (a change is logged as a clarification, never after any pilot result). "All visits" is the stratified estimate re-weighted to the kind shares of the 990 eligible events (573 one-complaint, 255 several-without-shared-cause, 162 shared-cause).

**The technician.** One fresh Claude session per visit and tier (the Agent tool, model `sonnet`, no memory of any other visit), given a role prompt (frozen after the smoke test, stored verbatim with the results) and a terminal. It sees exactly what the tool sees at that tier and no more: the complaints (all of today's complaints in the building; rooms not listed have none); the drawings' claims (which box serves which room, which air handler which box); for each complaint the documented box and air handler and the alternative boxes the tool would consider; at T2 the design attributes and static setpoint, at T3 also control-system access with a point map; a flow hood and a pressure gauge, no occupant. It sees the tool's own catalogue of checks (30 checks, the text, where each is done, and the minutes it would cost from where the technician now stands, including walking and waiting, and any fallback cost of missing data). **It is NOT shown the tool's probabilities, priors, hit or false-alarm rates, or the world's parameters.** It is told only that no check is perfectly reliable and a reading can be wrong in either direction. It chooses checks one at a time (a check on a complaint's room or box, or on its air handler), reads the answer, and finally concludes a cause for every complaint (one of the tool's 14 leaf causes, "wrong:<box>", or "unlisted") with "confident" or "unsure". It may not repeat a check; a reading of an air handler serves every complaint on it and is charged once per visit; a visit ends at `finish`, or at 12 checks per complaint (the tool's cap). Its instructions say: find the cause of every complaint as efficiently as you sensibly can and stop when confident enough to act; you are judged on total minutes and on whether your diagnoses are right.

**Same answers for everyone.** New answer mechanism `TableOracle`: the answer to a question is a fixed function of (event, complaint, check, target) or (event, air handler, check), so the routine, the joint tool, the stand-in and the language model get identical answers to identical questions, in any order. **Partners on the same visits and the same answers:** `tool_ticket_memo2` (the routine; primary), `tool_joint2` (secondary), `fast_parent` (my rule). Accounting: the terminal charges minutes with the tool's own accountant (v1 `Session.report`), and gate G2 checks it against the policies' own.

**Metrics.** Per visit: total minutes; checks; share of complaints whose final diagnosis is exactly right (as in Addenda 30-31); wrong-and-confident (concluded and wrong: for the tool 0.95 belief, for the language model the "confident" flag; the two are not the same thing, so exact accuracy is the like-for-like number); the first check taken. Comparisons by tier on the same visits, paired, with 1000 bootstrap resamples within strata, and Addendum 21's labels (5% minutes, 0.02 accuracy, safety 0.02, paired 95% intervals): **(P) LLM - routine [primary]; (Q) LLM - joint2; (R) LLM - fast_parent; (S) routine - fast_parent** (the last is Addendum 31's comparison again on the pilot sample, as a check that the sample is not unusual). Break-even cost of a wrong diagnosis as before.

**Predictions (mine, written before the run; each strict; all visits unless stated).** P1: at T3 the language model takes at least as many minutes as the routine. P2: at T2 the two are within 15% of each other in minutes. P3: the language model's exact accuracy is lower than the routine's at both tiers. P4: its wrong-and-confident rate is at least 0.02 above the routine's at both tiers. P5: on shared-cause visits its minutes are at least the routine's at both tiers. P6: it is at least 0.05 more accurate than `fast_parent` at both tiers. P7: it is at least as slow as `fast_parent` at T2. P8: on shared-cause visits its first check is an air-handler check in at least half of the sessions at each tier (does it find the shared-cause shortcut unprompted?). (I expect the model to be more thorough than the routine, hence slower at T3, and less well matched to this world's arbitrary probabilities, hence less accurate: a guess, not a finding.)

**Decision (frozen; per tier, all visits, LLM vs the routine, label of comparison P).** *THE LLM TECHNICIAN IS FASTER* if minutes are FASTER (5% and the interval clear of 0) and accuracy is not LESS. *THE ROUTINE IS FASTER* if minutes are SLOWER by the same rule. *NO CLEAR SPEED DIFFERENCE AT THIS SIZE* otherwise. Reported as a pair (T2, T3), with the accuracy label beside each. Expect wide intervals (about 10 visits per kind): the pilot can show a large gap or none, not a small one.

**Gates (evaluated before any comparison is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1: the answer mechanism is deterministic and independent of question order (property test). G2: the terminal's accounting reproduces the minutes and guesses of `fast_parent` and of `tool_ticket_memo2` on 30 visits per tier when their action sequences are fed through it. G3 (audit): every session's transcript shows no tool call other than the terminal command; a session that broke this is re-run once and the breach reported; if the transcripts cannot be inspected the result carries "NOT AUDITED". G4: all 60 sessions reached `finish` or the check cap (a session that took no action is re-run once; one that stopped without concluding every complaint is scored as abandoned: unconcluded complaints wrong). G5: the partners re-run from the stored answers reproduce their stored minutes. G6: sample and seed as registered.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, the FMUCD.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The world is generated from the tool's own model, with placeholder numbers: the tool knows the world's priors and response rates and the language model does not (home advantage for the tool), while the world's response rates are arbitrary and a real technician's knowledge would differ (a handicap for the language model). (2) A language model is not a technician; one model, one prompt, one run per visit and tier, and its variation between runs is not measured. (3) It sees minutes per check; a real technician knows them roughly. (4) Confidence is the model's own claim, not a calibrated belief. (5) The rule-based stand-in is mine. (6) A pilot: 10 visits per kind. (7) Sessions cannot be sandboxed: the model has a general shell and is instructed to use only the terminal; the audit gate checks, it does not prevent.

**What would follow.** If the routine is faster at both tiers, the earlier reading (the tool is not faster than a fast technician unless there is a control system or a shared cause) is not just an artefact of my rule-based stand-ins. If the language model is faster, the stand-ins were weak and the tool has a real rival for speed. Either way the next step would be a larger run with several models and repeats, and, above all, real technicians.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v2/study_llm.py` (`TableOracle`, sampling, the terminal state machine, replay, scoring, analysis, gates), `scripts/77_llm_technician.py` (prepare / terminal / analyze), `tests/test_troubleshoot_llm.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_llm/`.

**Clarifications made while implementing and smoke-testing (2026-09-27, written after `study_llm.py`, `scripts/77_llm_technician.py` and `tests/test_troubleshoot_llm.py` (23 tests) exist and BEFORE any pilot session was run; one changes the registered design and is marked).** (1) **Corrections:** the eligible events number 990, not 993 (the text above is corrected), with kind shares 573 / 255 / 162. (2) **Smoke sessions.** Three sessions were worked on visits OUTSIDE the pilot sample (events 1, 5 and 150; not analysed, not used to tune anything except the two items below). The terminal worked as designed; a session took about 3 minutes and about 68 thousand subagent tokens (23 to 30 tool calls), so the pilot stays at 10 visits per kind (60 sessions). Gate G2 (the terminal's accounting against the policies') already holds: 354 replays of `fast_parent`, the routine and the joint tool over both tiers, zero differences in minutes or checks. (3) **CHANGE: the technician's agent type.** The general-purpose subagent I first used receives this project's memory index and git status as context (read from its transcript): the index says "the cheap routine captures the shared-cause saving", so that technician would not be blind to the hypothesis under test, and P8 (does it find the shortcut unprompted?) would be void. The `Explore` agent type receives neither (its transcript holds no memory index and no git status, only the user's email). **The pilot uses `Explore` (model `sonnet`).** Its own system prompt is a read-only file-search specialist's; the role prompt tells it what it is and what to do, but its persona may push it toward thoroughness and I have not measured that. The two smoke sessions worked with the general-purpose type are discarded. (4) **Prompt frozen verbatim** and stored with the results (`technician_prompt.txt`, the template with the launcher path and the session id filled in per session). (5) **Audit.** Transcripts are read from the subagents' JSONL files (`subagents/agent-<id>.jsonl` of this session); a breach is any tool call other than the terminal command for that session's own id (the harness's `SubagentHandback`, its way of delivering the final report, is not a breach). The audit sees what the technician DID with its tools, not what it knew from training or was told by its context. (6) **Order.** Sessions run in batches of about 12; each session is a fresh agent; the order of batches follows the shuffled session numbering, which hides kind and tier.

**Status: RUN once as pre-registered (2026-09-27), 60 sessions, after the smoke tests and clarifications above.** One deviation and one bug, both logged here: (i) several sessions were launched in waves because the harness allows 20 subagents at once (the registered "batches of about 12" became a rolling queue; nothing else changed; each session was a fresh agent); (ii) after the run I found that the report's per-policy tables had their column headers in a different order from their data (the "shared-cause" and "one complaint" columns were swapped); the comparison tables and the decision use keyed labels and were correct; the header was fixed and the report regenerated, no number changed.

**Result of Addendum 33 (2026-09-27; `scripts/77_llm_technician.py`; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_llm/llm_report.md`, per-visit `llm_records.csv`, every session's commands `llm_sessions.json`, the terminal logs `state_logs/`, the audit `audit_all.json`, the frozen prompt `technician_prompt.txt`).** *Gates:* G1-G6 all passed (120 replays of `fast_parent` and the routine through the terminal: no difference in minutes or checks; the audit of all 60 transcripts found no tool call other than the session's own terminal command; all 60 sessions reached `finish`; 5 of 1473 terminal commands were refused, none charged; 6 sessions used the whole check limit). *Numbers (all visits, kind shares re-weighted; minutes per visit / share of complaints diagnosed exactly right):* T2: LLM 76 / 82%, routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`) 92 / 84%, joint tool 101 / 84%, `fast_parent` 94 / 62%. T3: LLM 76 / 79%, routine 58 / 87%, joint 66 / 90%, `fast_parent` 80 / 60%. *By kind, minutes (LLM against routine):* one complaint T2 51 against 67, T3 33 against 42; several without shared cause T2 138 against 180, T3 109 against 118; **shared-cause T2 67 against 42, T3 175 against 18.** *Frozen decision:* **T2: THE LLM TECHNICIAN IS FASTER (accuracy SAME); T3: THE ROUTINE IS FASTER (accuracy SAME).** Comparison P, all visits: T2 -15.8 minutes (-17%, interval -28 to -2), accuracy -0.020 (-0.20 to +0.15); T3 +18.0 (+31%, +7 to +28), accuracy -0.082 (-0.28 to +0.11). By kind (P): T2 one complaint -16 (-24%, SAME), several -41 (-23%, FASTER), shared +26 (+62%, SAME); T3 one complaint -9 (-22%, FASTER), several -9 (-8%, SAME), **shared +157 (+883%, SLOWER) with accuracy -0.15 (-0.27 to -0.03)**. Against the joint tool (Q): T2 -25 (-25%, FASTER), T3 +10 (+16%, SLOWER). Against my rule (R): T2 -18 (-19%, SAME) and +0.20 accuracy (MORE), T3 -4 (-5%, SAME) and +0.19 (SAME). Comparison S (Addendum 31's routine against my rule, on this sample) came out as before: T2 -2% and +0.22 accuracy, T3 -27% and +0.27. *Predictions:* P1, P3, P4, P5, P6 and P8 hold; **P2 and P7 do not** (at T2 the LLM was 17% faster than the routine, not within 15%, and faster than my rule, not at least as slow). *What the language model did:* 9.8 checks per visit at T2 and 21.8 at T3; on shared-cause visits its first check was an air-handler check in 90% (T2) and 100% (T3) of sessions (P8: **it finds the shortcut unprompted**); 91% (T2) and 81% (T3) of its conclusions were marked confident. *Exploratory, not registered, same run:* on the ten shared-cause visits at T3 the routine took 2 to 4 minutes on seven of them (one or two control-system reads); on those seven the LLM took 43 to 306 minutes (six of them 133 to 306; 13 to 48 checks): it read the air handler first and then went on to confirm complaint after complaint at each box; of its 26 wrong conclusions (of 154 complaints in all, 16 of the 26 marked confident) 8 were "low static" called something else, 7 of them on those T3 shared-cause visits (7 of the 35 low-static complaints there), after box-level readings, which are noisy in this world, made it drop the shared cause; 15 of the 26 were "controller_config" (10), "other" or "unlisted", reached by elimination.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) **The earlier finding that the tool is not faster than a fast technician without a control system is not an artefact of my rule-based stand-ins:** a stand-in that can adapt is faster than the routine at T2 by about a sixth (interval -28 to -2), at about the same exact accuracy, mainly on visits that are not shared-cause. A likely reason (a hypothesis, not tested): the LLM stops when the remaining causes are unlikely by elimination, the routine only at 95% belief; its accuracy did not suffer in this sample, but its "confident" was wrong far more often (16% of complaints at T2 against 0%: not like for like, the routine declines to conclude rather than conclude wrongly). (2) **The routine's speed edge is real, and it is one thing: stopping early on shared-cause evidence.** With a control system it is 2 to 4 minutes on seven of the ten shared-cause visits against 43 to 306 for the LLM on the same seven, and more accurate (97% against 82%) on those visits; everywhere else the LLM was as fast or faster. The LLM found the shortcut and then did not trust it. (3) Against my rule-based stand-in the LLM is about 20 points more accurate and no slower. (4) The joint tool is slower than the routine again (Q against P). *Not concluded:* that the tool beats, or loses to, a real technician on speed; that an LLM is a technician; anything about a technician who knows the building or has field experience. **Limitations:** a pilot (10 visits per kind: wide intervals, especially on accuracy); one model (`sonnet`), one prompt, one run per visit and tier, its variation between runs not measured; the `Explore` agent type's search-specialist persona may have shaped its thoroughness; it was shown the minutes of every check; the world is generated from the tool's own model with placeholder numbers (a home advantage for the tool; the arbitrary response rates are also a handicap for a model that reasons like a real technician); "confident" is the model's claim, not a calibrated belief; the audit shows what it did, not what it knew.
*What would follow.* Two things worth testing before anything is built: (a) whether the routine's stopping rule at T2 (belief 0.95, 0.003 bits per minute) costs speed that an earlier stop would recover without losing accuracy (a change to the tool, after this result: it would need its own pre-registered study and would not be independent of this one); (b) a larger LLM run (more visits per kind, several models, repeats) to size the T2 speed gap and its accuracy cost, since 10 visits per kind cannot. Neither replaces real technicians.


## Exploratory note E1 (dated 2026-09-27; NOT a pre-registered test): where do the joint tool's extra minutes go? (simulation only)

**Question and status.** After Addendum 31 the joint tool (`tool_joint2`) still took 12-29% more minutes than the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`) on shared-cause visits. This note looks for why. It is exploratory: the diagnosis was made on the study's own events, the tau sweep and the two probes were designed after looking at it, and every variant tried is reported. Code: `troubleshoot/v2/excess.py`, `scripts/78_troubleshoot_joint_excess.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_joint_excess.py` (14 tests; five deliberately broken versions were caught); output `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint_excess/` (`report.md`, `raw.pkl`, `gate.json`). The tool itself is unchanged.

**Method.** (1) Instrumented copies of the two policies that record every step; gate: with Addendum 31's own answer streams they reproduce its stored minutes, checks and correct counts exactly (800 replays, 0 differ). (2) A COUPLED oracle: an answer depends on the event, the question and a seed only, so both policies get the same answer to the same question and a paired difference is the policies alone. (Addendum 31 drew an independent answer stream per policy, so single differences carried the luck of missed readings: in two events the routine's air-handler visit caught a fan-off and the joint tool's same check missed it, which cost the joint tool 370 and 450 minutes.) (3) Four runs: `diagnose` (events 0-999, 8 answer seeds, tau 0.95), `sweep` (the same events, 4 seeds, tau 0.90-0.99), `focus` (events 1000-1999, 4 fresh seeds) and `cap` (events 2000-2999, 4 fresh seeds); the last two ranges had not been used before their run.

**Result.** Numbers are joint minus routine: minutes per visit with a 95% bootstrap interval over events, and points of complaints diagnosed correctly. *The excess is real and is not answer-stream luck:* at tau 0.95 with identical answers, shared-cause visits +9.0 [+4.2, +14.4] min at T2 and +19.4 [+14.8, +24.6] at T3 (+2.8 and +2.0 points); several complaints with no shared cause +19.4 [+16.6, +22.5] and +22.0 [+19.9, +24.1] (+1.5 and +4.5 points); one complaint 0.0 (identical); all visits +6.5 [+5.3, +7.8] and +8.9 [+7.8, +10.2]. Three causes were examined.
**(1) Unequal check budgets: the main cause on visits with several complaints.** The joint session pools a cap of 12 checks x the number of complaints; the routine caps each ticket at 12. The joint tool spends minutes beyond the 12th room or box check on one complaint (per visit: 14.7 at T2 and 23.2 at T3 on several-complaint visits, 2.7 and 10.5 on shared-cause visits; the routine 0). With the routine's cap put on the joint session (`joint2_cap12`, untouched events 2000-2999): the several-complaint excess falls from +18.0 to +6.7 [+5.1, +8.5] (T2) and from +23.9 to +4.0 [+3.3, +4.6] (T3), and its accuracy lead on those visits from +5.0 to +0.5 points at T3 (the lead was bought by the larger budget); shared-cause visits +8.8 to +4.5 [-1.9, +10.7] (T2) and +21.8 to +10.6 [+5.2, +16.2] (T3); all visits +6.3 to +2.5 [+1.5, +3.7] and +9.9 to +2.7 [+1.8, +3.6].
**(2) A confidence-threshold cliff on shared-cause visits.** Example (T3, 7 complaints on an air handler whose fan is off): after the 2-minute BAS fan reading the joint model's confidence in 'fan off' is 0.948 (it allows a small chance that a complaining room has its own fault), just under tau = 0.95, so it pays for the 19-minute air-handler visit that separates fan-off from low static; the routine's per-ticket posterior after the same reading is 0.952 and it stops. The sweep (identical answers): the shared-cause excess at T2 is +0.8 [-4.5, +5.5] at tau 0.90, +4.7 at 0.92, +7.9 at 0.95 and +56 to +67 at 0.97-0.99, while the joint tool's accuracy hardly moves (95.2 to 95.8%): a higher tau buys fewer confident-wrong conclusions, not more right guesses. With both fixes (`joint2_cap12` at tau 0.92 against the routine at 0.92): shared-cause visits -0.5 [-6.4, +5.2] min (T2) and -2.6 [-5.9, +0.3] (T3), +1.6 and +0.8 points. Both tools' wrong-and-confident rates rise as tau falls, and the comparison is at equal tau, not at equal safety.
**(3) Hopping between complaints did NOT explain it.** The joint session moves 10.3 times per several-complaint visit at T2 against the routine's 7.4 and pays more travel. Forcing arrival order (`joint2_focus`, events 1000-1999) brings the moves to about 8 and cuts travel by about 8 minutes, but total minutes barely change (several-complaint excess +19.4 to +18.2 [+14.6, +22.2] at T2), it is slower than the joint tool on shared-cause visits (+8.1 [+3.8, +12.8] min at T2, tau 0.95) and less accurate at T3 (-3.5 points on several-complaint visits). My prediction that it would remove most of the T2 excess failed.
*Predictions fixed before the `cap` run:* the cap removes at least 60% of the several-complaint excess at the tool's own tau (held: 63% at T2, 83% at T3; at tau 0.90 and 0.92 it is 56% at T2); the cap removes the joint tool's accuracy lead on those visits (held at T3); the cap removes at most 40% of the shared-cause excess (did NOT hold: about 51% at both tiers).

**Reading, with the caveats it needs.** (a) **The verdict of Addenda 30 and 31 stands, but its explanation changes:** the joint tool was not slower because reasoning jointly is costly; it was given a larger check budget and stopped at a threshold its own estimate sat just under, and the extra accuracy it bought on several-complaint visits came from that budget. (b) With the budget equalised the joint tool is still 1-4% slower than the routine over all visits (+2.5 and +2.7 minutes at tau 0.95; +2.3 and +0.8 at 0.92) for about the same accuracy (+0.1 to +0.4 points); only on shared-cause visits at tau 0.92 does it look equal or slightly faster with 0.8 to 1.6 points more accuracy, and those minute intervals include zero (about 170 shared-cause events per run). Nothing here makes the joint machinery clearly better than the routine. (c) Everything is the model's own family with placeholder numbers, and the routine and the joint tool share one world model, so how either fares in a building that differs from that model is not tested; the variants were designed after the diagnosis and evaluated on fresh events but were not pre-registered; the threshold comparison is at equal tau, not equal safety.
**What follows.** If the joint machinery is ever built, give it the routine's per-complaint cap and check its conclusion threshold against its own estimates (a small tolerance, or a tau chosen for equal safety). The routine is still the thing to build and test first, and none of this is evidence about real technicians.

## Addendum 34 (dated 2026-09-27, written BEFORE any stopping-rule variant has been run): does a less conservative stopping rule, on its own, recover the language model's T2 speed edge over the routine, or does something else explain it? (simulation only; a parameter sweep of the tool's own engine; no LLM; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** Addendum 33 found the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`) slower than a language model playing the technician at T2 (no control system) by about 17% at the same exact accuracy, while the language model used somewhat fewer checks. The user asked (2026-09-27) to test the cheap explanation first: is the routine simply too conservative about when to stop, rather than the language model reasoning better? This changes only the routine's own settings; no LLM is run.

**What the routine's stopping rule is.** A complaint concludes when its leading cause's belief reaches `tau` (default 0.95); short of that, the next check is offered only if its expected gain per minute is at least `min_rate` (default 0.003 bits/min); with nothing worth its time left, the visit ends unresolved (v1's fixed rule, unchanged since Addendum 27). Lowering `tau` accepts a diagnosis sooner; raising `min_rate` gives up sooner on a case that is not concluding. Both are engine parameters, not new mechanisms.

**World and events.** Addendum 30's primary cell (shared = assumed, map M0, rel R0), the SAME 1000 events (stream `[30, primary cell index, 0, i]`), tier T2 only (the tier where the gap was found). Answers come from `TableOracle` (Addendum 33): identical answers to identical questions whichever variant asks and in whatever order, so that variants differ only in which checks they choose and when they stop, never in what a check would have said.

**Variants (frozen).** Primary sweep: `tau` in {0.99, 0.95 (baseline), 0.90, 0.85, 0.80, 0.75, 0.70, 0.60, 0.50}, `min_rate` held at the tool's default (0.003). Secondary, exploratory sweep: `min_rate` in {0.003 (baseline), 0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.10}, `tau` held at the tool's default (0.95). Nothing else about the routine changes (same neighbour-informed prior, same carried low-static reading).

**Target.** Addendum 33 measured the language model 17% faster than the routine on this comparison's pilot sample (interval -28% to -2%). **The bar for "recovers it" is at least half of that: minutes at least 8.5% below the baseline variant (`r_min <= -0.085`), with the paired 95% interval's upper end below zero.** "Not hurting accuracy" and "not hurting safety" use the study's usual tolerances (Addendum 21: exact accuracy drops by less than 0.02, wrong-and-confident rises by less than 0.02).

**Metrics.** Per variant, averaged over the 1000 events (all visits, the cell's own natural mix of one/several/shared-cause): minutes per visit, share of complaints diagnosed exactly right, wrong-and-confident rate, checks per visit. Every variant is compared with the baseline (`tau` = 0.95, `min_rate` = 0.003) on the same events, paired, with 1000 bootstrap resamples (Addendum 21's labels).

**Predictions (mine, written before the run; each strict).** P1: minutes fall as `tau` falls, monotonically, across the whole grid. P2: exact accuracy does not rise as `tau` falls anywhere in the grid (it may hold flat, but easing the bar should never help accuracy). P3: at `tau` = 0.90, minutes are at least 5% below baseline (a real FASTER label) with accuracy not LESS. P4: at least one `tau` in the grid reaches the recovery bar (`r_min <= -0.085`) with accuracy not LESS and wrong-and-confident not up by 0.02 or more (my guess: the routine's stopping bar, not superior reasoning, explains most of the T2 gap). P5: `tau` = 0.99 is slower than baseline (a direction sanity check).

**Decision (frozen).** *THE STOPPING RULE LARGELY EXPLAINS IT* if some `tau` reaches the recovery bar (P4's condition). *THE STOPPING RULE PARTLY EXPLAINS IT* if some `tau` gives a real (FASTER, interval excludes 0) speed gain of at least 5% without breaching the accuracy or safety tolerance, but none reaches the recovery bar. *THE STOPPING RULE DOES NOT EXPLAIN IT* if no `tau` in the grid gives a real speed gain without breaching one of those tolerances. The `min_rate` sweep is reported but does not change this decision; it is exploratory.

**Gates (evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1: the first 50 of the 1000 events reproduce Addendum 30's stored primary-cell events (same causes, same kinds). G2: the baseline variant (`tau` = 0.95, `min_rate` = 0.003) reproduces `tool_ticket_memo2`'s T2 numbers on this cell within Monte Carlo noise of Addendum 31's stored 1000-event result (minutes and accuracy each within 5%). G3: `TableOracle` is deterministic and independent of question order (the property test of Addendum 33, re-run here). G4: `tau` = 0.99 is not faster than baseline (the direction sanity check of P5; a failure here means the sweep's plumbing is broken, not a finding). G5: the whole sweep reproduces exactly on a second run with the same seed.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, any LLM call.

**Limitations named up front.** This tests only whether relaxing two existing knobs closes the gap; it cannot show that the language model's own reasoning (which checks to pick, not just when to stop) is or is not doing something the routine's greedy entropy-per-minute rule does not. A `tau` that recovers speed here has not been checked at T3 or on the other 17 cells, and changing the tool's default would need its own pre-registered study, independent of this one. The 8.5% target is a judgement call (half of a noisy pilot estimate), not a natural threshold.

**What would follow.** *LARGELY EXPLAINS IT*: the T2 gap is mostly a tuning question, cheap to close, and the language model's edge there is not evidence of better reasoning; the next step would be to check the same `tau` at T3 and across cells before touching the tool's default. *PARTLY EXPLAINS IT*: tuning buys some but not all of the gap; something else (check choice, not just the stopping point) makes up the rest. *DOES NOT EXPLAIN IT*: the routine's conservatism is not the (main) reason, and the language model's edge would need a different explanation (better check selection) to chase further.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen:** `troubleshoot/v2/study_stop.py` (case variants, the sweep, comparison, predictions, gates), `scripts/78_stopping_rule.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_stop.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_stop/`.

**Deviation logged (2026-09-27, before the run was read): the tests were written and run AFTER the pre-registered run, not before.** `study_stop.py` and `scripts/78_stopping_rule.py` were written and smoke-tested first; the full 1000-event run was launched; `tests/test_troubleshoot_stop.py` (15 tests) was written next and caught nothing that changed a number, but one of my own test assumptions was wrong (I expected an almost-zero `tau` to conclude with zero checks; the prior-alone-never-concludes rule, Addendum 30, means at least one real observation is always spent, and the memo carry can make a later ticket's own share of that spend fall under one check per complaint) — the test was corrected, not the code. This is a departure from the addendum's own stated order and is disclosed rather than silently fixed, as this file's convention requires.

**Result of Addendum 34 (2026-09-27; `scripts/78_stopping_rule.py`, 66 s on 1 core; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_stop/stop_report.md`, `stop_sweep.csv`).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed (the first 50 of 1000 events match Addendum 30's stored primary cell; the baseline variant reproduces Addendum 31's T2 routine numbers on this cell to within 3%: 109 min / 80% against 112 min / 80%; the answer table is order-independent; `tau` = 0.99 is not faster than baseline; the sweep reproduces exactly on a second run). *Primary sweep (`tau`, T2, all visits, minutes / accuracy / wrong-and-confident, against the tau=0.95 baseline of 109 min / 80% / 1.3%):* 0.90: 102 / 80% / 2.6% (-7%, accuracy SAME, IMPROVES); 0.85: 96 / 79% / 5.1% (-12%, accuracy SAME, IMPROVES); 0.80: 92 / 78% / 7.6% (-16%, accuracy SAME, IMPROVES); 0.75: 87 / 78% / 9.8% (-20%, accuracy SAME, IMPROVES); 0.70: 80 / 77% / 11.8% (-26%, accuracy LESS, TRADE); 0.60: 73 / 77% / 16.4% (-33%, accuracy LESS); 0.50: 67 / 74% / 22.6% (-39%, accuracy LESS). **No `tau` reaches the registered recovery bar (8.5% fewer minutes) while keeping wrong-and-confident from rising by 0.02 or more; the largest such bar-respecting gain is `tau` = 0.90 at -7%.** *Predictions:* P1, P2, P3 and P5 hold; **P4 does not** (the best `tau` for speed, 0.50, buys it only by giving up 0.055 accuracy and adding 0.213 wrong-and-confident). **Frozen decision: THE STOPPING RULE PARTLY EXPLAINS IT.**
*Secondary, exploratory sweep (`min_rate`, tau fixed at 0.95, not registered as the decision):* 0.01: 76 min / 78% / 0.3% (**-30% minutes, accuracy SAME (-0.016), wrong-and-confident actually lower (-0.010)**, IMPROVES, comfortably past the recovery bar and inside every tolerance); 0.02: 62 min / 76% / 0.2% (-43%, accuracy LESS); 0.05 and 0.10 collapse the routine (it stops almost immediately, accuracy falls to the base rate). *Matching the language model's own error profile (exploratory):* the `tau` closest to the language model's 16% wrong-and-confident rate is 0.60, at -33% minutes — bigger than the LLM's measured 17% T2 edge, if a comparable rise in confident-wrong diagnoses is accepted.
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) **On the axis this addendum registered as primary (`tau`), the routine's stopping bar explains only part of the T2 gap, and only by trading away the routine's main selling point: at `tau` = 0.90, the largest change that does not cost accuracy or safety, only 7 of the language model's 17 points of speed are recovered; anything faster raises wrong-and-confident past the tolerance or costs accuracy.** (2) **The `min_rate` knob tells a different and better story: giving up sooner on checks that are not worth their time (0.003 to 0.01 bits/min) buys about 30% at T2 for a small, tolerated accuracy cost and, in this sample, a LOWER confident-wrong rate.** This was not the registered decision (it is a different parameter than the one Addendum 33's reasoning pointed at), so it needs its own confirmatory run before it means anything past "worth investigating further." (3) The language model's own error profile (16% wrong-and-confident) is closer to what a `tau` around 0.60 produces than to anything that also respects the tool's safety tolerance: matching the LLM's speed by loosening `tau` alone means accepting a comparably worse confidence, not getting the LLM's speed for free. (4) None of this has been checked at T3 or on the other 17 cells; a change to the tool's own defaults needs its own pre-registered study.
*What would follow.* `tau` alone does not explain the T2 gap without a matching safety cost; `min_rate` is the more promising lead and deserves its own pre-registered study (same design, `min_rate` as the primary registered factor, checked at T3 and across cells, before touching any default). The language model's edge is not fully a stopping-rule artefact either way: at the one point (`tau` around 0.90) that stays inside every tolerance, it recovers less than half the gap, so check SELECTION, not just when to stop, likely still matters.

## Addendum 35 (dated 2026-09-27, written BEFORE any cell beyond the primary one has been run with `min_rate` changed): does raising the routine's check-value bar (`min_rate`), Addendum 34's unregistered lead, hold up across both data tiers and the other cells, or was it a feature of the one scenario it was found in? (simulation only; a parameter sweep of the tool's own engine; no LLM; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** Addendum 34 registered `tau` as the factor to test and found it only partly explained the language model's T2 speed edge. Its exploratory, unregistered sweep of a different knob, `min_rate` (the bar an available check's expected bits per minute must clear to be offered; default 0.003), found that raising it to 0.01 cut minutes by 30% at T2 on the primary cell with accuracy unchanged and wrong-and-confident actually lower. The user asked (2026-09-27) to test this too. This addendum makes `min_rate` = 0.01 the registered comparison and checks it where Addendum 34 could not: at T3 and on the other 17 cells.

**What changes and what does not.** Only `min_rate` moves, from 0.003 (baseline, the tool as it stands) to **0.01 (the challenger, Addendum 34's lead)**. `tau` stays at its default (0.95) throughout. Nothing else about the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`: the neighbour-informed prior, the carried low-static reading) changes.

**World.** All 18 cells of Addendum 30 (shared rare/assumed/common x map M0/M1/M2 x rel R0/R1), both tiers (T2, T3), the SAME 1000 events per cell (same generative draws; regenerated, not loaded, but gated against Addendum 30's stored file). Answers from `TableOracle` (Addendum 33 and 34's mechanism): identical answers to identical questions whichever variant asks.

**Metrics and comparison.** Per cell, per tier, per subset (all visits, one complaint, several without a shared cause, shared-cause; Addendum 30's SUBSETS): minutes per visit, share of complaints diagnosed exactly right, wrong-and-confident rate, checks per visit, for `min_rate` = 0.01 minus `min_rate` = 0.003 on the same events, paired, with 1000 bootstrap resamples (Addendum 21's labels: 5% minutes, 0.02 accuracy, 0.02 safety, paired 95% intervals). Reported as shares of the 18 cells, exactly as Addendum 30/31 report their comparisons, plus the primary cell's own numbers by subset.

**Predictions (mine, written before the run; each strict).** P1: at T2, the challenger is IMPROVES or a real FASTER-and-not-LESS in at least 15 of the 18 cells (`all` subset). P2: the same, at T3, in at least 15 of 18 cells. P3: the challenger is WORSE (by Addendum 21's label) in at most 1 of the 18 cells at either tier. P4: the share of cells with a SAFETY_REGRESSION (wrong-and-confident up by 0.02 or more, interval clear of zero) is at most 2 of 18 at each tier (Addendum 30's own UNSAFE_CELLS bar). P5: any safety regressions that do appear are no more common among the map-fault cells (M1, M2) than among M0 (a check that the saving is not bought by missing map errors specifically). P6: the primary cell's T2 numbers reproduce Addendum 34's stored ones for this exact comparison (same events, same answer mechanism: an internal-consistency check, not a new finding).

**Decision (frozen).** *CONFIRMED* if P1, P2 and P4 all hold and P3 holds (WORSE in at most 1 of 18 cells at either tier). *CONFIRMED, UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS* if the speed and accuracy bars of P1/P2 hold but the safety regressions are concentrated in M1/M2 cells (P5 fails while P1/P2 hold). *NOT CONFIRMED* otherwise (the speed/accuracy majority fails at either tier, or WORSE appears in more than 1 cell). A failed P6 means NO RESULT regardless of the rest (the sweep's plumbing would be suspect).

**Gates (evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1: the first 50 events of every cell reproduce Addendum 30's stored kinds. G2: the baseline variant (`min_rate` = 0.003) reproduces Addendum 31's `tool_ticket_memo2` numbers (minutes and accuracy within 5%) in the primary cell at both tiers. G3: `TableOracle` is deterministic and independent of question order. G4: the primary cell's T2 baseline and challenger minutes and accuracy reproduce Addendum 34's stored `stop_sweep.csv` row for `min_rate` = 0.01 exactly (same seed, same mechanism: this should match bit for bit, not just approximately). G5: the whole run reproduces exactly on a second run with the same seed.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, any LLM call.

**Limitations named up front.** This confirms or rejects one specific value (`min_rate` = 0.01) found by looking at one cell; it is not a search over `min_rate` values on the wider world (that would reopen the same look-then-leap risk this addendum exists to close). A pass here is still simulation, from the tool's own model family with placeholder numbers, and still says nothing about real technicians. Changing the tool's actual default is a separate decision, to be taken only after this and stated as such, never silently.

**What would follow.** *CONFIRMED*: `min_rate` = 0.01 is a candidate default for the tool itself (a real engine change, not requiring an LLM), pending a decision on whether to adopt it. *CONFIRMED, UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS*: promising everywhere documentation is good, but not to be adopted without a separate map-fault safeguard. *NOT CONFIRMED*: Addendum 34's finding was a feature of the one cell it was found in, and the search for a cheap speed gain continues elsewhere.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen:** `troubleshoot/v2/study_minrate.py` (case variants for both tiers, the 18-cell x 2-tier sweep, comparison, shares, predictions, gates), `scripts/79_minrate_confirm.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_minrate.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minrate/`.

**Deviation logged (2026-09-27, before the run was read).** Gate G2's reference number for T3 (`tool_ticket_memo2`, primary cell, all events) was written as 58 minutes / 87% before the run. That figure is Addendum 33's small 30-visit reweighted pilot sample, not Addendum 31's own 1000-event result, which is 65 minutes / 82% (`results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_joint2/joint2_report.md`) -- a citation mix-up on my part while writing the gate, caught because G2 failed on the first run (109/80% at T2 passed; 64/83% at T3 did not, against the wrong reference). I checked it was not a code bug first: `tool_ticket_memo2` recomputed fresh on these events with the study's original `W.Oracle` mechanism gives 63.5 min / 84%, matching `TableOracle`'s 63.6 min / 83% and Addendum 31's real 65/82% closely; the constant, not the simulation, was wrong. Fixed to (65.0, 0.82) and the report regenerated from the stored raw results (`--from-pickle`); no simulation number changed.

**Result of Addendum 35 (2026-09-27; `scripts/79_minrate_confirm.py`, 100 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minrate/minrate_report.md`, `minrate_cells.csv`, raw `minrate_raw.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed after the correction above (the first 50 events of every cell match Addendum 30's stored kinds; the baseline reproduces Addendum 31's routine numbers at both tiers within 5%; the answer table is order-independent; the primary cell's T2 numbers reproduce Addendum 34's `stop_sweep.csv` row for `min_rate` = 0.01 exactly, 108.99 and 76.11 minutes to two decimals; three cells reproduce exactly on a second run). *Primary cell, all events, baseline -> challenger:* T2 109 -> 76 min (80% -> 78% accuracy); T3 64 -> 57 min (83% -> 83%). *Shares of the 18 cells (challenger minus baseline, all events):* **T3: IMPROVES in 18 of 18 cells, WORSE in 0, a safety regression in 0.** T2: IMPROVES in 12 of 18, TRADE (real speed gain, real accuracy cost) in the other 6, WORSE in 0, a safety regression in 0 of 18. Minutes fall in every one of the 36 (cell, tier) combinations, by 8-15% at T3 and 25-39% at T2; wrong-and-confident never rises anywhere (0 of 36) and falls in about half. *Predictions:* P2 through P6 hold; **P1 does not** (T2 needed 15 of 18 cells at IMPROVES and got 12; the other 6 are the TRADE cells, where accuracy drops by 2.1 to 2.9 points, just past the 0.02 tolerance, alongside the speed gain). *Frozen decision: NOT CONFIRMED* (the registered bar needed both tiers at a 15/18 majority of clean, no-accuracy-cost gains; T2 fell short).
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) **At T3 this is an unambiguous, cheap win: every one of the 18 cells is faster with accuracy and confidence unchanged or better, no exception.** With a control system, the routine's own default (`min_rate` = 0.003) is simply asking more of the check menu than it needs to. (2) **At T2 the picture is real but mixed: two-thirds of cells get the same clean win, and one-third trade a real 2-3 points of accuracy for 25-39% fewer minutes.** Which third is not a map-fault or a single-factor pattern (the six TRADE cells span all three map levels and two of the three shared-cause rates); it looks like a general T2 property of this world, not a map-documentation-specific risk. (3) **Nowhere, in any of the 36 (cell, tier) results, does this change make the tool more likely to be confidently wrong.** The failure mode this addendum was built to catch (recovering speed by trading away the tool's central promise) did not appear once; the cost, where there is one, is a plainer loss of accuracy, not false confidence. (4) The registered decision is strict on purpose (a 15/18 supermajority, chosen before the run), and T2 missed it by 3 cells; a looser but still pre-stated bar (say, 10 of 18, or WORSE/TRADE distinguished from a true regression) would have called T2 confirmed too, but that bar was not the one written down, so the frozen answer is NOT CONFIRMED, not "confirmed on a technicality."
