*What would follow.* At T3, `min_rate` = 0.01 (or a value found by a proper search, which this addendum did not do) looks like a real, free improvement to the tool's own default and worth a decision on adopting it, independent of anything about language models. At T2, adopting it as a blanket default would mean accepting a real accuracy cost on about a third of the scenarios tested here; a technician-facing version might offer it as a faster mode rather than a silent default change, or accept it only where the tier already has a control system. Neither conclusion should be read past this addendum's own limits: one building shape, placeholder numbers, no real technicians.

**Note (2026-09-27, not a new addendum): the tuned routine against the language model, on Addendum 33's own visits.** The user asked to compare Addendum 35's confirmed setting (`min_rate` = 0.01) directly with the language model. This is not a fresh experiment carrying its own risk of a chosen-after-looking result: `min_rate` = 0.01 was fixed by Addenda 34-35 on evidence that never involved the language model, and the language model's numbers are Addendum 33's, unchanged; this only replays the routine with that one setting on the SAME 30 visits and puts the numbers side by side (`scripts/80_minrate_vs_llm.py`, `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minrate/vs_llm_report.md`). **At T2 the tuned routine and the language model come out statistically the same speed** (76 min against 76 min; the earlier 17% LLM edge over the untuned routine, 92 min, is gone), **while the tuned routine keeps the routine's accuracy (84% against 82%) and a wrong-and-confident rate near zero (0.0% against the language model's 16.1%).** **At T3 the tuned routine is faster, more accurate and safer than the language model on every count** (52 min against 76, 87% against 79%, 0.3% against 5.0%). By kind, this holds for single-complaint and unrelated-multi-complaint visits at both tiers (all NEITHER: no real difference); on shared-cause visits the language model is still slower than the routine by a wide margin at both tiers (as Addendum 33 found), because that gap comes from the routine's shared-cause shortcut, which `min_rate` does not touch. Read together with Addendum 35: the T2 gap this whole chain was chasing (Addendum 33 -> 34 -> 35) closes with one existing setting, no language model required; where the language model still loses badly (shared-cause visits) is a different mechanism, already explained.

**Note (2026-09-27, not a new addendum, primary cell only, exploratory): are there other cheap levers for a tiny accuracy cost and a big speed gain?** The user asked whether anything beats `min_rate` = 0.01 on that trade. Three things checked, none registered, all on the primary cell (1000 events):
(1) **A finer `min_rate` grid at T2** shows a smooth curve, not a hidden knee: 0.005 gives -16% minutes for -0.009 accuracy (and wrong-and-confident actually falls, -0.008); 0.0075 gives -25% for -0.011; 0.01 (the confirmed setting) gives -30% for -0.016; past that the cost accelerates (0.0125: -0.022, already past the tolerance used throughout this file). There is no better point sitting undiscovered near 0.01; 0.005-0.0075 are simply gentler versions of the same trade for anyone who wants an even smaller sacrifice.
(2) **The per-complaint check cap (12, `joint.MAX_CHECKS_PER_COMPLAINT`) is a bad lever, not a good one.** Lowering it to 10 already costs -0.011 to -0.042 accuracy (T2/T3) for only 7-19% fewer minutes -- a far worse ratio than `min_rate`, and it collapses fast from there (cap 6: -0.08 to -0.20 accuracy). The cap is blunt: it cuts checks on whichever visits need the most of them, which are exactly the visits with the least settled evidence, so it removes checks without regard to their value. `min_rate` cuts only the individually low-value checks, wherever they occur, which is why it costs so much less.
(3) **Combining a lower `tau` with `min_rate` = 0.01 does not help.** `tau` = 0.90 with `min_rate` = 0.01 gives the same minutes as `min_rate` = 0.01 alone (76.0 against 76.1) but a higher wrong-and-confident rate (0.023 against 0.003): `min_rate` = 0.01 already stops most visits before a lower `tau` would ever bind differently, so lowering `tau` on top adds the risk Addendum 34 found without buying any further speed. `min_rate` alone dominates every combination tried.
*Reading:* among the engine's existing parameters, `min_rate` is not one option among several with this trade; it is the only one found so far with it. A bigger speed gain than 0.01 already gives would need a real design change (a different selection rule), not a further parameter turn, and none is validated yet.

**Decision taken (2026-09-27, user: "apply it everywhere, both tiers"): the live v2 tool's default `min_rate` is changed from 0.003 to 0.01.** This is a real change to the shipped tool, not another study. Scope, deliberately narrow: only `troubleshoot/v2/cli.py` (the interactive/demo entry point a technician or `--demo` run actually uses) changed, via a new `--min-rate` flag defaulting to 0.01 and documenting why in its help text; `make_case_v1`'s own historical default (`v1/engine.DEFAULT_MIN_RATE = 0.003`) is untouched, and so is every study script (`world2.make_cases`, `study_stop.py`, `study_minrate.py`, and whatever `excess.py` is doing), which keep building cases at the old 0.003 baseline they were frozen at. That scoping is deliberate: Addenda 34-35 tested `tool_ticket_memo2` on the world built by `world2.make_cases()`, not v1's plain single-complaint tool, so only the path that was actually tested (v2's live CLI) changes; and every historical addendum's own regression gates (which re-run old policies and check they reproduce stored numbers) must keep reproducing the world as it was when they ran, or a future re-run would report a false regression. The user accepted the known cost knowingly: at T2, about a third of the 18 studied cells trade a real 2-3 point accuracy loss for the speed; at T3 every cell was a clean win; confidence (wrong-and-confident) never got worse anywhere in either study. 69 -> 72 tests (`tests/test_troubleshoot_v2.py`): the new default is exercised, an explicit override still works, and v1's own default is confirmed unchanged.

**Further decision taken (2026-09-27, user: "make it 0.015"): the live v2 tool's default `min_rate` is raised again, from 0.01 to 0.015.** Unlike the move to 0.01, **this step has NOT been confirmed across Addendum 30's other 17 cells** the way Addendum 35 confirmed 0.01; the only evidence behind it is the primary cell alone, given to the user before they chose it: T2 109 -> 68 minutes (accuracy 0.799 -> 0.770, a 2.9-point drop, wrong-and-confident 0.013 -> 0.002); T3 64 -> 52 minutes (accuracy 0.832 -> 0.827, a 0.6-point drop, wrong-and-confident 0.011 -> 0.008). Both moves are logged together so the two are not confused: **0.01 is a confirmed result (Addendum 35, all 18 cells); 0.015 is a user-directed step beyond it, checked on one cell only.** If a future addendum runs the full 18-cell confirmation on 0.015 and it fails the bar Addendum 35 used, that would be a reason to move the default back toward 0.01, not evidence that anything here was done wrong: this was a known, disclosed gap at the time the default changed. `troubleshoot/v2/cli.py`'s `--min-rate` help text carries the same numbers and the same caveat. Tests updated in place (`tests/test_troubleshoot_v2.py`: the default-value test now expects 0.015; still 72 tests, still passing).

**Note (2026-09-27, not a new addendum): the LLM comparison replayed at the live default (0.015).** `scripts/80_minrate_vs_llm.py` now takes `--min-rate` (defaulting to whatever `v2/cli.py` currently ships, so it can never silently drift from the live tool) and writes a separate report per value instead of overwriting Addendum 35's confirmed one (`vs_llm_report.md` stays the 0.01 comparison; `vs_llm_report_0.015.md` is the new one). **At 0.015, on Addendum 33's own 30 visits, the tool is no longer merely tied with the language model at T2: it is faster outright** (64 minutes against 76, about 16%) for a one-point accuracy difference (81% against 82%) and a far lower confident-wrong rate (0.0% against 16.1%). **At T3 the margin over the language model widens further** (49 minutes against 76, 87% against 79% accuracy, 0.3% against 5.0% confident-wrong). By kind, the accuracy give-up is concentrated in T2's multi-complaint (no shared cause) visits specifically (about 5 points there, against the language model on this sample); single-complaint visits show no real difference either way; shared-cause visits are unaffected by this tuning, as before. This still is not the 18-cell-confirmed comparison 0.01 has; it inherits 0.015's own caveat (checked on the primary cell only) on top of the 30-visit LLM sample's own small size.

## Addendum 36 (dated 2026-09-27, written BEFORE any cell beyond the primary one has been run with `min_rate` = 0.015): does the live default (`min_rate` = 0.015, adopted directly by the user beyond Addendum 35's confirmed 0.01) hold up across both tiers and the other 17 cells, or was it a feature of the one cell it was chosen from? (simulation only; a parameter sweep of the tool's own engine; no LLM; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** The user raised the live tool's `min_rate` from 0.01 (Addendum 35, confirmed across all 18 cells) to 0.015 directly ("make it 0.015"), on the primary cell's numbers alone (T2 109 -> 68 minutes, accuracy 0.799 -> 0.770; T3 64 -> 52 minutes, 0.832 -> 0.827), disclosed as an unconfirmed step at the time. The user then asked for the same 18-cell confirmation Addendum 35 gave 0.01. This addendum is that check, structured identically to Addendum 35 so the two are directly comparable; only the challenger value differs.

**What changes and what does not.** Only `min_rate` moves, from 0.003 (baseline, unchanged) to **0.015 (the challenger, the live tool's current default)**. `tau` stays at its default (0.95). Nothing else about the routine changes. This is a separate, frozen module (`study_minrate2.py`), not a parameterisation of Addendum 35's `study_minrate.py`, so nothing about Addendum 35's own reproducibility is touched by this addendum existing.

**World, metrics, comparison, predictions, decision, gates:** identical in structure to Addendum 35 (all 18 cells, both tiers, the same 1000 events per cell, `TableOracle`, Addendum 21's labels, the same P1-P5 thresholds: 15-of-18 majority, at most 1 WORSE cell, at most 2 safety-regression cells, map-fault safety regressions no worse than M0's). The one substantive difference: **P6 (the reproducibility check) compares against this session's own primary-cell numbers for 0.015, since no earlier addendum measured it**: T2 minutes 67.9 and accuracy 0.770; T3 minutes 52.1 and accuracy 0.827 (computed once, before this addendum's cells were run, and given to the user before they chose 0.015). A failed P6 means NO RESULT.

**Decision (frozen).** *CONFIRMED* if P1, P2, P3 and P4 all hold. *CONFIRMED, UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS* if P1-P3 hold but P5 fails. *NOT CONFIRMED* otherwise.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, any LLM call.

**Limitations named up front.** Same as Addendum 35's: one building shape, placeholder numbers, no real technicians; a pass here is still simulation. Given 0.015 is already the live default, a NOT CONFIRMED result here means the live tool is currently running on a step beyond what full confirmation supports, not that anything was silently broken; the honest response would be to consider moving the default back toward 0.01 pending that finding, not to hide it.

**What would follow.** *CONFIRMED*: 0.015 earns the same standing 0.01 has; no further change needed. *CONFIRMED, UNSAFE UNDER MAP FAULTS*: keep 0.015 where documentation is good, reconsider it where map faults are expected. *NOT CONFIRMED*: report this plainly and recommend reverting the live default to 0.01 (or re-examining 0.015) rather than leaving an unconfirmed value shipped without saying so.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen:** `troubleshoot/v2/study_minrate2.py`, `scripts/81_minrate015_confirm.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_minrate2.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minrate2/`.

**Result of Addendum 36 (2026-09-27; `scripts/81_minrate015_confirm.py`, 97 s on 8 workers; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_minrate2/minrate2_report.md`, `minrate2_cells.csv`, raw `minrate2_raw.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed on the first run (P6 matched the pre-computed reference to the second decimal: primary cell T2 67.88 min / 0.770 against 67.9 / 0.770; T3 52.13 / 0.827 against 52.1 / 0.827). *Primary cell, all events, baseline -> challenger:* T2 109 -> 68 min (80% -> 77% accuracy); T3 64 -> 52 min (83% -> 83%). *Shares of the 18 cells (challenger minus baseline, all events):* **T3: IMPROVES in 18 of 18 cells, WORSE in 0, a safety regression in 0 -- unchanged from 0.01, still a clean sweep.** **T2: IMPROVES in only 5 of 18 (28%), TRADE in the other 13 (72%); WORSE in 0 of 18; a safety regression in 0 of 18.** Every one of the 18 T2 cells is 33-47% faster (bigger than 0.01's 25-39%); accuracy falls by 1.2 to 3.7 points in every cell, crossing the 0.02 tolerance in 13 of them (against 6 of 18 at `min_rate` = 0.01); several cells show a safety GAIN (wrong-and-confident falling), none a regression. *Predictions:* P2 through P6 hold; **P1 does not** (needed 15 of 18 cells at T2, got 5). *Frozen decision: NOT CONFIRMED.*
*Reading.* (1) **At T3, 0.015 is exactly as clean a win as 0.01 was: every cell, no exceptions, no confidence cost.** (2) **At T2, 0.015 is a materially bigger step than 0.01, not a marginal extension of it.** Where 0.01 traded real accuracy in a third of the 18 T2 scenarios, 0.015 does so in nearly three-quarters of them, at a similar per-cell size (2-4 points) but far less often clean. The single primary-cell preview the user chose 0.015 from (T2 109 -> 68 min, 0.799 -> 0.770) undersold how common the T2 accuracy cost would turn out to be across the other 17 cells; it did not undersell the cost's SIZE (68 min / 0.770 here matches the preview almost exactly) or say anything wrong about T3. (3) **Confidence is untouched everywhere, at both `min_rate` values, in every one of the 72 (cell, tier, value) results measured across Addenda 35 and 36.** Whatever this family of changes costs, it has never once cost false confidence.
*What this means for the live default, stated plainly per the addendum's own rule: 0.015 is NOT CONFIRMED, more decisively than a near-miss.* Addendum 35's own bar (15 of 18 T2 cells clean) was written before either run; 0.01 reached 12 of 18, 0.015 reaches 5 of 18. `min_rate` = 0.01 remains the only value in this family with the 18-cell confirmation Addendum 35 gave it. Whether to keep 0.015 knowing this, move back to 0.01, or pick a value in between (0.0125, seen only on the primary cell in the earlier exploratory note) is the user's call to make with this result in hand, not something to decide silently here.

**Final disposition on `min_rate` (2026-09-27, user: "I think 1.2%-3.7% is not a huge deal breaker").** Having seen Addendum 36's full result (T2: only 5 of 18 cells clean, the rest trading 1.2-3.7 accuracy points for 33-47% fewer minutes; T3 unaffected, a clean win everywhere; confidence never worse at either `min_rate` in any of the 72 cell/tier/value results), the user judged that per-cell accuracy cost acceptable and kept the live default at **0.015**. This is a considered decision taken with the NOT CONFIRMED result in hand, not an oversight: `min_rate` = 0.01 remains the value with full 18-cell backing against Addendum 35's own pre-registered bar, and 0.015 is the value actually shipped, on the user's explicit judgement that the bar's specific threshold (a 15-of-18 clean-cell majority) is stricter than the accuracy cost it is protecting against actually warrants here. Nothing in the code changes as a result (0.015 was already `v2/cli.py`'s default); this note exists so a later reader of this file sees a decision, not a gap.

## Addendum 37 (dated 2026-09-28, written BEFORE any code below exists): add "the economizer is not delivering the free cooling it should" as a third upstream cause -- does the routine still capture the shared-cause saving once a cause exists that mostly does NOT make several rooms complain together? (simulation only; a real change to the causes shared by v0/v1/v2; DESIGN ONLY, NOT YET IMPLEMENTED, NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** A real technician video ("VAV Not Cooling EP173"; see TROUBLESHOOT_V2.md) diagnosed a stuck-low economizer (15% position, "should be opened a lot more") as the true cause of a no-cool call, after checking return/supply temperatures and manually overriding a water valve that turned out fine. Neither "the economizer under-delivers free cooling" nor "the discharge/supply-air setpoint is wrong" exists anywhere in `causes.py`; the only two upstream/AHU-level causes are `ahu_fan_off` and `low_static`. A literature check (same file) found this is not a one-off: 64% of surveyed economizers have some fault (New Buildings Institute, cited in a CEC/WCEC case study); California Title 24 has required automated fault detection for it since 2014, naming "not economizing when it should" and "damper not modulating" among the faults it must catch; PNNL's "Rooftop Unit Embedded Diagnostics" (PNNL-23790) defines named algorithms for exactly this (AFDD1: is the damper modulating; AFDD3: does the unit economize when it should) built on a handful of BAS temperature and command points; and a fault of this kind is caught only 25% of the time without an automated prompt, versus 75% with one -- a cited number for the exact premise this tool is built on.

**The cause, PLACEHOLDER, not fitted.** `econ_fault`: the outdoor-air economizer does not bring in as much free cooling as conditions allow when there is a call for cooling -- a stuck-near-minimum or non-modulating damper, an actuator or linkage failure, or a wrong changeover/discharge-air setpoint, lumped as one cause the same way `low_static` already lumps several distinct mechanisms under one label. Per PNNL's own account, the fault is usually invisible to occupants because the compressor "picks up the slack"; only in extreme cases (high load, hot weather) does it produce an actual comfort complaint. That asymmetry -- common as a latent equipment fault, uncommon as a complaint trigger -- is the central modelling problem this addendum has to get right, and the two must not be conflated: `f`'s upstream-cause shares are about complaint-causing events in a visit's window, not the 64% figure, which is about latent equipment condition.

**Design: how this attaches to the existing model, and why every default construction is unaffected.** Checked against the current code (`v1/causes.py`, `v1/catalogue.py`, `v1/engine.py`, `v2/joint.py`, `v2/world2.py`) before writing this, not assumed:
1. **The new cause is defined but not enrolled.** A `CauseSpec("econ_fault", "upstream", OTHER, ...)` is added to `causes.py` as a named object, **not** appended to the shared `CAUSES` tuple, so `DEFAULT_SHARES`, `BY_ID`, and the file's own `assert sum(shares) == 1` invariant are untouched -- no existing caller anywhere in v0, v1 or v2 sees a different cause list or a different (even infinitesimally, via the `PRIOR_FLOOR = 1e-6` floor) prior unless it explicitly opts in.
2. **Two functions gain an additive, default-empty parameter.** `leaf_causes(reheat, pressure_independent, extra: tuple = ())` returns its current list **plus** `extra`; `make_prior_v1(..., extra_causes: tuple = ())` passes `extra_causes` through to `leaf_causes` and relies on its own existing normalisation (`total[lump] = sum(shares[c.id] for c in leaves if c.lump == lump)`) to renormalise automatically once an extra cause and its share are present -- no manual rescaling of the other nine `OTHER`-lump shares is needed; supplying `shares={**C.DEFAULT_SHARES, "econ_fault": 0.06}` alongside `extra_causes=(C.ECON_FAULT,)` is sufficient. With `extra=()` (the default), both functions are byte-for-byte what they are today.
3. **`CaseV1` gains one field, `extra_causes: tuple = ()`,** threaded from a new `make_case_v1(..., extra_causes=())` parameter to `Session._prior()`'s existing call into `make_prior_v1`. `checks` and `shares` are already overridable per case (confirmed in `engine.py`), so the two new checks below need no engine change at all: a case that wants them is built with `checks=K.CATALOGUE + (K.BAS_ECONOMIZER, K.AHU_ECONOMIZER_TEST)`.
4. **Two new checks, defined but not enrolled in `catalogue.py`'s `CATALOGUE` tuple** (same additive pattern): `bas_economizer` (control-system read, subject `ahu`, access 0 / do 2 / wait 0, matching `bas_ahu_fan`/`bas_static`'s `BAS_AHU` need and BG=0.03, `detects=lv(econ_fault="strong", low_static="weak")`, built on PNNL AFDD1/AFDD3's comparison of the outdoor-air damper command against outdoor/return/mixed-air temperatures) and `ahu_economizer_test` (physical, subject `ahu`, access 15 / do 5, matching `ahu_visit`/`ahu_gauge`'s need, `detects=lv(econ_fault="strong")`, standing in for the video's manual-override verification). Placeholder response levels only, in the file's own `lv()` scale (`strong=0.92`); no numeric superiority over existing checks is assumed.
5. **`v2/joint.py` and `v2/world2.py` are generalised, additively, from two upstream states to N.** `JointSettings` gains `extra_states: tuple = ()`, `extra_affect: dict = {}` (each extra state's P(a room on it complains), the `r_fan`/`r_low` equivalent) and `extra_share: dict = {}` (each extra state's share of `f`, generalising `fan_share`); `states`/`upstream` become properties (`STATES + self.extra_states`, `self.states[1:]`); `state_prior` becomes `[1-f, f*fan_share*(1-extra_total), f*(1-fan_share)*(1-extra_total), *(f*extra_share[s] for s in extra_states)]` where `extra_total = sum(extra_share.values())` -- **with `extra_states=()` this is algebraically identical to today's formula** (`extra_total=0`). `ahu_row`, `bears_on_ahu` and `JointModel`'s J-table construction, currently hand-written for exactly two named states (`i_fan`, `i_low`, a `(3, n_causes)` array), are rewritten as loops over `self.upstream`, generic in the number of states. **One registered semantic change inside that generalisation:** `bears_on_ahu` currently requires a check to detect **both** named upstream causes at >=0.5; generalised literally to "all" it would stop qualifying `static_at_box` the moment a third upstream state exists (since `static_at_box` does not mention `econ_fault` and falls back to its 0.04 background rate). The registered rule is instead **"at least one"** upstream cause at >=0.5 and every non-upstream cause at <=0.5 -- provably identical to "both" in the current two-state case, because `static_at_box` detects both existing upstream causes strongly, so "any" and "both" agree there; it only starts to differ once a third state exists. `WorldParams` gets the matching `extra_states`/`extra_share` fields and generalised `state_prior`; `draw_event`'s `rng.choice(3, p=sp)` becomes `rng.choice(len(sp), p=sp)`, identical to today whenever `len(sp) == 3`; `Event.shared`/`kind` check membership in whichever upstream tuple the study actually used, not the bare module-level `UPSTREAM`. `R_AFFECT` stays a plain dict; a study wanting `econ_fault` extends its own copy, `{**R_AFFECT, "econ_fault": r_econ}`, rather than editing the module's.
6. **Not registered as part of this addendum, left out to keep scope down:** a `FEEL_MULT["too_hot"]["econ_fault"]` multiplier (plausible -- a warm-but-not-airless complaint is a better fit for this cause than a generic one -- but untested and not needed to answer this addendum's question); splitting `econ_fault` into PNNL's separate AFDD1 (damper not modulating) and AFDD3 (not economizing when it should) mechanisms; economizer sensor faults (AFDD2) and excess-outdoor-air faults (AFDD4-6), the opposite problem.

**Placeholder numbers, cited where the citation supports the shape of the number and not its value (nothing here is fitted).**

| Quantity | Value | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| `econ_fault`'s `causes.py` share (added via `extra_causes`/`shares`, existing nine `OTHER`-lump shares untouched, renormalised automatically) | 0.06 | Between `ahu_fan_off` (0.05) and `low_static` (0.12): plausible as a first-order cause of a complaint, but the PNNL account that the compressor usually "picks up the slack" argues against ranking it above `low_static`. |
| `f`'s three-way split (`fan_share`, `1-fan_share` scaled by `1-extra_total`, `extra_share["econ_fault"]`) | 5 : 12 : 6 of `f` (~0.217 : 0.522 : 0.261) | The existing derivation, `causes.py`'s own placeholder shares turned into odds (5/17 : 12/17 already used for `FAN_SHARE`), extended the same way with 0.06 added. |
| `r_econ` (`extra_affect["econ_fault"]`) | 0.15 | Well under `r_fan` (0.9) and `r_low` (0.5), reflecting "only in extreme cases" (PNNL-23790) -- the single most uncertain number in this table, since it collapses a genuinely weather- and load-dependent phenomenon (how close to design capacity the compressor already is) into one fixed rate. Not modelled: outdoor temperature, load, or time of day. |
| `bas_economizer` detects | `econ_fault` = 0.92 (strong), `low_static` = 0.20 (weak), bg 0.03 | Matches `bas_ahu_fan`/`bas_static`'s own placeholder pattern (a strong primary detection, a weak cross-detection of the other upstream cause, the same background rate). |
| `ahu_economizer_test` detects | `econ_fault` = 0.92 (strong), bg 0.04 | Matches `ahu_visit`/`ahu_gauge`'s pattern: the same information as the cheap read, at the AHU-visit access cost (15 min). |

**Predictions (written before any code exists; each strict; primary-cell-equivalent world, `f = 0.03`, both tiers unless stated).** P1 (gate, not graded): with `extra_states=()`/`extra_causes=()` (nothing about this addendum invoked), every function touched -- `state_prior`, `ahu_row`, `bears_on_ahu`, the J-table, `draw_event` -- reproduces Addendum 31's own stored 20-event replay exactly; a failure here means the generalisation was not actually additive and the whole design is rejected before anything else is read. P2: on events whose true first-order cause is `econ_fault`, `tool_ticket_memo2`-with-econ names it correctly at least 70% of the time at T3 (a low sanity floor on whether the new cause is diagnosable at all with the tools given it, not a claim about how good those tools are). P3: on `shared` econ-caused visits (two or more complaints whose cause is `econ_fault` on the same air handler), `tool_ticket_memo2`-with-econ takes at most 130% of `tool_joint2`-with-econ's minutes -- Addendum 30/31's "the routine captures it" verdict, re-tested at a looser tolerance because `r_econ`'s low affect rate means genuinely shared econ-caused visits will be rarer and the routine's "ask the neighbours" trick has less to work with; **this could go either way and is the addendum's actual question**, not assumed. P4 (safety): `econ_fault` complaints' wrong-and-confident rate is at most 2 points worse than `low_static`'s rate in Addendum 31's stored primary-cell numbers, at either tier.

**Decision (frozen).** *NO RESULT* if P1 fails (the extension itself is broken; nothing past it is meaningful). Otherwise, on the P3 question: *THE ROUTINE STILL CAPTURES IT* if P3 holds and P4 holds; *THE ROUTINE NEEDS HELP FOR THIS CAUSE* if P3 fails; *UNSAFE* (reported regardless of P3) if P4 fails. P2 is reported as a diagnosability check alongside whichever of these applies, not as a separate named outcome.

**Gates (evaluated before any table is read; a failed gate means NO RESULT).** G1 the extension is additive (P1, above; also checked directly: causes.py's `assert sum(shares)==1` for the unmodified `CAUSES`/`DEFAULT_SHARES` still holds, unedited). G2 the new cause's own probabilities are internally consistent (the three-way `f` split and the renormalised `OTHER`-lump shares each sum to 1 to 1e-9). G3 replay (a stored trace of the new policies re-run from their own logged answers reproduces the same beliefs and minutes). G4 reproducibility across worker counts. G5 completeness and pairing across the cells run. G6 the tool's settings (the new fields included in whatever tag this study's `tool_settings_tag`-equivalent uses) are identical across every cell of a run.

**Scope of this first run.** One cell at the shared-frequency, map and reliability levels Addendum 30 called `assumed`/`M0`/`R0` (its primary cell), both tiers, 1000 events -- **not** the full 18-cell sweep Addenda 30/31/35/36 each eventually got. This addendum answers "does the extension work, and what does a first look say" before spending an 18-cell run on placeholder numbers this uncertain; a full sweep is a natural follow-up (Addendum 38+) if this one's answer is interesting enough to be worth confirming broadly, not assumed here.

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, any LLM call. The video and the literature review motivate the cause and its check design; no number from either is used as a probability without being marked PLACEHOLDER in the table above.

**Limitations named up front.** Everything Addendum 30/31 already disclose applies (one building shape, placeholder numbers everywhere, stand-ins are mine, nothing about real buildings or real technicians). In addition: `r_econ` collapses a weather- and load-dependent phenomenon into one fixed rate, which is very likely the wrong shape of assumption, not just the wrong number; `econ_fault` lumps at least two mechanistically different PNNL fault categories (a stuck actuator vs. a wrong setpoint) that this addendum does not try to tell apart; the two new checks' reliabilities are unmeasured placeholders exactly like every other check in the catalogue; a single cell at one frequency level cannot say whether the P3 answer generalises; the whole exercise starts from one real video and a literature review of fault PREVALENCE, neither of which says how often a REAL visit's complaint is actually caused by this, which remains as unknown as the shared-cause frequency Addendum 30/31 already flagged as the thing real data would need to answer.

**What would follow.** *THE ROUTINE STILL CAPTURES IT*: no change to the earlier verdict is needed; the cheap routine generalises past the two causes it was tested on. *THE ROUTINE NEEDS HELP FOR THIS CAUSE*: the first concrete case where the joint machinery's extra reasoning would be worth its extra minutes for a specific, evidenced real-world fault, worth a closer look rather than shelving the machinery. *UNSAFE*: fix the new checks' placeholder reliabilities or `r_econ` before reading anything else from this cause. Either way, a full 18-cell sweep and a real-data check of how often complaints are actually economizer-caused are the honest next steps, not assumed here.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `v1/causes.py` (`ECON_FAULT`, three new `SOURCES` entries, `GROUPS["upstream"]`'s text, the `extra`/`extra_causes` parameters), `v1/catalogue.py` (`BAS_ECONOMIZER`, `AHU_ECONOMIZER_TEST`), `v1/engine.py` (`CaseV1.extra_causes`, `make_case_v1(extra_causes=...)`), `v2/joint.py` (the `extra_states`/`extra_affect`/`extra_share` generalisation of `JointSettings`, `ahu_row`, `bears_on_ahu`, the J-table, `apply`), `v2/world2.py` (the matching `WorldParams` fields, `draw_event`'s `len(sp)` fix, `Event.shared`/`kind`), a new `v2/study_econ.py` (cell, run, analyse, predictions, gates, decision, mirroring `study_joint2.py`'s structure), `scripts/82_troubleshoot_econ.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_econ.py` (including an explicit test that every function above is byte-identical to today with no extra state supplied); outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_econ/`.

**Status: implemented and run (2026-09-28), after the user said "ok, build this".** One clarification made while implementing, before any event was drawn: `bears_on_ahu`'s registered "at least one" rule needed one existing unit test's boundary case rewritten (`tests/test_troubleshoot_joint2.py`, "weakening one of `static_at_box`'s two detections still qualifies through the other, only weakening both does not") -- the behaviour was registered above before implementation; the test was locked to the old "both" wording and needed updating to match, not a deviation from the design. Before any event was drawn, the additive refactor was verified directly: 3,720 replayed episodes across Addenda 30's and 31's full policy sets (`tool_joint`, `tool_ticket_memo`, `tool_joint2`, `tool_ticket_memo2`, `tool_joint2_la2`, `tool_joint3`) against their ORIGINAL stored numbers, 0 differ; the full 974-test suite passed unchanged.

**Result of Addendum 37 (2026-09-28; `scripts/82_troubleshoot_econ.py`, 104 s single-threaded, no parallel workers needed; report `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_econ/econ_report.md`, `econ_cells.csv`, raw `econ_raw.pkl`/`econ_ref.pkl`).** *Gates:* G1-G5 all passed (G1: 14,400 re-run Addendum 30 episodes against its stored numbers, 0 differ). *P1 (additive) holds.* *P2 (diagnosable) holds at both tiers*: of 51 complaints whose true cause was `econ_fault`, the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`) named it correctly 78.4% of the time at T2 and 88.2% at T3 (both above the 70% floor). **P3, the addendum's actual question, holds cleanly at both tiers, and more decisively than registered:** on the 16 `econ_fault`-shared visits (of 1000; a rare event, as `r_econ` = 0.15 predicts), the routine took 99% of `tool_joint2`'s minutes at T2 (206 vs 209) and only **49%** at T3 (81 vs 166) -- the joint machinery was more than TWICE as slow as the routine on this cause at T3, not merely not-better. **P4 (safety) fails at both tiers:** the routine's wrong-and-confident rate on `econ_fault` complaints was 5.9% at both T2 and T3, against a reference `low_static` rate (a fresh 2-state world, same routine) of 1.6% / 1.4% -- about 4.3-4.5 points over the registered +2-point tolerance. *Frozen decision: UNSAFE* (P4 failing overrides P3 holding, exactly as registered).
*Reading, with the caveats it needs.* (1) **The routine's speed advantage generalises, and by more than the registered tolerance anticipated:** even for a cause with a much lower affect rate than `ahu_fan_off`/`low_static`, engineered specifically to make shared visits rare and the routine's "ask the neighbours" trick less obviously useful, the routine matched or beat the full joint machinery on speed. The T3 result (joint machinery over twice as slow) suggests the joint session's per-complaint check budget (Exploratory note E1's diagnosis of the earlier excess) bites harder, not softer, on a rarer, more BAS-detectable cause: `bas_economizer` is cheap and informative once the routine's own memo/neighbour logic has ruled out the usual suspects, so the routine reaches `tau` fast, while the joint session's larger pooled budget lets it keep spending minutes other complaints in the group do not need. (2) **The safety failure is real, not registered noise:** the SAME 5.9% rate at both tiers, against a materially lower and mutually consistent low_static reference at both tiers, is a specific, checkable signal, not a coincidence of one tier's placeholder numbers. A plausible mechanism (a hypothesis, not tested here): `econ_fault`'s lower causes.py share (0.06) and lower `r_econ` (0.15) mean the routine's prior has to travel further before crossing `tau`, and once it does, the belief is resting on a thinner base rate than `low_static`'s -- exactly the setting in which a placeholder detection rate (`bas_economizer`'s "strong" = 0.92, chosen to match the existing catalogue's convention, not measured) can produce a confident, wrong conclusion more often. (3) Only 51 `econ_fault` complaints and 16 `econ_fault`-shared visits fed every number above; a single cell, not the registered-as-future 18-cell sweep, cannot say whether this generalises across map faults or shared-cause frequencies. (4) Everything here is the model's own placeholder family; nothing is evidence about a real economizer fault's real detectability or a real technician's real confidence.
*What would follow.* The speed question (P3) is answered clearly enough that a full 18-cell sweep is now worth doing if this cause is ever taken further. The safety question (P4) is the one that actually blocks calling this settled: before anything here is treated as more than a simulation exercise, the placeholder detection rate on `bas_economizer`/`ahu_economizer_test` (both "strong" = 0.92, unmeasured) would need to be examined first, since P4's failure could be an artefact of that specific placeholder rather than a property of low-base-rate causes in general -- a cheap next check, not requiring a new addendum, would be a small sweep of that one detection rate holding everything else fixed.

**The named next step, answered against real data (2026-09-29, Gabe Fierro recommended LBNL's Fault Detection and Diagnostics dataset; user: "build it and run it against the dataset").** LBNL's Experimental RTU dataset (ORNL's Flexible Research Platform: a real 12.5-ton Trane RTU serving 10 VAV zones, ground-truth-labeled faults, freely available, DOI 10.25984/1881324) includes exactly the fault this check targets: "OA damper stuck" at 4 severities (5%, 10%, 50%, 100% open) and "incorrect economizer setpoint" at 4 offsets, imposed for a full day each across 4 seasons, at 1-minute resolution, with the OA/RA damper command and OA/MA/RA temperatures `bas_economizer` compares. Implemented the check's own stated rule literally (AFDD1-style: during occupied hours, a minute qualifies as "conditions call for more free cooling" if OAT < 50F and OAT < RAT - 2F; a day reads back ABNORMAL if the OA damper command sits at or below 15 (near the documented 10% minimum) in at least half of qualifying minutes; days with fewer than 30 qualifying minutes excluded). Code: `scripts/86_econ_afdd_real_data.py`; output `results/docmismatch/econ_afdd_real/econ_afdd_report.md`.

**Result.** Clean signal on the check's literal target, though small-sample: **0% false-positive rate on 5 unfaulted days; 100% true-positive rate on the 4 days testing "damper stuck near minimum"** -- consistent with (not proof of, at n=4-5) the placeholder "strong"=0.92, not a reason to lower it for that exact scenario. **A real scope gap, not a measurement artefact: the check's wording ("stuck near its MINIMUM") only covers half of what the dataset labels "OA damper stuck" -- the other half, stuck OPEN (50%, 100%), is missed 100% of the time** (0 of 5 evaluable days flagged), because a wide-open damper never matches "near minimum". The incorrect-setpoint cases are NOT a reliable finding at this sample size (1-3 usable days total per direction: lowered-setpoint showed 0% flagged where some detection was expected; raised-setpoint showed 100% flagged where a miss was expected, plausibly the documented mixed-air 45F low-limit interlock -- a control interaction this simple rule does not model -- dominating the few very-cold qualifying minutes left at a raised setpoint) -- reported for completeness, not treated as a result.

**Reading.** Addendum 37's own named next step ("the placeholder detection rate would need to be examined first... P4's failure could be an artefact of that specific placeholder") now has a real, if partial, answer: on the ONE failure mode the check actually targets, real lab data does not contradict the placeholder. But the exercise also surfaces something the placeholder debate had not: `econ_fault` as modeled is a single lumped cause with a single detection rate, standing in for a check that, on real data, only reliably catches one of at least two distinct real-world "OA damper stuck" failure signatures. If stuck-open failures are anywhere near as common in practice as stuck-low ones, the true aggregate detection rate for "economizer not delivering the free cooling it should" is likely lower than 0.92, not higher -- which would make Addendum 37's UNSAFE finding (P4) more likely to hold, not less, once real detection performance is accounted for rather than assumed. This changes no frozen decision and touches no code path the live tool uses; it is real-data evidence bearing on a placeholder, not a new addendum.

**A widened rule closes the stuck-open gap (2026-09-29, user: "so, even from paper, we have no way to fix economizer?" / "yes, build and test that widened rule").** The gap above was in how narrowly the check's own description was read, not necessarily in the underlying detection idea: Rule B drops the "stuck near minimum" direction and just asks whether the damper moved at all -- one calendar day is ABNORMAL if occupied-hours OAT swings by at least 8F (a real reason to modulate existed) while the damper's own range of motion that day is at most 5 percentage points (it didn't; needs 200+ occupied minutes to evaluate a day). Same script, same data, added as a second rule reported alongside the first.

**Result.** Rule B catches BOTH directions on a larger sample than Rule A could evaluate: stuck-low 88% (7 of 8 days -- one miss right at the OAT-swing threshold), stuck-open 100% (8 of 8 days), false-positive rate still clean at 0 of 14 baseline days (a bigger, still-clean sample than Rule A's 5). **Rule B does NOT catch either incorrect-setpoint direction (0 of 16 days) -- correctly so, not a failure:** a shifted setpoint doesn't freeze the damper, it just changes when a still-modulating damper decides to move, a different fault mechanism a "did it move" test was never going to see; that would need a genuinely different check (comparing WHEN the economizer engages against the correct temperature threshold), not a wider version of this one.

**Reading.** A corrected, two-sided version of the check's own logic catches both real stuck-damper failure modes at a rate at least as good as the 0.92 placeholder, on real data -- so the detection-rate concern raised above is now substantially narrowed: the remaining, unaddressed gap is specifically the incorrect-setpoint fault, not "economizer faults" broadly. This still changes no frozen decision (Addendum 37 stays UNSAFE; the safety failure's other named hypothesis -- the low base rate of a rare cause meeting a check that also weakly cross-detects `low_static` -- is untouched by any of this) and touches no code path the live tool uses; it narrows, rather than closes, the open question Addendum 37 left for anyone taking this cause further.

**A third rule closes the setpoint gap too (2026-09-29, user: "update the setpoint check for the correct threshold").** Rule A/B both target a stuck damper; neither looks at WHEN the economizer engages, so neither could ever catch a wrong enable temperature. Rule C: estimate each day's own empirical crossover (the warmest OAT the damper was ever seen clearly open, >20%) and compare it to the correct 50F; flag if 3F or more off in either direction, or if the damper never opened despite OAT reaching 45F or colder that day. Same script, same data, added as a third rule.

**Result.** Clean on baseline (0 of 5 days flagged). **100% detection on the `Inc_Eco_SP_-2/-4` files (5 of 5 days)**, whose empirical crossover sits 3-9F above the correct 50F. The `Inc_Eco_SP_2/4` files gave only 1 evaluable day under this rule (most days in that group never got cold enough to be evaluable at all) -- that one day was also correctly flagged (never opened despite OAT reaching 43.5F). **A correction, surfaced by this exercise and reported rather than carried forward silently: the PREVIOUS write-up's group labels ("LOWERED" for `-2/-4`, "RAISED" for `2/4`) assumed a physical direction from the filename's sign that Rule C's own measurements contradict** -- the `-2/-4` files behave as an effectively HIGHER/less restrictive setpoint (opens at higher OAT than correct) and the one evaluable `2/4` day behaves as an effectively LOWER/more restrictive one (never opens even when clearly cold enough), the opposite of what was assumed. The report's group labels are corrected to describe filename suffix and observed behavior, not an assumed mechanism.

**Reading.** Between Rule B (both stuck-damper directions) and Rule C (the setpoint direction), every fault type in this dataset now has a working detector on real data, at a rate at least as good as the 0.92 placeholder Addendum 37 shipped with unmeasured. The gap this whole three-rule exercise found was in how narrowly the ORIGINAL check's wording was read (one direction of one failure mode), not in whether these faults are detectable at all -- on this dataset, they all are. What remains genuinely untested, and is not resolved by any of this: whether a single simulated check with one placeholder detection number can honestly stand in for three distinct real signatures at once, and Addendum 37's other named safety hypothesis (the check's weak cross-detection of `low_static`), which no version of this exercise touches. Frozen decision unchanged: UNSAFE. Code and data unchanged from the note above (`scripts/86_econ_afdd_real_data.py`, same report file, extended with a third rule and table).

**Deriving and shipping an honest single number, then re-testing P4 with it (2026-09-29, user: "do 1, 2, 3" -- update the placeholder, re-run P4, and only then consider wiring it in and re-checking the LLM comparison).**

*1. The placeholder.* Rules A-C each report a detection rate CONDITIONAL on the day they could evaluate at all -- but several of those days simply weren't cold enough that season to test the fault, and a real technician doesn't get to pick which day they visit on. Combining Rule B and Rule C (OR: flag if either fires) and counting EVERY test day per fault type, not just the ones each rule alone could evaluate, gives the honest, unconditional, single-visit detection rate: stuck-low 100% (8/8), stuck-open 100% (8/8), setpoint (`-2/-4` files) 62% (5/8), setpoint (`2/4` files) 12% (1/8) -- **pooled across all 32 real fault-days: 68.8%**, against 0% false positives on 14 baseline days. Most of the shortfall against the old 0.92 is weather, not the check's own sensitivity: a wrong setpoint is often simply unobservable on a day that never gets cold enough to expose it. `bas_economizer`'s `detects=lv(econ_fault=...)` changed from the shared "strong" (0.92) to an explicit, measured 0.69 (`v1/catalogue.py`); `ahu_economizer_test` (a manual override at the AHU, a different, non-weather-dependent method this dataset has no data on) is left unchanged. New source added (`v1/causes.py`, `SOURCES["LBNL_FDD"]`). Full test suite unaffected (no test pinned the old value).

*2. Re-running P4.* `scripts/82_troubleshoot_econ.py --out results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_econ_afdd_v2/` (same script, same seed, same cell -- only the catalogue's live detection rate differs from the original frozen run, which is preserved untouched at `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_econ/`). All 5 gates passed again. **Result: econ_fault's wrong-and-confident rate is 5.9% at BOTH tiers -- numerically IDENTICAL to the original run's 5.9%/5.9%, to one decimal place, despite the detection rate dropping from 0.92 to 0.69.** Diagnosability (P2) fell as expected with the lower detection rate (T2 78.4%->78.4%, unchanged; T3 88.2%->74.5%, a real drop) and speed (P3) still holds at both tiers. **P4 still does not hold; the frozen decision is still UNSAFE.**

*Reading.* This is a more decisive result than either detection-rate exercise anticipated: **the wrong-and-confident rate did not move at all when the detection rate was substantially lowered, which is strong direct evidence against Addendum 37's own hypothesis that "the placeholder detection rate could be an artefact" behind P4's failure.** A rate this stable across a large change in one input points at a different, structural driver -- most likely the OTHER named hypothesis (bas_economizer's weak cross-detection of low_static, `lv(..., low_static="weak")`, giving belief a path onto the wrong candidate independent of how well the true-positive side is tuned) or the low prior share/`r_econ` dynamics, neither of which this exercise touched. The detection-rate concern that motivated most of today's work is now closed, not open: it was checked, partly fixed, and shown not to be the thing keeping this UNSAFE.

*3. Wiring it into the live tool / re-checking the LLM comparison -- not done, and not warranted.* This step was explicitly conditional on P4 coming back safe. It did not; if anything it is now on firmer ground than before. `econ_fault` stays exactly where Addendum 37 left it: unenrolled, untouched by the live tool (`v2/cli.py`), and outside the world the LLM comparison (Addendum 33 and this session's extensions) was run against. Nothing about "our tool vs. the LLM" changes as a result of any of today's work.

**Additional, unregistered finding (2026-09-28, asked directly by the user after the result above): did adding `econ_fault` make the overall tool faster, not just faster on the rare shared-econ subset P3 tested?** No. Comparing this run's "all events" minutes against Addendum 31's ORIGINAL stored primary-cell numbers (same nominal settings, `min_rate` = 0.003 at both; different draws, and the world is structurally different once `f` is split three ways instead of two, so this is directional, not a matched-pairs comparison): T2 routine 111.7 -> 119.6 min (+7.1%), T2 `tool_joint2` 118.8 -> 129.5 min (+9.0%), T3 routine 64.6 -> 64.0 min (-0.9%, flat), T3 `tool_joint2` 71.2 -> 77.9 min (+9.4%). Three of four rows got slower; none got meaningfully faster. **The mechanism is generic, not specific to this cause:** once `econ_fault` exists as a candidate, every visit -- including the 98.4% with nothing to do with an economizer -- must weigh and usually rule it out, a small constant tax paid everywhere; P3's saving is real but is earned only on the 1.6% of visits where the cause is actually present, too rare to show up in the aggregate. This does not change the frozen decision (P4's safety failure already blocks calling this settled), but it corrects an impression the P3 result alone could leave: the addendum's registered predictions never asked whether the whole tool gets faster, only whether the routine keeps its edge over the joint machinery on the subset that exercises this cause, and it should not be read as a net speed win for the tool as shipped.

**Second additional, unregistered finding (2026-09-28, asked directly by the user): did overall accuracy at least hold steady while speed did not? No -- it fell in all four rows**, comparing the same two runs: T2 routine 79.7% -> 77.4% (-2.3 points), T2 `tool_joint2` 80.2% -> 79.2% (-1.0 points), T3 routine 82.5% -> 80.1% (-2.4 points), T3 `tool_joint2` 84.3% -> 79.6% (-4.7 points). The same directional caveat as the speed comparison applies (different draws, not matched-pairs). **The mechanism is the whole-tool version of P4's finding, not a separate one:** `econ_fault` is a live candidate with nonzero prior on every complaint now, including ones whose true cause is something else, and `bas_economizer`'s weak cross-detection of `low_static` (0.20 in the catalogue entry) gives belief mass a concrete path to drift from a true `low_static` cause onto the wrong new candidate where before there was no such option. Adding a candidate cause did not only cost minutes everywhere (the first finding above) and produce overconfident wrong answers specifically on `econ_fault` complaints (P4); it measurably degraded correctness on the whole population of visits, most of which have nothing to do with an economizer. Combined with the two findings above, this addendum's honest scorecard is three problems, not one: no net speed win, a real accuracy cost, and a safety failure on the complaints it targets.

## Exploratory note E2 (dated 2026-09-29; NOT a pre-registered test): what does BAS access + a point map buy when attributes are not documented? (simulation only)

**Question and status.** Addendum 27's data tiers are cumulative: T3 = T2 + `bas_access` + `point_map`, so every T3 result already reported also has T2's `attributes` (reheat type, K-factor, design airflow, inlet size, static setpoint). The user reported that in the buildings they actually see, T1 and T3-level access are common but T2-level attribute documentation is rare -- i.e. control-system access and a point map often exist without documented design values. No existing tier covers that combination. This note is exploratory, asked and built directly (no predictions were written before running it): it adds one arm and reads off what the existing apparatus already says.

**Method.** Reuses Addendum 27's exact machinery unmodified: the same synthetic building, the same per-episode draws, the same seed (`study_tiers.py`). Adds one new arm, **T3-noattr** = T1's items (`equipment_list`, `claims`) plus `bas_access` and `point_map`, but no `attributes` -- a custom item set, not a named tier (`data.py`'s `tier_items()` already supports this; the docstring gives exactly this kind of combination as its example use). The new arm runs on a previously-unused RNG channel (index 5; the original five arms use 0-4), so it cannot perturb any arm Addendum 27 already reported -- verified directly: the four shared arms (T1, T2, T2+access, T3), recomputed here, are byte-identical to a fresh call of the original, untouched `study_tiers.run_cell` (30 episodes, every field, every check sequence). Code: `troubleshoot/v1/study_tiers_ext.py`, `scripts/83_troubleshoot_tiers_ext.py`; output `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tiers_ext/` (`tiers_ext_report.md`, `tiers_ext_raw.pkl`, `tiers_ext_analysis.pkl`, `tiers_ext_cells.csv`). All 12 of Addendum 27's cells, 500 episodes each, hardware/configuration-cause episodes only (the ones every arm can be compared on). Run time 110 s.

**Result.** In the primary cell (R0 / assumed / full, n = 438): T1 91 min / 71% accuracy, T2 85 min / 77%, T2+access 85 min / 84%, **T3-noattr 55 min / 79%**, T3 48 min / 82%. Over all 12 cells, three comparisons: (1) **T3-noattr vs T1** (what access + a map buy with nothing else added): IMPROVES in 12/12 cells, -39% minutes and +8 accuracy points in the primary cell -- a clean win, no downside. (2) **T3-noattr vs T2** (access + map without attributes, vs. attributes alone, no access): IMPROVES in 12/12 cells, -35% minutes and +2.3 accuracy points in the primary cell -- the point map beats documented attributes on both axes at once. (3) **T3-noattr vs T2+access** (isolating the point map itself, since both arms otherwise differ only in whether attributes vs. the map is missing): IMPROVES in 11/12 cells, TRADE (faster-but-less-accurate) in 1/12; in the primary cell -35% minutes but -4.6 accuracy points -- the map's speed win is large and the accuracy cost of skipping attributes is real, just usually smaller than the speed gain by Addendum 21's frozen thresholds. (4) **T3 vs T3-noattr** (what attributes still buy once access + map already exist): IMPROVES in 7/12 cells, NEITHER in 5/12, TRADE in 0/12 -- attributes never make T3-noattr worse, but only about half the time do they clear the threshold to count as a meaningful further improvement; in the primary cell, full T3 is 7.2 min (13%) faster and 3.2 accuracy points ahead of T3-noattr. Wrong-and-confident stays at 0-1% for every arm in the primary (R0) cell; it rises with degraded reliability (R1/R2) across all arms roughly together, not specifically for T3-noattr -- no distinct safety signal for this arm.

**Reading.** For a documentation profile like the one reported -- point map and BAS access present, attribute/design-value documentation absent -- this model's own placeholder family says most of T3's speed benefit survives without T2's attributes: T3-noattr is 55 min against T3's 48 (about 87% of full T3's speed) and clearly beats both T1 (91 min) and T2 (85 min). The place a real cost shows up is accuracy relative to T2+access specifically (attributes-with-access-but-no-map): -4.6 points in the primary cell. That comparison matters less for a profile where T2+access itself is rare (per the user's report, T2-level documentation is the uncommon case, not the common alternative on the table) -- the more relevant baselines, T1 and T2 alone, are both beaten on speed AND accuracy by T3-noattr. The honest caveat: this changes no frozen decision and adds no new cause or check; it is a re-slicing of Addendum 27's own model family under one new documentation combination, with the same placeholder minutes, placeholder detection rates, and synthetic-building limitations that note already discloses. It says the reported real-world gap between T2 and T3 documentation matters much less than skipping T2 entirely might suggest -- not that any of these numbers describe a real building or a real technician.

**Scoping (2026-09-29, per Gabe Fierro): this documentation profile (T1/T3 common, T2 rare) is specific to buildings over 5,000 sq ft**; smaller buildings' profile is unaddressed here.

**Extended to the language-model comparison (2026-09-29, user: "run this new T1 and T3 combined thing against LLM technician and see how it holds up").** A different question from the result above: does T3-noattr hold up against Addendum 33's language model, on Addendum 33's own world and 30-visit sample, not just against the routine's own other tiers on Addendum 27's synthetic building? Not a new experiment in the sense of running the language model again: Addendum 33's stored `llm_records.csv` numbers are reused unchanged (no new LLM call); only the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`) is replayed at the T3-noattr item set on the exact same 30 visits and world (`world2.py`'s Addendum 30 primary cell), the same pattern `scripts/80_minrate_vs_llm.py` used for the `min_rate` variants. `T3-noattr` is added to `world2.TIER_ITEMS` as one new key at import time (a strictly additive dict mutation nothing existing looks up; `TIER_NAMES` was already bound to `("T2", "T3")` at that module's import, so nothing that loops over it sees a third tier). Code: `v2/study_llm_ext.py`, `scripts/84_troubleshoot_llm_ext.py`; output `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tiers_ext/tiers_ext_vs_llm_report.md`.

**Result.** Routine @ T3-noattr: 68.1 min / 89% accuracy / 0.3% wrong-and-confident. For reference on this same sample: LLM @ T2 76.2 min / 82% / 16.1%; LLM @ T3 75.9 min / 79% / 5.0%; routine @ T2 92.0 min / 84% / 0.3%; routine @ T3 57.9 min / 87% / 0.3%. **Point estimates favor T3-noattr over both language-model pilots on every axis** (vs LLM@T2: -8.2 min/-11%, +6.9 accuracy points, -15.8 wrong-and-confident points; vs LLM@T3: -7.8 min/-10%, +9.8 accuracy points, -4.8 wrong-and-confident points) **but on this 30-visit sample neither difference clears Addendum 21's significance bar (both labeled NEITHER)** -- directionally still ahead, not a proven win the way full T3's margin over the LLM was in Addendum 33. Against the routine's own other tiers on this same world: T3-noattr clearly beats T2 (-24 min/-26%, +4.9 accuracy points, a clean IMPROVES) and is clearly behind full T3 (+10.2 min/+18%, labeled WORSE) -- the same direction as the result above, though a larger gap than the 13% seen on Addendum 27's synthetic building (a different world and a much smaller sample, so not expected to match exactly). **The by-kind breakdown hides a real split inside the "NEITHER" against LLM@T3:** on ordinary complaints (not shared-cause), T3-noattr is actually SLOWER than the LLM -- single +17.1 min (+51%), multi +27.2 min (+25%), both labeled WORSE -- a real, accuracy-neutral cost from missing attributes (checks that need a documented design value fall back to extra-minute penalties, or a fully unavailable check like `design_load`, when attributes aren't on file). On shared-cause visits T3-noattr is dramatically faster (-150.9 min, -86%, IMPROVES), because that shortcut comes from the shared-parent read, which needs no attribute documentation at all; this one kind swamps the ordinary-complaint cost when the kinds are reweighted into the "all visits" aggregate, which is why the aggregate reads NEITHER despite two of three kinds moving in opposite, real directions.

**Reading.** T3-noattr does not lose its edge over the language model outright once the LLM comparison's own noisier, smaller sample is the yardstick -- it stays ahead on every metric in point estimate -- but it is a materially less certain result than the routine's clean win at full T3 (Addendum 33), and the aggregate NEITHER conceals a genuine cost on non-shared visits that the shared-cause shortcut is currently masking. If most of the visits a technician actually works are NOT shared-cause (single-complaint or unrelated-multi visits are the more common case in Addendum 33's own kind shares), the practical experience of working from a T3-noattr building could be noticeably slower than either full T3 or the language model on an ordinary day, even though the aggregate number looks fine. This does not change any frozen decision (nothing here is registered; it is exploratory, like the note it extends) and needs no new gate, since it reuses two already-verified, unmodified pieces of machinery (Addendum 33's stored LLM numbers, Exploratory note E2's item set) rather than introducing new modeling risk.

**Does tuning `min_rate` fix the non-shared-visit gap? (2026-09-29, user: "how can we make it faster to beat the LLM technician... fine if we sacrifice a TINY bit of accuracy for a huge speed gain. Look carefully").** Motivation: the mechanism behind T3-noattr's cost is now known precisely (checked directly against the primary cell's 500 stored episode traces of `study_tiers_ext.py`) -- without documented attributes, `k_factor`/`max_airflow`/`static_at_box` each pay a fallback-minute penalty every time they are used (42, 66 and 54 uses respectively in the primary cell, vs 0, 0 and 301 at T2 and 61, 215, 50 at full T3), and `design_load` (the cheapest, most direct check for a capacity-related cause) is used **zero** times at T3-noattr (vs 293 at T2, 123 at T3) because it has no fallback at all -- the tool substitutes costlier general checks (`flow_hood`, `ask_occupant`) instead. `min_rate` (the bits-per-minute bar a check must clear to be offered) is exactly the lever that makes the tool reluctant to pay for a check this marginally informative, and it is already a confirmed, shipped lever (Addenda 34-36) for the fully-documented tiers -- so the natural next question is whether it also works here, a combination never tested (Addenda 34-36 only swept T2 and T3). No new engine code: reuses `study_minrate.py`'s sweep unchanged, pointed at T3-noattr. Code: `v2/study_minrate_ext.py`, `scripts/85_troubleshoot_tiers_ext_minrate.py`; output `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tiers_ext/tiers_ext_minrate_report.md`.

**Result.** A fine sweep on the primary cell (baseline `min_rate` = 0.003, 1000 events): minutes fall smoothly (78.1 -> 58.3 at 0.02, -25%) for a small, gently-eroding accuracy cost (82.3% -> 80.4%, -1.9 points) and wrong-and-confident actually improves throughout (1.50% -> 0.87%); the trade-off then worsens sharply from 0.025 (77.5% accuracy, -35% minutes) to 0.03 (68.2% accuracy, -59% minutes) -- a cliff, not a gentle slope, the same shape Addendum 34 found for the fully-documented tiers, just at a somewhat higher `min_rate` (T3-noattr's checks are individually pricier here, so the bar has more room to rise first). **A 5-cell spot-check across a spread of shared-frequency / map-fault / reliability levels (not just the primary cell) confirms the direction and rough size at `min_rate` = 0.02 holds up: 24-31% fewer minutes, a 0.4-2.6 point accuracy cost, and wrong-and-confident improves in every one of the 5 cells checked, never worsens.** Replayed against Addendum 33's own 30 visits at `min_rate` = 0.02: the aggregate becomes a clean, statistically real **IMPROVES over both LLM pilots** (-38% minutes vs either LLM@T2 or LLM@T3, accuracy still higher than the language model's, not lower). **The specific problem the previous note found -- T3-noattr losing to the LLM on ordinary complaints -- is fixed at this value: single flips from +51%/WORSE to +1%/NEITHER, multi flips from +25%/WORSE to -15%/NEITHER** (no longer losing on either kind); shared-cause visits are unaffected (that shortcut never depended on this knob) and remain a massive win (-86%).

**Reading.** `min_rate` = 0.02 is a well-motivated, working answer to the user's question: a real, mechanistically-understood lever (the same one already shipped for the other tiers) that trades under 2 accuracy points for roughly a quarter to a third fewer minutes, closes the specific gap the LLM comparison exposed, and never costs safety across every condition checked. **Named caveat, upfront and not to be glossed over: this is exploratory, at the same evidentiary weight Addendum 34's original `min_rate` lead had before Addendum 35 gave it the full 18-cell confirmation.** The 5-cell spot-check is reassuring, not that bar. If `min_rate` = 0.02 (or a nearby value) is to be adopted as a real default for buildings matching this documentation profile, the honest next step is the same 18-cell pre-registered confirmation Addenda 35/36 gave the other tiers' values -- not assumed here, and not yet requested.

**Further decision taken (2026-09-29, user: "ok, can you set the min_rate to 0.02"): the live v2 tool's default `min_rate` is raised again, from 0.015 to 0.02.** The live CLI (`v2/cli.py`) has only two named tiers, T2 and T3 -- there is no separate "T3-noattr" option -- so this change applies globally, not only to the user's own documentation profile. Two things were checked before making it: (1) **the live tool already behaves like T3-noattr whenever fed a real building file that has control-system points but no attribute data for a piece of equipment** (`DataView.attr()` gates on `"attributes" in items` first, but then reads the real per-equipment dict; an empty dict degrades exactly the same way whether "attributes" is nominally in the tier's item set or not), so for the user's actual buildings (BAS access and a point map, no attribute documentation, per their own report and Gabe Fierro's >5,000 sq ft scoping) requesting tier T3 already reduces to exactly the T3-noattr case tested above. (2) **A direct check of what 0.02 does to genuinely fully-documented T2 and T3 (real attribute data present, primary cell)**, since the shipped default is one number for every building regardless of its actual documentation: T3 tolerates 0.02 about as well as it tolerated 0.015 (a further ~0.6-point accuracy cost, 83.7%->83.1%, consistent with the trend already accepted); **T2 (no BAS access at all) takes a larger additional hit not yet seen at 0.015** (77.7%->75.9%, on top of the cost 0.015 already carries there per Addendum 36's finding that T2 was NOT confirmed at the 18-cell bar). T2 is the tier the user reports as rare in their own buildings, so this is disclosed as a known, accepted side-effect of a global constant, not a validated cost for that case (primary cell only, not the 18-cell sweep). Code changed: `v2/cli.py` (`LIVE_MIN_RATE_DEFAULT` and the `--min-rate` help text), `tests/test_troubleshoot_v2.py` (the default-value test now expects 0.02), `scripts/80_minrate_vs_llm.py` (the docstring's shipped-default comment). `scripts/85`'s own recommendation and every number in this note's Result are unaffected (they already used min_rate=0.02 explicitly, independent of the CLI's default). This follows the same disclosure pattern as the 0.015 decision: the gap is logged so a later reader sees a decision, not an oversight.

**Regression check surfaced a real, disclosed side effect (2026-09-29): the CLI's own headline demo now runs on zero checks.** Running the full test suite after the change above found two failures. One was a stale historical-sync test (`study_minrate2.CHALLENGER` had been asserted equal to the live default; it is now frozen at 0.015 forever, since that constant is what Addendum 36's own stored report is about, and the live default has since moved past it -- fixed by updating the assertion, no behavior changed). The other exposed something real: the module docstring's own scripted example (`--demo --tier T3 --complaints Room-0,Room-1,Room-2,Room-3 --truth low_static,low_static,low_static,low_static`) now performs **zero checks** at `min_rate` = 0.02 -- the shared-cause group-informing prior alone already clears `tau` (96-99%) before any check is offered, so the session states its conclusion immediately. Checked a mixed-cause variant too (3 rooms genuinely share `low_static`, 1 room's real cause is `damper_mech`): also zero checks, and the fourth room is wrongly folded into the shared "low static 96%" diagnosis. **This wrong answer is not new** -- replayed the same scenario at 0.015, and it reaches the identical wrong conclusion after 3 checks, none of which actually probe the fourth room's own box; 0.02 only removes checks that were never catching this pre-existing weakness of the group-informing rule (an unrelated complaint sharing an air handler can be swept into the group diagnosis), it does not introduce a new correctness failure. What IS new: the demo no longer shows any of the tool's reasoning, which matters for anyone using `--demo` to understand how the tool works, independent of correctness. **User's decision (2026-09-29, "its fine, I think we can keep the 0.02 for massive speed gain"): 0.02 stays as the live default**, this side effect accepted knowingly rather than reverted. `tests/test_troubleshoot_v2.py`'s demo test updated to assert the new, correct-for-this-default behavior (zero checks, the known wrong-fold on the fourth room) instead of the old assumption that at least one check would run; its actual point (the `DemoTechnician` states/truths wiring) is unchanged and still checked. Full suite passes after both fixes.



## Addendum 38 (dated 2026-10-08, written BEFORE the live v2 tool has been run on either real case): on two real, narrated technician calls, does the live tool's behaviour look plausible next to what the technician actually did? (retrospective replay of two YouTube videos; n = 2; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** `FIELD_SOURCES.md` (2026-10-08) shortlisted real service calls. Auto-caption transcripts were pulled for four of them; two have a complaint-to-box path the tool can take as input, and the user said "yes, encode those two and run the tool on them": Everything HVACR, "the office and cubicle area are too cold in the morning" (`field_cases/cold_morning.json`; two boxes, tags 206 and 232, one of which was mislabelled) and Nighthawk HVAC EP160, "VAV's Electric Heat Not Heating" (`field_cases/ep160.json`; two boxes, T117 and T212). The other two transcripts (a stuck actuator on a call edited from a repeat visit; a failed static-pressure transducer with no zone complaint) were not encoded.

**What is run.** The live v2 tool exactly as the CLI runs it: `v2.cli.run` over a `JointSession` with `min_rate` = `LIVE_MIN_RATE_DEFAULT` (0.02), drawings `mostly_right`, `f` = 0.03, lookahead 1, `group_informing` off. Tier: T3-noattr (equipment list, claims, BAS access, point map; no documented attributes), because no video shows a design value being consulted. Declared up front: `bas_access=yes`, `instrument=no`, `occupant=no`, and each box's reheat as the video shows it. No `feel` or `airflow` symptom is declared (the live v2 CLI takes neither). The harness is `scripts/88_field_cases.py`.

**Encoding discipline (frozen by hash before the run).** The encoder had already read both transcripts, so it knew each outcome; blind encoding is impossible here. Mitigations: (1) every one of the 30 catalogue checks is classified in the case file before the tool is run, so the encoding cannot be steered by what the tool recommends; (2) **evidence rule:** a check is answered only when the narration or an on-screen reading shows the quantity the check asks about (by any route, BAS or at the device); everything else is answered `unsure` (the check's minutes are spent, nothing is learned); **no answer is inferred from the final diagnosis**; (3) the case files and the building they encode are hashed in `field_cases/FROZEN.sha256` (frozen 2026-10-09T02:58:55Z UTC): `cold_morning.json` 5cc7b228..., `ep160.json` 37d2dcf8...; the runner refuses to run if either has changed. A later change is a logged deviation, never a silent edit. Each answer carries its transcript timestamps and a basis.

**Scaffolding that is invented, not evidenced (stated so nobody reads it as data).** (a) **Padding rooms.** Neither video says how many other rooms share the air handler; the shared-cause belief depends strongly on it (a complaining pair among few quiet neighbours makes a shared fault likely, among many makes it unlikely). The runner adds k non-complaining rooms, k = 2, 6, 12; **6 is primary**; all three are reported. (b) **Air handler.** The cold-morning technician guessed that rooftop 2 serves the upstairs (10:15-10:45); both boxes are encoded on it for that reason only. EP160 never names one; AHU-1 is a placeholder and no air handler is ever read. (c) **Zone and air handler point maps** are assumed present (the premise of the tier). (d) **The mislabel** in the cold-morning call (the unit labelled 206 is the box the BAS calls 250) is encoded as: VAV-206 has no point map, so every BAS read of it costs the catalogue's +8-minute fallback, the nearest the tool has to "find it in the device list"; VAV-250 is on the equipment list because the BAS device list showed it. (e) **EP160's two boxes are encoded as two complaints** (the description says one tenant, one suite, two boxes; the tool takes one room per complaint).

**Accepted causes (frozen).** Cold-morning, Room-206: `zone_sensor_setpoint` or `controller_config`; Room-232: `controller_config` or `zone_sensor_setpoint`. EP160, Zone-T212: `reheat_fail` only; Zone-T117: `reheat_fail` or `other` (its own element worked, a melting wire was found, and the suite was cold mainly because the sibling box was down). **Facts the tool cannot express at all** are recorded, not scored: in the cold-morning call, the rooftop's discharge setpoint on a reset at 59 and 62 against a typical 55, economizers disabled, an outdoor-air sensor reading about 65 against an estimated 45-50, and the heating minimum-airflow setpoint on a box without reheat; and the device-list lookup that exposed the mislabel.

**Measured (per case and per k).** The tool's first recommended check against the technician's; checks recommended, and the share answerable from the video (**coverage**); minutes (all, and spent on checks the video does not answer); probability mass on the accepted causes before and after; the leading cause per complaint; whether each complaint concluded (leading cause at or above `tau` = 0.95 with a real observation) and, if so, whether it was wrong (**wrong and confident**); the technician's mapped checks the tool never recommended, and the tool's checks the technician never did; the air handler belief.

**Predictions (strict; written before any real-case output exists; the only tool output seen so far is the harness self-test on the synthetic example building, where, for two complaints on one air handler, the first recommended check was an air handler static read).**
- **P1 (first check).** At k = 6, in both cases the tool's first recommended check is NOT the technician's (`bas_zone` for the cold-morning call, `bas_reheat` for EP160).
- **P2 (coverage).** At k = 6, fewer than half of the checks the tool recommends can be answered from the video, in both cases.
- **P3 (direction, EP160).** At k = 6, the probability mass on `reheat_fail` for Zone-T212 is higher after the replay than before it.
- **P4 (direction, cold-morning).** At k = 6, the mass on {`zone_sensor_setpoint`, `controller_config`} for Room-206 is higher after the replay than before it.
- **P5 (safety).** No complaint is wrong and confident, at any k, in either case.
- **P6 (k).** The first recommended check differs across k = 2, 6, 12 in at least one case.
- **Reported, not graded:** the cold-morning call's device-list lookup (about 2 minutes in the video) has no counterpart in the catalogue; the nearest doc-checking options are `confirm_acts` (9 min), `override_diffuser` (11), `duct_trace` (25) and `override_room` (38), none of which tells a mislabelled box from a correct one by reading a list.

**Decision (frozen).** *NO RESULT* if a gate fails. Otherwise this is descriptive, with no pass/fail on the tool. Per case at k = 6 the label is *ALIGNED* if every complaint's leading cause is in its accepted set and nothing is wrong and confident, else *NOT ALIGNED*; first-check agreement and coverage are reported separately. Neither label validates or invalidates the tool: two calls chosen after their transcripts were read cannot do that.

**Gates (checked before reading any table).** G1: case-file hashes match `FROZEN.sha256`. G2: the harness self-test passes on the synthetic building. G3: both cases run at all three k with no error. G4: the run is deterministic (two runs give identical traces). G5: every answer in each table names a check that exists in the catalogue and a complaint or air handler that exists in the building.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The encoder knew the outcomes. (2) The scripted technician answers `unsure` to anything the video does not show; a real technician would answer it, so the tool's minutes include time spent on checks that taught it nothing, and the v2 CLI has no `skip` that would avoid that. (3) Evidence classes are judgement calls (for example, the thermostat setting was read through the BAS, not at the room, and one area-level read is counted for both zones); each is stated in its `basis`. (4) The videos are edited, with auto-generated captions that contain errors, one technician each; EP160 starts mid-call. (5) The cases were shortlisted by the encoder after reading the transcripts. (6) The tool's catalogue has no way to take the device-list evidence, the discharge reset, the economizer state or the outdoor sensor. (7) n = 2; padding and the assumed air handler change the shared-cause belief and so the order of checks. Minutes in the videos are for reference only: they omit waiting and travel and are not comparable with the catalogue's.

**Not used:** any LLM to judge or encode, any new download, any fitted quantity, any real number as a probability.

**Status: run (2026-10-08).** Results: `results/docmismatch/field_cases/` (`report.md`, per-run `trace_*.json`, the tool's own text in `cli_*.txt`). Case files unchanged since the freeze (hashes above); runner unchanged since the self-test (`88_field_cases.py` sha256 09be233f...). **Gates: G1-G5 all passed** (hashes match; self-test passes; both cases ran at k = 2, 6, 12 without error; two runs gave byte-identical traces; every answer names an existing check and complaint).

**Registered predictions: all six held; both cases are ALIGNED at k = 6.**

| | Cold-morning, k = 6 | EP160, k = 6 |
|---|---|---|
| P1 first check differs from the technician's | holds: `bas_static` (an air handler read) against `bas_zone` | holds: `bas_static` against `bas_reheat` |
| P2 coverage under 50% | holds: 3 of 7 checks answerable (43%) | holds: 4 of 13 (31%) |
| P3 / P4 mass on the accepted causes rises | holds: Room-206 30% -> 99% | holds: Zone-T212 8% -> 94% |
