| P5 never wrong and confident | holds: no complaint was, at any k | holds: no complaint concluded at any k |
| P6 first check changes with k | holds: `bas_static` at k = 2 and 6, `bas_zone` at k = 12 | holds: `bas_static` at k = 2 and 6, `bas_zone` at k = 12 |
| Label (leading cause in the accepted set for every complaint, none wrong and confident) | ALIGNED | ALIGNED |

**How much that is worth, said plainly.**
1. **P3, P4 and the ALIGNED label are close to true by construction.** The answered checks are the technician's own diagnostic readings, and the catalogue's response model treats them as pointing at the causes he named (`bas_zone` abnormal -> `zone_sensor_setpoint`, `reheat_coil` abnormal -> `reheat_fail`). They show that the catalogue's reading of those observations agrees with the technician's, not that the tool would have found them.
2. **The cold-morning conclusion for Room-206 (99%) is partly an artifact of my encoding, found after the run (exploratory, not registered).** `bas_zone` and `thermostat` were both answered from the same on-screen reading (5:45-6:30), and the tool treats checks as independent evidence, so one observation counted twice. With the duplicate `thermostat` answer removed (in memory; the frozen file is untouched), Room-206 reaches 79% at k = 6 and is no longer concluded (73% at k = 2, 79% at k = 12), and at k = 2 the tool asks for 13 checks and 72 minutes instead of 8 and 34. The registered result above is the frozen one; this is the honest qualifier on it.
3. **The informative findings are about order, coverage and gaps, not accuracy.** (a) The tool opens with an air handler static read in both calls at k = 2 and 6 (neither technician read an air handler first; in the cold-morning call the rooftop was read last) because two complaints on one air handler raise a shared fault; with 12 quiet neighbours it opens with `bas_zone` instead. The first check depends on the invented padding. (b) Coverage was 43% and 31%: **17 of the tool's 32 minutes (cold-morning) and 36 of its 72 (EP160) went on checks the video does not answer.** In EP160 the technician's first check, `bas_reheat`, came 8th of 13, after seven unanswered 2-minute BAS reads; the in-room `thermostat` check (11 minutes, twice) and `reheat_coil` (16, twice) are the expensive ones. Tool minutes and video minutes (about 15 and 6.5) are not comparable. (c) **Documentation:** the tool recommended no check that could surface the mislabelled box and, at every k, no box-level check on VAV-206 (the only recommended checks carrying VAV-206 as their target were the zone reads `bas_zone` and `thermostat`, which read the room; a BAS read of the box itself would carry the +8-minute fallback, and the 'documented box is wrong' prior is small); no v0 check was recommended in either case (verified in the traces). In this call the mislabel did not change the diagnosis, so this does not show the tool would have missed a mislabel that mattered; it shows nothing in the catalogue would surface one at the technician's cost. (d) **EP160 never concluded:** with both boxes' BAS read and element inspection all abnormal, the leading cause stalled at 94-95% against `tau` = 0.95 and the session ended "nothing left worth its time" with the right cause leading; this follows from the catalogue's 0.75 detection rates, not from anything in the video. (e) The line "checks the tool recommended that the technician did not do" in `report.md` lists `thermostat` for the cold-morning call although it was answered from a BAS read of the thermostat setting; it is computed from the technician-sequence mapping, which labels that read `bas_zone`.

**What this does not show.** Whether the tool would save a technician any time (the scripted technician answers `unsure` where a real one would answer, so the minutes are inflated for the tool and deflated for the technician), whether the placeholder rates are right, or anything about calls in general (n = 2, cases shortlisted after their transcripts were read, one technician each). It also does not exercise the tool's own handling of a wrong claim: the v2 CLI has no 'add equipment' path, and the cold-morning mislabel was encoded as an unmapped point map.

**What would follow.** If this is worth extending: encode the other two transcripts and the NEBB and CSE written cases; replace the scripted `unsure` with a real or LLM-played technician who answers only from the transcript (Addendum 33's machinery); and add a catalogue check for 'which box is this, by the BAS device list', which is the single most informative step in the cold-morning call and has no counterpart. None of that is started.


## Addendum 39 (dated 2026-10-08, written BEFORE the competition below has been run): the live v2 tool against the real technician in the video, on the two calls of Addendum 38 -- who reaches a diagnosis in fewer steps, and is the tool right? (retrospective; n = 2; NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** After Addendum 38 (a scripted technician answering "unsure" to everything the video does not show) the user asked for the tool to compete against the real technician instead, "in video or text". This addendum does that on the two calls already encoded (Everything HVACR "cold in the morning", Nighthawk EP160). Text sources (NEBB, ACHR, CSE) are left out, decided before any run: they give no per-step timing and their root causes (a reversed return fan, a minimum-outdoor-air override) are outside the catalogue, so the tool cannot be right there by construction. The stuck-actuator video (C6sMPDEDHEU) is left out too: it is a repeat visit in which the diagnosis was made the day before, so the recorded steps are not the diagnosis.

**The contest.** Same case, same complaint, same information.
- *The technician* is his recorded sequence (`field_cases/compete_*.json`): his steps from the first diagnostic action to the statement of each accepted cause, in catalogue-check units where a step maps to a catalogue check and as one step each where it does not (the device-list lookup, the heating-minimum-airflow read). Set-up, repairs and walking are excluded (listed in each file). Cold-morning: 4 steps (5 if each complaint's check is counted separately), 4.5 video minutes. EP160: 4 steps, 4.25 video minutes. He is correct by construction, because the accepted causes are his own findings; EP160's repair was verified (current restored), the cold-morning fix was not.
- *The tool* is the live v2 session exactly as in Addendum 38 (JointSession, `min_rate` 0.02, drawings `mostly_right`, T3-noattr, same declared context), with one difference: it is also given the symptoms the technician had, `feel = too_cold` in both calls and `airflow = yes` in EP160 ("I've already verified that I have air flow"), which the live CLI cannot take but the engine can. It stops when every complaint is concluded or nothing left is worth its time; its diagnosis is its leading cause at that point.

**The world that answers the tool's checks (frozen).** A check the video shows gets the video's answer (the frozen tables of Addendum 38). Any other check is answered by the **primary policy `model`**: a draw from the catalogue's own response model, `K.p_pos(check, true cause, target)`, with the true cause fixed per complaint (the first accepted cause: cold-morning Room-206 `zone_sensor_setpoint`, Room-232 `controller_config`; EP160 both `reheat_fail`) and each air handler `ok`; 1000 worlds per case, each draw a pure function of (world, complaint, check, target), so the result does not depend on the order of the tool's checks. **This world is home-field for the tool** (it answers as the tool believes checks behave), so a TOOL verdict is weak and a TECHNICIAN verdict is strong. Two sensitivities, deterministic, one run each: `normal` (every unshown check comes back normal) and `unsure` (Addendum 38's rule, now with symptoms). Padding rooms: k = 2, 6, 12 (6 primary), as in Addendum 38.

**Primary metric and decision (frozen).** Steps to a diagnosis (the project's goal is fewer trial-and-error checks), with correctness. Per case at k = 6, with ratio = the tool's median steps over the technician's steps: **TECHNICIAN FEWER STEPS** if ratio >= 1.25; **TOOL FEWER STEPS** if ratio <= 0.8 and the tool is right on every complaint in at least 90% of worlds; otherwise **TIE**. Reported alongside, not scored: the share of worlds in which the tool needs no more steps than the technician; the share right on every complaint; wrong and confident (concluded, leading cause outside the accepted set); concluded; the first check it picks; the checks it asks most often; the tool's catalogue minutes against the video minutes. **Minutes are not scored**: catalogue minutes are placeholders that include walking and waiting, which the videos edit out, so tool minutes (tens) against video minutes (about 4) are not comparable and no pace correction is fitted.

**Predictions (strict; written before any real-case output exists; the only tool output seen is the harness self-test on the synthetic example building, where, with two complaints on one air handler and no padding, the tool's median was 9 steps and it was right in 87% of 30 worlds).**
- **P1.** Cold-morning, k = 6: ratio >= 1.25 (the tool needs 5 or more steps against the technician's 4).
- **P2.** EP160, k = 6: ratio >= 1.25.
- **P3.** The tool is right on every complaint in at least 90% of worlds, in each case at k = 6.
- **P4.** Wrong and confident in at most 2% of worlds, in each case at k = 6.
- **P5.** The tool's median catalogue minutes are at least three times the technician's video minutes, in both cases (reported only, for the reason above).
- **P6.** In EP160 the tool's most common first check is not `bas_static` (Addendum 38's first check, before it was given the symptoms).
- **P7.** Both cases are TECHNICIAN FEWER STEPS at k = 6 (follows from P1 and P2).

**Gates.** G1: the case files (Addendum 38's two and these two) match their recorded hashes. G2: the harness self-test passes (draws reproduce exactly and vary). G3: both cases complete at k = 2, 6, 12 and under both deterministic policies. G4: two runs give byte-identical `summary.json`. G5: no draw raises an error. A failed gate means NO RESULT.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The accepted causes come from the technician, so he cannot lose on accuracy; the tool can only tie or lose it. (2) The world for unshown checks is the tool's own model, so it is kind to the tool; the sensitivities are not. (3) Counting steps in catalogue-check units hides granularity: the technician's one `reheat_coil`-type step in EP160 holds about seven measurements, and his one area read in the cold-morning call speaks for both rooms where the tool pays for each room. The per-complaint count (5 for the cold-morning call) is reported as a sensitivity. (4) The technician brought expertise, site knowledge and (in EP160) earlier steps that are off camera; the tool brought only the declared symptoms. (5) The device-list lookup that took 2 of the cold-morning technician's 4.5 minutes has no counterpart in the catalogue, so the tool cannot take that route. (6) Two calls, shortlisted after their transcripts were read, one technician each; the padding rooms are invented. (7) The tool's recommendations were never shown to a technician; this is a replay, not a field test.

**Not used:** any LLM, any new download, any fitted quantity.

**Status: run (2026-10-08).** Results: `results/docmismatch/field_compete/` (`report.md`, `summary.json`, per-world rows in `draws_*_k6.csv`). Frozen files unchanged (hashes in `field_cases/FROZEN.sha256` and `FROZEN_COMPETE.sha256`). **Logged deviation:** gate G4 failed on the first pair of runs, because one reporting dictionary (`checks_most_asked`) broke ties by set iteration order, which varies with Python's hash seed; every number was identical (compared with sorted keys). Fixed by sorting; two runs under different hash seeds then gave byte-identical `summary.json` and the same numbers as the first run. The runner's final sha256 is 5ac314d5.... **Gates G1-G5 pass.**

**Result at k = 6 (the frozen decision): TECHNICIAN FEWER STEPS in both calls.**

| | Cold-morning | EP160 |
|---|---|---|
| Technician | 4 steps (5 per-complaint count), 4.5 video min | 4 steps, 4.25 video min |
| Tool, steps median (IQR) | 11 (11-12) | 7 (7-7) |
| Tool / technician | 2.75 | 1.75 |
| Worlds in which the tool needs no more steps than the technician | 0% | 0% |
| Right on every complaint | 73% (Room-206 100%, Room-232 73%) | 99% |
| Wrong and confident | 0% | 0% |
| Concluded (reached `tau`) | 7% | 1% |
| Catalogue minutes, median (not scored) | 69 | 14 |
| First check the tool picks | `bas_static` (1000 of 1000) | `bas_static` (1000 of 1000) |

Sensitivities. k = 2 and 12: cold-morning 11 and 10 steps, EP160 7 and 6, technician fewer steps in all. First check at k = 12: `bas_zone` (cold-morning) and `bas_reheat` (EP160), i.e. the technician's own first check; at k = 2 and 6: `bas_static`. `normal` world: cold-morning 21 steps and NOT right; EP160 7 steps, right. `unsure` world (Addendum 38's rule, now with symptoms): cold-morning 7 steps, right; EP160 12 steps, right.

**Predictions.** P1 held (2.75), P2 held (1.75), P4 held (0%), P5 held (cold-morning about 15 times, EP160 about 3.3 times; not comparable), P7 held. **P3 failed in the cold-morning call** (right on every complaint in 73% of worlds, not 90%; EP160 99% held). **P6 failed** (in EP160 the first check is still `bas_static` at k = 6 even with `too_cold` and `airflow = yes`).

**What the numbers do and do not say.**
1. **The cold-morning gap as registered is overstated by an inconsistency in my own frozen design (found after the run).** The world's fixed answer for Room-232 (`bas_zone` abnormal, from the video) points at a setpoint cause, while the sampling truth I froze for Room-232 was `controller_config`, so the world contradicted itself: later sampled answers then pulled the tool to `capacity_design`, `other` and `flow_sensor`. This is the whole cause of P3's failure. With Room-232's sampling truth set to `zone_sensor_setpoint` (consistent with the video; exploratory, unregistered, in memory only): tool median **6 steps** (ratio 1.5), right on every complaint **99%**, concluded **89%**, wrong and confident 0%. The verdict (technician fewer steps) is unchanged; the size of the gap is closer to 1.5 than to 2.75. EP160 has no such inconsistency (its fixed answers and its sampling truth agree).
2. **What the tool does in EP160.** In 80% of worlds it follows one sequence: `bas_static`, `bas_reheat` on each box, `bas_zone` on each box, `bas_airflow` on each box (7 steps). Its second and third steps are the technician's first two steps. The other five are reads he did not make (an air handler static read on an air handler that was fine, and zone and airflow reads on both boxes), and it never inspects the element or connections (`reheat_coil` is asked in 18% of worlds), where the technician found the fault. It stops with a median leading-cause probability of 87% (range 82-98%) against `tau` = 0.95, right in 99% of worlds. Checked afterwards (exploratory): `reheat_fail` already leads in every world after the first three checks (median 75%), and the other four reads raise it to 87% without changing the answer.
3. **First check depends on an invented number.** With two complaints on one air handler and few quiet neighbours the tool opens with an air handler read; with 12 quiet neighbours it opens with the technician's own check. How many neighbours there were is not in either video.
4. **The contest is stacked in two directions.** Against the tool: the technician is correct by construction; his one electric-heat step holds about seven measurements; one area read of his speaks for two rooms where the tool pays per room; he has expertise and (in EP160) earlier steps that are off camera. For the tool: the world answers the tool's checks the way the tool believes they behave, so a TOOL verdict would have been weak, and a TECHNICIAN verdict is the robust one.
5. **The comparator is an experienced technician.** The tool's value has been argued to lie with less-experienced people (the 2026-10-06 and 2026-10-07 signals); an experienced technician's step count is the hardest benchmark, and no novice's step count is in any video.

**Not shown.** Whether the tool saves a technician any time (minutes are not scored and are not comparable); how a real technician would use its recommendations; anything beyond two calls.

**What would follow.** (a) Fix the frozen sampling truth to be consistent with the video's fixed answers and re-register, rather than quote the exploratory 6. (b) If the aim is to cut steps, the EP160 pattern suggests the stopping rule (the tool keeps buying 2-minute reads after the right cause already leads, at 75% rising to 87%) and the opening air handler read are the two places to look; neither is changed here. (c) Compare against a novice, which needs either a novice's recorded call or a field pilot.


## Addendum 40 (dated 2026-10-08, written BEFORE any pace-priced number exists and BEFORE its runner is written): in working time at the technician's own pace, is the tool faster or slower than the real technician on the two calls of Addenda 38 and 39? (retrospective; n = 2; REGISTERED ONLY, NOT YET IMPLEMENTED, NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** Addendum 39 scored steps and left minutes unscored, because catalogue minutes are placeholders that include walking and waiting, which the videos cut. The user then asked "did it beat technician by time?"; an unregistered back-of-envelope (the tool's check mix priced at the technician's observed pace) put EP160 near a tie and the cold-morning call slower, and the user asked for that comparison to be registered properly. This is it.

**The question.** Price both competitors in the same currency, working time at the technicians' own observed pace, and compare: for each call, does the tool take less working time to reach its answer than the technician took to reach his?

**Frozen inputs: `field_cases/pace_table.json`** (sha256 f1608ffa..., frozen 2026-10-09T03:43:08Z UTC), plus the case files of Addenda 38 and 39 (hashes in `FROZEN.sha256`, `FROZEN_COMPETE.sha256`).
- **Currency.** Minutes of doing a check. Travel (access) and waiting are excluded for both competitors, because the videos cut them. The tool's check is priced as a family ratio times its catalogue `do_min`.
- **Two families, one mechanical rule.** A check is in the *BAS* family if its catalogue place is `bas` (the eight `bas_*` reads, `do_min` 2); every other check, 22 of the 30, is in the *physical* family (reference check `reheat_coil`, `do_min` 10). The ratio is the technician's observed minutes over the reference check's `do_min`.
- **Observations the paces come from (all in the file with timestamps).** BAS reads: 0.5, 0.5 (EP160, one box each), 0.75 (cold-morning area read, which speaks for two rooms), 1.25 (cold-morning box read). Physical box inspections: 2.75 and 0.5 (EP160, electric heat at two boxes).
- **Three pace scenarios, fixed now.** FAST = the shortest observation in each family (BAS 0.5 min per read, `reheat_coil` 0.5), CENTRAL = the mean (BAS 0.75, `reheat_coil` 1.625; **primary**), SLOW = the longest (BAS 1.25, `reheat_coil` 2.75). Every other physical check is its `do_min` times the physical ratio (0.05, 0.1625, 0.275).
- **Technician's time.** His video minutes to diagnosis as registered in Addendum 39: cold-morning 4.5, EP160 4.25 (set-up, repair and walking excluded; includes his two steps with no catalogue counterpart).
- **Tool's time.** The live v2 session exactly as in Addendum 39 (T3-noattr, same declared context, symptoms `too_cold` and, in EP160, `airflow = yes`), 1000 worlds per case at k = 6 (primary) and 2, 12; unshown checks answered by the catalogue's own model; the video's answers where shown. Its time is the sum of its checks priced as above, from the first check to its stop.
- **Sampling truth changed from Addendum 39, and why.** Addendum 39 found that its frozen sampling truth for Room-232 (`controller_config`) contradicted the video's fixed answer for that room (`bas_zone` abnormal), so its world contradicted itself and inflated the cold-morning result. The primary here uses `zone_sensor_setpoint` for both cold-morning complaints, consistent with the fixed answers. **That choice was made after seeing Addendum 39's exploratory result (6 steps, 99% right, instead of 11 and 73%); it is disclosed here, and Addendum 39's own truth is run as sensitivity S2.** EP160's truth is unchanged.

**Decision (frozen).** Per case at CENTRAL, ratio = the tool's median time over the technician's time. **TECHNICIAN FASTER** if ratio >= 1.25. **TOOL FASTER** if ratio <= 0.8 and the tool is right on every complaint in at least 90% of worlds. Otherwise **TIE**. A label is **ROBUST** if it is the same in FAST, CENTRAL and SLOW, otherwise **SENSITIVE TO PACE**. Reported alongside: the share of worlds in which the tool is faster than the technician, the tool's time quartiles, and the share right on every complaint.

**Sensitivities (each reported, none changes the primary).**
- **S1, like-for-like endpoint, EP160 only.** The technician's endpoint is a physical finding (the dead fuse, the burnt wire); the tool's usually is not (it asks for no `reheat_coil` in most worlds). S1 adds his physical confirmation, 3.25 minutes (2.75 + 0.5), to the tool's time in every world whose session contains no `reheat_coil` on a complaint.
- **S2, Addendum 39's sampling truth** (Room-232 = `controller_config`).
- **S3, waiting added.** The catalogue's `wait_min` of each check the tool asks, unscaled, added to the tool's time (unfavourable to the tool; the technician's own waiting is cut from the videos).
- Padding k = 2 and 12.

**What I had already seen when writing this (so the predictions are not blind).** All of Addendum 39, including its exploratory consistent-truth result; and an unregistered tally of the tool's check mix in Addendum 39's registered-truth worlds: in EP160 a median of 7 BAS reads and no physical check (a physical check in 18% of worlds), in the cold-morning call 7 BAS reads and 5 physical or in-room checks. I have not computed any pace-priced time for the primary worlds.

**Predictions (strict).**
- **P1.** EP160, CENTRAL: the label is TIE, and knife-edge: 7 BAS reads at 0.75 is 5.25 minutes against 4.25, a ratio of about 1.24, just under the 1.25 cutoff.
- **P2.** Cold-morning, CENTRAL, primary truth: the label is TIE or TECHNICIAN FASTER, not TOOL FASTER.
- **P3.** No case is TOOL FASTER at CENTRAL.
- **P4.** EP160 is SENSITIVE TO PACE: not TECHNICIAN FASTER at FAST (about 3.5 minutes, ratio 0.82), TECHNICIAN FASTER at SLOW (about 8.75 minutes, ratio 2.06).
- **P5.** S1 (EP160): ratio >= 1.25 at CENTRAL (about 8.5 minutes against 4.25).
- **P6.** The share of worlds in which the tool is faster than the technician is under 50% at CENTRAL, in both cases.
- **P7.** The tool is right on every complaint in at least 90% of worlds, in both cases, under the primary truth.
- **P8.** S2, cold-morning, CENTRAL: TECHNICIAN FASTER.

**Gates (checked before reading any table; a failure is NO RESULT).** G1: the Addendum 38, 39 and pace-table files match their recorded hashes. G2: the pricing function is correct on hand-checked toy sequences, and every one of the 30 catalogue checks is classified into a family with a `do_min`. G3: **reproduction.** Under S2 the new runner's per-world check sequences are identical to Addendum 39's stored `draws_*_k6.csv` for all 1000 worlds in both cases (so the new runner reproduces that world and that tool exactly). G4: two runs under different hash seeds give byte-identical output. G5: no draw raises an error; every check priced; the number of asked checks that carried a data-fallback surcharge (`extra_min` > 0) is logged, since fallbacks are excluded from the price.

**Limitations named up front.**
1. **The physical pace rests on two observations of one check family from one video** and is applied to 22 checks never observed (in-room visits, duct tracing, linkage inspection and others). FAST and SLOW bracket only the observed range, not that extrapolation.
2. **The pace is calibrated on the same two calls it is then used to judge**, by design (at his pace), so nothing here is out-of-sample.
3. **His video minutes include talking to the camera and exclude cuts**; the direction of the net bias is unknown.
4. **Excluding waiting favours the tool** on physical checks (S3 shows how much).
5. **His two steps with no catalogue counterpart** (the device-list lookup, 2.0 minutes of the cold-morning call, and the heating-minimum-airflow read, 0.5) are in his time and the tool cannot take them.
6. Everything Addendum 39 already names: he is correct by construction (accepted causes are his own findings); the world is the tool's own model, so a TOOL verdict would be weak and a TECHNICIAN verdict robust; two calls shortlisted after their transcripts were read; padding rooms are invented; an experienced technician is the hardest comparator.
7. A time comparison cannot say how a real technician would use the tool's recommendations, only how long its check mix would take at his pace.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `scripts/90_field_pace.py`, reusing `scripts/89_field_compete.py`'s world, sampling and tool setup so that G3 can hold, adding the pricing, the endpoints, the sensitivities and the report; no change to the tool, the catalogue, or any frozen file.

**Not used:** any LLM, any new download, any fitted quantity beyond the three scenario paces above.

**Status: implemented and run (2026-10-08), after the user said "yes, run the pace comparison".** Runner `scripts/90_field_pace.py` (sha256 9975a82c..., written and self-tested BEFORE the run and unchanged since); results in `results/docmismatch/field_pace/` (`report.md`, `summary.json`, per-world `draws_*_k6.csv`). **Gates G1-G5 pass.** G2: pricing hand-checked, all 30 checks classified (8 BAS, 22 physical). G3: under S2 the runner reproduced Addendum 39's stored per-world sequences, steps and catalogue minutes in all 1000 worlds of both cases (0 differ). G4: `summary.json` and `report.md` byte-identical under hash seeds 1 and 7. G5: no error; asked checks carrying a data-fallback surcharge (excluded from the price): EP160 none; cold-morning 6, 10 and 6 across the primary runs at k = 12, 2, 6, and 594 in the S2 run (Addendum 39's self-contradicting world, where the tool asks for `k_factor` and similar), so S2's cold-morning time is slightly understated.

**Result (primary: k = 6, primary truth; tool working time against the technician's; all eight registered predictions held).**

| | Cold-morning (technician 4.5 min) | EP160 (technician 4.25 min) |
|---|---|---|
| FAST pace | 2.60 min, ratio 0.58, TOOL FASTER | 3.50 min, ratio 0.82, TIE |
| **CENTRAL (primary)** | **4.95 min, ratio 1.10, TIE** | **5.25 min, ratio 1.24, TIE** (knife-edge, as predicted) |
| SLOW pace | 8.30 min, ratio 1.84, TECHNICIAN FASTER | 8.75 min, ratio 2.06, TECHNICIAN FASTER |
| Robustness | SENSITIVE TO PACE | SENSITIVE TO PACE |
| Tool's typical session | 4 BAS reads + 2 in-room checks (6 steps) | 7 BAS reads, no physical check |
| Right on every complaint | 99% | 99% |
| S1: plus his physical confirmation | not applicable | 8.50 min, ratio 2.00, TECHNICIAN FASTER |
| S2: Addendum 39's truth | 9.31 min, ratio 2.07, TECHNICIAN FASTER | same world as primary: 5.25, TIE |
| S3: waiting added | no change (these checks carry no waiting) | no change |
| k = 2 / k = 12 | 4.95 (1.10) / 4.20 (0.93), both TIE | 5.25 (1.24) / 4.50 (1.06), both TIE |

**Predictions.** P1 (EP160 CENTRAL = TIE, ratio 1.235), P2 (cold-morning not TOOL FASTER), P3 (no TOOL FASTER at CENTRAL), P4 (EP160 sensitive to pace), P5 (S1 ratio 2.00), P6 (the tool is faster in 0% of worlds at CENTRAL), P7 (99% right in both) and P8 (S2 cold-morning TECHNICIAN FASTER, ratio 2.07) all held.

**How to read it.**
1. **In working time at his own pace the tool is roughly 10% (cold-morning) to 24% (EP160) longer than the technician, a TIE on the registered rule, not a technician win.** The step gap of Addendum 39 (1.5 to 1.75 times in the consistent world) shrinks a lot in time, because the tool's extra steps are cheap BAS reads.
2. **The label is decided by the technician's BAS-read pace**, which is 0.5 to 1.25 minutes per read in the four observations. FAST to SLOW moves the ratio from 0.58 to 1.84 (cold-morning) and from 0.82 to 2.06 (EP160). Neither call is ROBUST.
3. **On the like-for-like endpoint the technician is clearly faster (EP160 S1: 8.5 against 4.25 minutes, about twice).** He ended on a physical finding (the dead fuse and the melting wire); the tool's session usually ends on a BAS-only answer and asks for no inspection, which the repair needs anyway. S1 adds his own 3.25 minutes for that, so it is a fixed addition, not a priced tool check.
4. **"Worlds where the tool is faster" is not a probability here.** The sampled worlds barely vary the tool's session (85% of cold-morning and 80% of EP160 worlds follow one sequence), so at CENTRAL even the fastest world is a hair slower than the technician (4.95 against 4.5; 5.25 against 4.25), which is why that column reads 0%. The uncertainty that matters is the pace, not the world draw.
5. **Where it would flip:** with 12 quiet rooms on the air handler, cold-morning drops to 4.20 minutes (0.93, the tool faster in 89% of worlds). The number of quiet rooms is not in either video.

**Limitations that carry over (see above).** The physical pace is two observations of one family from one video, applied to 22 unobserved checks (in the cold-morning primary run two in-room `thermostat` checks are priced this way, 1.95 of its 4.95 minutes). The pace is calibrated on the same two calls it judges. The technician is correct by construction and his video minutes include narration and exclude cuts. The world is the tool's own model. Two calls, shortlisted after their transcripts were read.

**What would follow.** (a) More BAS-read timings from the other transcripts already in hand (the stuck-actuator call, EP173, EP52) would narrow the one number the label turns on; a cheap, separate step. (b) In EP160 `reheat_fail` already leads after three reads (median 75%); at CENTRAL pace three reads cost 2.25 minutes against the 5.25 the tool spends. Whether a different stopping rule or opening check would close the gap is a change to the tool and would need its own registration; none is made here.


## Addendum 41 (dated 2026-10-08, written BEFORE any 18-cell run with `tau` changed and BEFORE its code exists): does lowering the conclusion threshold `tau` cut checks and minutes without costing accuracy or safety, across Addendum 30's 18 cells, including the cells where checks are worse than the tool assumes? (simulation only; a parameter sweep of the tool's own engine; no LLM; REGISTERED ONLY, NOT YET IMPLEMENTED, NOT YET RUN)

**Origin.** After Addenda 38-40 the user observed that the tool "is asking for cheap reads to get more confident" and asked whether lowering the confidence threshold would keep it right while cutting checks. An unregistered probe on the two real calls (live v2 CLI path, k = 6, the consistent worlds of Addendum 40, `min_rate` 0.02) found: `tau` 0.90 changes nothing; 0.85 and 0.80 cut steps and time with accuracy unchanged; 0.60 starts to cost accuracy and safety (reference table below). That is exactly the look-then-leap pattern Addendum 35 warns about, on two calls and a world that answers the way the tool expects. This addendum tests the lever where the lead did not come from, before anything about the tool's default is decided.

**What I had already read when writing this.** Addendum 34 (a `tau` sweep on one cell at T2, `min_rate` 0.003): `tau` 0.90 was the largest step with neither an accuracy nor a confident-wrong cost, and going lower traded one away. Exploratory note E1: `tau` 0.95 -> 0.90 saved about 7 minutes at T2 for wrong-and-confident 1.7% -> 2.9%. Addendum 35's exploratory note (3): `tau` 0.90 on top of `min_rate` 0.01 gave the same minutes (76.0 against 76.1) but wrong-and-confident 0.023 against 0.003. All of these were at settings that are no longer the live ones (live `min_rate` is now 0.02, for the T3-noattr profile), none was run across the 18 cells with `tau` moved, and Addenda 34-36 swept the routine (`tool_ticket_memo2`), not the joint session the CLI runs. No 18-cell number at a lower `tau` under the live settings has been seen.

**What changes and what does not.** Only `tau`: **0.95 (baseline: the tool as it stands), 0.90, 0.85, 0.80, 0.75.** The grid is fixed now. 0.60 is not run (the probe already showed it unsafe on both calls). Everything else is as the studies' own `world2.tool_settings()` and the live default `min_rate` = 0.02 build it, unchanged.

**Policies.** **Primary: `tool_joint`** (`JointSession`, `group_informing` off, look-ahead 1): the path the CLI and the real-call replays of Addenda 38-40 run. **Secondary: `tool_ticket_memo2`**, the routine Addenda 34-36 swept, so that results connect to that lineage.

**World.** All 18 cells of Addendum 30 (shared rare/assumed/common x map M0/M1/M2 x reliability R0/R1; R1 is checks worse than assumed, so 9 of the 18 cells are stress cells), the same 1000 events per cell (regenerated from Addendum 30's streams, gated against its stored file), answers from `TableOracle`. **Tiers: T3-noattr (primary; BAS access plus a point map, no attributes: the profile the live default was tuned for)** and T3 (secondary, full attributes). T2 is not run (the user reports it as rare); that omission is named, not hidden.

**Comparison.** Per cell, tier, policy and subset (all, shared, multi, single): minutes per visit, share of complaints diagnosed exactly right, wrong-and-confident rate and checks per visit, each `tau` level minus the 0.95 baseline on the same events, paired, 1000 bootstrap resamples, Addendum 21's labels and tolerances (5% minutes, 0.02 accuracy, 0.02 safety, 95% intervals), reported as shares of the 18 cells exactly as Addenda 35-36 do. **The decision is on the `all` subset.**

**Level label (frozen), per policy and tier, each level against the baseline.**
- **UNSAFE** if wrong-and-confident rises by at least 0.02 with the interval clear of zero (a safety regression) in 3 or more of the 18 cells. Overrides everything else.
- **PASSES** if not UNSAFE, IMPROVES (faster by at least 5% without losing 0.02 accuracy, Addendum 21's label) in at least 15 of the 18 cells, and WORSE in at most 1. These are Addendum 35's bars.
- **NO EFFECT** if NEITHER in at least 15 of the 18 cells.
- **TRADES** otherwise.
- **Stress check on top of PASSES.** A level that PASSES must also be IMPROVES in at least 8 of the 9 R1 cells, WORSE in none of them, with at most 1 safety regression among them. If it passes overall but fails this, its label is **PASSES ONLY WHERE CHECKS ARE AS RELIABLE AS ASSUMED** and it is not adoptable.

**Overall outcome (frozen), read on `tool_joint` at T3-noattr.** Let tau\* be the lowest level on the grid that PASSES with the stress check satisfied. *A LOWER THRESHOLD IS SAFE TO OFFER (tau\* = x)* if one exists. *ONLY WHERE CHECKS ARE AS RELIABLE AS ASSUMED* if the lowest passing level fails the stress check. *NO LEVEL BELOW 0.95 PASSES* otherwise. Every level's label, for both policies and both tiers, is reported whatever the outcome.

**The two real calls (descriptive; no decision rests on them).** For each `tau` level, with the live CLI path at k = 6 in the consistent worlds of Addendum 40 (1000 worlds each): steps, working time at the CENTRAL pace of `pace_table.json` as a multiple of the technician's, right on every complaint, wrong and confident, concluded.

**Reference values from the exploratory probe (the real-call part of the run must reproduce them exactly, gate G5).**

| `tau` | Cold-morning: steps, central min | EP160: steps, central min |
|---|---|---|
| 0.95 | 6, 4.95 | 7, 5.25 |
| 0.90 | 6, 4.95 | 7, 5.25 |
| 0.85 | 5, 3.98 | 7, 5.25 |
| 0.80 | 4, 3.23 | 5, 3.75 |
| 0.75 | 3, 2.25 | 5, 3.75 |

**Predictions (mine, strict, written before any cell has been run).**
- **P1.** `tool_joint`, T3-noattr: `tau` 0.90 is NO EFFECT (NEITHER in at least 15 of 18 cells); it rarely binds.
- **P2.** `tool_joint`, T3-noattr: `tau` 0.80 is at least 5% faster in at least 12 of the 18 cells (all subset, minutes only).
- **P3.** `tau` 0.80 does NOT PASS at `tool_joint`, T3-noattr (it is TRADES or UNSAFE).
- **P4.** If any level passes, it is 0.90 or 0.85; no level at or below 0.80 passes.
- **P5.** The cell-mean wrong-and-confident rate at `tau` 0.75 exceeds the baseline's by at least 0.02 (`tool_joint`, T3-noattr).
- **P6.** At `tau` 0.80 (`tool_joint`, T3-noattr) safety regressions are more common among the R1 cells than the R0 cells (or there are none).
- **P7.** Real calls, `tau` 0.80: both are faster than the technician at the CENTRAL pace (ratio below 1.0) with right on every complaint in at least 98% of worlds (this reproduces the probe).
- **P8.** The routine (secondary) goes the same way as `tool_joint`: at `tau` 0.80, minutes lower in at least 12 of 18 cells at T3-noattr.

**Gates (checked before any table is read; a failure is NO RESULT).** G1: the first 50 events of every cell reproduce Addendum 30's stored kinds. G2: plumbing for the new policy path: `tool_joint` at `tau` 0.95 and `min_rate` 0.003 reproduces Addendum 30's stored `tool_joint` primary-cell numbers at T3 (minutes and accuracy, exactly), and the routine at 0.003 reproduces Addendum 31's `tool_ticket_memo2` numbers within 5%. G3: the routine at `tau` 0.95, `min_rate` 0.02, T3-noattr, primary cell reproduces the E2 note's stored numbers (58.3 minutes, 80.4% accuracy) within 2%. G4: `TableOracle` is deterministic and independent of question order. G5: the real-call rows reproduce the probe table above (steps and central minutes exactly; accuracy to the rounded percent). G6: the whole run reproduces exactly on a second run with the same seed (a three-cell subset, as in Addendum 35).

**Not used:** real data of any kind, SEALED data, any fitted quantity, any download, any LLM call.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) The world is the tool's own model family with placeholder numbers; R1 is one level of degraded reliability, so a pass says little about a building whose checks are much worse. (2) Everything is simulation; none of it is evidence about real technicians. (3) A coarse grid, and the grid and the 15-of-18 bars were chosen before the run but after the probe showed where the interesting range is. (4) T3-noattr is built in the simulation as an item set, with the simulation's own declared context (a gauge on hand, no occupant), not the context of the real calls. (5) The two real calls are where the lead came from and are n = 2; they are reported, not decisive. (6) A pass here would justify offering a lower `tau`, not changing the default: any change is the user's decision, would be made in `v2/cli.py` through a flag as the `min_rate` changes were, and would leave `model.Settings.tau` at 0.95 so that every frozen study keeps reproducing. (7) A lower `tau` and a higher `min_rate` partly do the same job (Addendum 35's note (3)); a combined search would reopen the look-then-leap risk and is not part of this addendum.

**What would follow.** *SAFE TO OFFER*: a candidate setting, with its cell-by-cell cost, for the user to decide on; the real-call numbers are then only illustration. *ONLY WHERE CHECKS ARE AS RELIABLE AS ASSUMED*: a threshold that is a faster mode, not a default, and a reason to measure real check reliability (the pilot's case records) before trusting it. *NO LEVEL BELOW 0.95 PASSES*: the cheap-reads pattern is not fixed by this knob, and the remaining levers are the opening check and a different stopping rule, each needing its own registration.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen (a deviation is logged, never silently fixed):** `troubleshoot/v2/study_tau.py` (case variants carrying `tau` and `min_rate`, the 18-cell x 2-tier x 2-policy x 5-level sweep, comparison, level labels, real-call table, predictions, gates), `scripts/91_tau_sweep.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_tau.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tau/`. No change to the tool, the catalogue, or any frozen file.

**Status: implemented and run (2026-10-09), after the user said "yes, write the sweep code and run it".** Code: `troubleshoot/v2/study_tau.py` (sha256 89031340...), `scripts/91_tau_sweep.py` (c8e27909...), `tests/test_troubleshoot_tau.py` (a6d06f69...; 18 tests pass; 7 of 7 deliberate breaks of the module were caught: `tau` ignored, a wrong unsafe bar, the stress check dropped, the wrong tau* rule, a loosened WORSE bar, a changed baseline, R1 read as R0); all three hashed before the run. Results: `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tau/` (`report.md`, `summary.json`, `cells.csv`, `raw.pkl`, `g3_corrected.txt`). The sweep took about 3 minutes on 8 workers.

**Registered outcome: NO RESULT, because gate G3 failed.** G1, G2, G4, G5 and G6 passed (G2: `tool_joint` at T3 reproduced Addendum 30's stored per-event results exactly; G5: all ten real-call rows reproduced the probe table; G6: three cells reproduced exactly on a second run, one worker against many). **G3 failed:** the routine at `tau` 0.95 and `min_rate` 0.02, T3-noattr, primary cell gave 56.6 minutes and 81.0% against E2's stored 58.3 and 80.4% (minutes 2.9% off, past the 2% bar).

**Logged deviation (found after reading part of the report).** The failure is a mistake in the gate's reference, not in the sweep. E2's `primary_sweep` draws its events from stream `[30, 0, 0, i]` (cell index 0) with the primary cell's world parameters, while G3 compared its numbers with the primary cell's own stream (cell index 6). `scripts/92_tau_g3_check.py` (sha256 1611de55...) reproduces E2 exactly on E2's stream (78.1 min / 82.3% at 0.003 and 58.3 / 80.4% at 0.02) and shows the sweep's code path identical to the old one on both streams; on the primary cell's own stream the same code gives 75.5 / 82.9% and 56.6 / 81.0%, so the 2.9% is sampling noise between streams. The corrected gate passes. **Disclosure:** I had read the gate lines, the overall outcome and the first table's rows before diagnosing this, so deciding to treat the corrected G3 as satisfied was not made blind to the results; no sweep number was changed or recomputed, and the report keeps its NO RESULT banner. The numbers below stand as computed and are reported under this deviation, not as the registered result.

**What the sweep says (tool_joint, T3-noattr, cell means over the 18 cells; minutes, accuracy, wrong-and-confident rate).**

| `tau` | minutes | accuracy | wrong and confident | label | safety regressions (of 18 cells) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.95 (baseline) | 62.7 | 68% | 0.022 | - | - |
| 0.90 | 61.8 (-2%) | 68% | 0.054 | UNSAFE | 9 |
| 0.85 | 55.3 (-12%) | 67% | 0.132 | UNSAFE | 18 |
| 0.80 | 53.0 (-15%) | 66% | 0.176 | UNSAFE | 18 |
| 0.75 | 49.2 (-22%) | 65% | 0.217 | UNSAFE | 18 |

**Every level is UNSAFE, for both policies and both tiers** (the routine and T3 give the same pattern: 0.90 -0% to -2% minutes with 9 or 10 safety regressions; 0.85 about -10% to -13% with 18). The overall outcome by the frozen rule is *NO LEVEL BELOW 0.95 PASSES*. Post hoc, from `cells.csv`: in the nine cells where checks are as reliable as assumed (R0) the wrong-and-confident rate goes 0.008 at 0.95, 0.019 at 0.90, 0.062 at 0.85, 0.084 at 0.80, 0.110 at 0.75; in the nine stress cells (R1) 0.036, 0.089, 0.203, 0.268, 0.325. At 0.90 every one of the nine regressions is an R1 cell and none is an R0 cell; at 0.85 and below every cell regresses. The primary cell (shared assumed, map M0, R0): 58.1 minutes and 0.5% wrong-and-confident at 0.95; 57.6 and 1.4% at 0.90 (not flagged); 53.5 and 4.6% at 0.85; 51.9 and 6.3% at 0.80; 49.4 and 8.8% at 0.75, with accuracy 0.817, 0.817, 0.808, 0.807, 0.790.

**Predictions: seven of eight held; P6 failed.** P1 (0.90 is NEITHER in 18 of 18 cells), P2 (0.80 at least 5% faster in 18 of 18), P3 (0.80 UNSAFE), P4, P5 (cell-mean wrong-and-confident +0.195 at 0.75), P7 (the two real calls at 0.80: ratios 0.72 and 0.88, right 100% and 99%) and P8 (the routine's minutes lower in 18 of 18 cells) held. **P6 failed:** at 0.80 the safety regressions are in 9 of 9 R1 cells and 9 of 9 R0 cells, so they are not concentrated in the stress cells; they are concentrated there only at 0.90.

**How to read it.**
1. **Lowering `tau` buys little time and a lot of confident error.** 0.90 saves about 2% and already raises the confident-wrong rate about 2.5 times, in the stress cells. 0.85 saves about 12% and raises it about 6 times. The probe's picture (0.80 and 0.85 fine) came from two easy calls; across the broad world it does not hold.
2. **Accuracy barely moves; confidence does.** Accuracy falls 0.1 to 3.7 points while wrong-and-confident rises 2.5 to 10 times. That is what a threshold does: it changes when the tool commits, not what it believes, so cases where its leading cause was wrong and it would have said "not sure" become confident wrong answers. This is consistent with Addendum 35's exploratory note (3) and Exploratory note E1, and not with Addendum 34's reading that 0.90 was a safe step (a one-cell result at older settings).
3. **The cheap-reads pattern is not fixed by this knob.** The two real calls' extra steps (EP160: seven reads when `reheat_fail` leads after three) have a different cause from `tau`.

**Not shown.** Real buildings or technicians (simulation from the tool's own model family, placeholder numbers; R1 is one level of degraded reliability); other values of `min_rate` combined with a lower `tau` (Limitation 7: not searched).

**What follows.** No level passes, so there is no case for offering a lower threshold, and the default stays 0.95. The remaining levers for fewer steps are the opening check and a different kind of stopping rule (for example, stopping when further cheap reads stop changing the leading cause, rather than lowering the commit threshold); each would need its own registration. None is started, and no default has changed.


## Addendum 42 (dated 2026-10-09, written BEFORE its code exists and BEFORE any sharper-than-assumed world has been run): if the answers to the tool's checks are MORE reliable than the tool assumes, how much more accurate is it, and does a lower conclusion threshold become safe? (simulation only; the other half of Addendum 41's reliability axis; no LLM)

**Origin.** The user asked: if someone actually answers the computer-made answers (a real technician in place of the simulation), "the accuracy would be much higher, right?" The honest answer was "possibly, it could go either way": the simulated answers are drawn from the tool's own odds for each check, and real answers could be sharper (a dead fuse is near certain, not the 75% the model gives an inspection of the element) or messier (several faults at once, correlated mistakes, causes outside the list). Every simulation so far tested only worse-than-assumed answers (Addendum 30's R1: probabilities pulled toward 0.5). This adds the other direction.

**What changes.** Only the world's answers. The tool keeps its own response model, exactly as now, so it is still **under-confident relative to the world**, as it would be if its placeholder rates are conservative. A world's check is **sharpened by `b`**: every response probability `p` of a check moves a fraction `b` of the way to certainty, `p -> p + b(1-p)` if `p > 0.5`, `p -> p(1-b)` if `p < 0.5`, and a probability of exactly 0.5 stays (the mirror image of `catalogue.degrade`, which moves probabilities toward 0.5). **`b` = 0** is the world as assumed (it must reproduce Addendum 41's R0 cells exactly); **`b` = 0.5** is half the way to certainty (a hit rate of 0.75 becomes 0.875, of 0.92 becomes 0.96, a false-alarm rate of 0.05 becomes 0.025); **`b` = 1.0** is fully reliable: each check answers deterministically according to whether the tool's own model gives it a probability above or below 0.5 for the true cause (then clipped to the engine's rate floor). `b` = 1.0 is a **ceiling**, not a forecast: it says what accuracy the tool's reasoning and catalogue can reach if no answer is ever noisy.

**World and events.** The nine cells of Addendum 30 at reliability R0 (shared rare/assumed/common x map M0/M1/M2), the SAME 1000 events per cell as Addendum 41 uses for those cells (event stream `[30, cell index, 0, i]`; the answers differ only through `b`), so reliability can be compared on identical events. Answers from `TableOracle` with the sharpened checks (identical answers to identical questions whichever variant asks). The policies, tiers, `min_rate` (0.02) and `tau` grid {0.95, 0.90, 0.85, 0.80, 0.75} are Addendum 41's: `tool_joint` and the routine, T3-noattr (primary) and T3. 9 cells x `b` in {0, 0.5, 1.0} x 2 tiers x 2 policies x 5 `tau` levels.

**Comparisons.** (1) **Reliability effect:** at each `tau`, `b` = 0.5 and `b` = 1.0 against `b` = 0 on the same events, paired, with Addendum 21's labels and tolerances; cell means of minutes, checks, accuracy (the exact true cause), wrong-and-confident rate and concluded; accuracy by visit kind (single, multi, shared). (2) **Threshold effect within each `b`:** each lower `tau` against 0.95 on the same events, with Addendum 41's labels scaled from 18 cells to 9 (rounding to the stricter integer): **UNSAFE** if a safety regression (wrong-and-confident up by at least 0.02, interval clear of zero) in 2 or more of the 9 cells; **PASSES** if not UNSAFE, IMPROVES in at least 8 of 9 and WORSE in none; **NO EFFECT** if NEITHER in at least 8 of 9; **TRADES** otherwise. The lowest passing level per `b` is reported (tool_joint, T3-noattr). (3) **What is left wrong at `b` = 1.0:** per true cause, tool_joint at T3-noattr and `tau` 0.95, the share diagnosed exactly and the most common wrong answers, so the ceiling is explained and not just reported.

**Predictions (mine, strict, written before any run; tool_joint, T3-noattr, cell means over the 9 cells).**
- **P1.** Accuracy at `tau` 0.95 is at least 10 points higher at `b` = 1.0 than at `b` = 0.
- **P2.** It is still below 97% at `b` = 1.0 (some causes share a check signature, so perfect answers do not give a perfect diagnosis).
- **P3.** It rises monotonically with `b`, and `b` = 0.5 is at least 3 points above `b` = 0.
- **P4.** Wrong-and-confident at `tau` 0.95 is no higher at `b` = 1.0 than at `b` = 0.
- **P5.** Minutes at `tau` 0.95 are at least 10% lower at `b` = 1.0 than at `b` = 0.
- **P6.** At `b` = 1.0, `tau` 0.85 is not UNSAFE (safety regressions in at most 1 of 9 cells): lowering the threshold becomes safe when answers are fully reliable.
- **P7.** At `b` = 0.5, `tau` 0.85 is still UNSAFE (2 or more of 9): half-way is not enough.
- **P8.** Single-complaint visits gain more accuracy from `b` = 1.0 than shared-cause visits do (shared-cause visits are already the easy ones).

**Gates (checked before any table is read; a failure is NO RESULT, and I will not re-define a gate after seeing a result).** G1: the first 50 events of every cell reproduce Addendum 30's stored kinds. G2: at `b` = 0 every variant (both tiers, both policies, all five `tau` levels) reproduces Addendum 41's stored R0-cell arrays exactly (minutes, checks, accuracy, wrong-and-confident). G3: `TableOracle` is deterministic and independent of question order, and the sharpened oracle is the original oracle at `b` = 0. G4: the sharpening is correct (the identity at 0; every probability stays in [0, 1]; moves toward 0 or 1 and never across 0.5; binary at 1.0; a strong check's hit rate under its true cause rises with `b`). G5: three cells reproduce exactly on a second run (one worker against many).

**Limitations named up front.** (1) It is still the tool's own model family: sharpening is a uniform rule applied to every check, whereas real reliability differs by check and by technician. (2) Real answers also bring what the simulation lacks (several faults at once, causes outside the catalogue, mislabelled equipment, correlated mistakes), none of which a sharper simulated answer represents, so `b` = 1.0 is a ceiling under the tool's own assumptions and not a forecast for real calls. (3) The tool's response model is held fixed; a tool whose rates were recalibrated to the sharper world would conclude sooner, and that is not tested here. (4) Nine cells per level, one sharpening rule, simulation only; nothing here is evidence about real technicians. (5) The result does not change any default.

**What would follow.** If P1 and P6 hold: the tool's accuracy and its safe-to-lower threshold are both limited by the placeholder reliabilities, which makes measuring real check reliability (the pilot's case records) the most valuable next step; a recalibrated model could then be tested. If P1 fails: more reliable answers do not much help, and the accuracy ceiling lies in the catalogue's causes and checks, not in answer noise.

**Implementation, to be written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen:** `troubleshoot/v2/study_rel.py` (the sharpening, the sharpened oracle, the sweep, comparisons, labels, predictions, gates, the wrong-answer breakdown), `scripts/93_reliability_sweep.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_rel.py`; outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_rel/`. No change to the tool, the catalogue, or any frozen file.

**Status: implemented and run (2026-10-09), after the user said "yes, run the sweep with more reliable answers".** Code: `troubleshoot/v2/study_rel.py` (sha256 6ba76618...), `scripts/93_reliability_sweep.py` (592fb3ef...), `tests/test_troubleshoot_rel.py` (8e6989fb...; 18 tests pass), all hashed before the run. A first mutation check missed 3 of 8 deliberate breaks (a vacuous oracle test, a one-level `lowest_passing` test, a gain size that passed both bar values); I strengthened those three tests before the run, after which all 8 breaks were caught. Results: `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_rel/` (`report.md`, `summary.json`, `cells.csv`, `raw.pkl`). **Gates G1-G5 all pass** (G2: at b = 0 all 180 cell-variants reproduce Addendum 41's R0 cells exactly). No deviation.

**Result (tool_joint, T3-noattr, `tau` 0.95, cell means over the nine cells; the same events at every `b`).**

| `b` | minutes | checks | accuracy | wrong and confident | concluded |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 (as assumed) | 63.1 | 11.2 | 79% | 0.0083 | 31% |
| 0.5 (half the way to certainty) | 60.6 (-4%) | 10.8 | 87% (+7.5 pts, all 9 cells) | 0.0056 | 36% |
| 1.0 (fully reliable: a ceiling) | 58.6 (-7%) | 10.4 | 93% (+13.8 pts, all 9 cells) | 0.0039 | 43% |

Accuracy by visit kind at b = 0, 0.5, 1.0: single-complaint 76%, 85%, 93%; multi-complaint 79%, 88%, 96%; shared-cause 88%, 89%, 90%. The routine and T3 show the same pattern (T3 at b = 1.0: 95%).

**Lowering `tau` in each world (tool_joint, T3-noattr).** b = 0: 0.90 NO EFFECT, 0.85 and below UNSAFE (safety regressions in 9 of 9 cells; wrong-and-confident 0.0083 -> 0.062 at 0.85). b = 0.5: 0.90 NO EFFECT; 0.85 and 0.80 UNSAFE (8 of 9 cells), 0.75 UNSAFE (9). **b = 1.0: 0.90 NO EFFECT; 0.85 TRADES (7 of 9 cells faster, 0 regressions); 0.80 PASSES (-9% minutes, accuracy 93%, wrong-and-confident 0.0039 -> 0.0052); 0.75 PASSES (-11%, wrong-and-confident 0.0103).** Lowest passing level: none at b = 0, none at b = 0.5, 0.75 at b = 1.0. The routine and T3 give the same picture.

**Predictions: seven of eight held; P5 failed.** P1 (accuracy rise of at least 10 points at b = 1.0: +13.8), P2 (still below 97%: 93.0%), P3 (monotone, and +3 points at b = 0.5: +7.5), P4 (wrong-and-confident no higher: halved), P6 (b = 1.0, `tau` 0.85 not UNSAFE: 0 of 9 cells), P7 (b = 0.5, `tau` 0.85 still UNSAFE: 8 of 9) and P8 (single-complaint gain +16.5 points against +2.1 for shared-cause visits) held. **P5 failed:** minutes at b = 1.0 are 7% lower, not the 10% predicted.

**What is left wrong even when every answer is deterministic (b = 1.0, pooled over the nine cells).** `low_static` 90% exactly right (mistaken for `capacity_design` 286 times and for `ahu_fan_off` 217), `ahu_fan_off` 92% (for `capacity_design` 194, `low_static` 151), `controller_config` 83% (for `capacity_design` 113, `other` 89), `capacity_design` 72% (for `other` 173), 'documented box is right but the feeder is unlisted' 47% (for 'wrong box'); every other cause 97-99%. At b = 0 the same groups are the weak ones, plus `controller_config` 64%, `capacity_design` 59%, `other` 61% and 'wrong box' 77%, which sharper answers repair.

**How to read it.**
1. **The user's intuition is right in direction and the size is large:** if answers are more reliable than the tool's placeholder rates, accuracy rises about 8 points at half-way and about 14 at the ceiling, and confident errors fall by about half. Some of the 79% (68% across the stress grid) reflects the placeholder reliabilities, not only the catalogue.
2. **The ceiling is 93%, not 100%.** Perfect answers still leave causes the catalogue's checks cannot separate (low static against a stopped fan against a box that is too small; a mis-set controller against capacity or 'other'). That limit is in the catalogue, not in answer noise.
3. **Time barely changes (-4% to -7%).** The tool still assumes noisy answers, so it keeps buying about ten checks. Its model, not the answers, is what holds it back.
4. **A lower threshold becomes safe only at the ceiling** (0.80 and below, at b = 1.0), for 9-11% fewer minutes. At the assumed or half-way reliability it is still unsafe. A real technician is unlikely to be fully reliable, so this is an upper bound.

**Limitations that carry over.** The simulation is the tool's own model family and one uniform sharpening rule, not real checks or technicians. Real answers also bring what the simulation lacks (several faults at once, causes outside the catalogue, mislabelled equipment, correlated mistakes), so b = 1.0 is a ceiling under the tool's own assumptions and not a forecast. The tool's response model is held fixed; a tool recalibrated to the sharper world would conclude sooner and is not tested. Nine cells, simulation only; nothing here is evidence about real technicians, and no default has changed.

**What follows.** The most valuable next step is to measure real check reliability (the pilot's case records), because both the accuracy and the safe threshold depend on it; a tool recalibrated to measured rates could then be tested. The ceiling points to a second lever, a check that separates low static from a stopped fan from an undersized box; that would be a catalogue change needing its own registration.


## Addendum 43 (dated 2026-10-12, written BEFORE any tree was run on any simulated visit and BEFORE any aggregate of a tree exists): is a strong branching expert decision tree already close to the adaptive tool, and does the tool's advantage survive realistic conditions? (simulation only; no LLM in the loop; the only language-model use is two blind authors of trees)

**Origin.** The user asked: compare the adaptive tool with a strong branching expert decision tree for the same VAV/AHU/zone faults ("not a weak flat checklist"), on the exact same cases, check outcomes, check times, fault distributions and stopping rules; report root-cause accuracy, median and mean diagnostic time, number of checks, confident-wrong rate, abstention rate and performance by fault type; stress-test cause frequencies, sensor noise, check reliability and model mismatch; and say whether the tree is already close to the adaptive system and whether the remaining gap is large enough to matter commercially. Every earlier comparison (Addenda 26, 28, 33, 38-40) used stand-ins I wrote, flat checklists, a language model, or two real calls; none used a real branching tree. This one does, and it adds two trees whose author is not me.

**What is compared (the arms).** All on the same visits and the same answer table.
- `tool_joint`: the adaptive tool the CLI runs (JointSession, `tau` 0.95, `min_rate` 0.02 = `v2/cli.py`'s live default at registration, 12 checks per complaint pooled over the visit). `tool_ticket_memo2`: its routine (Addenda 31-36), a secondary reference.
- `tree_E` (mine) and `tree_Ed`: the same tree in two forms. **E (confirming)** declares a cause only on a finding that names only that cause (power missing, signal missing, controller offline, fan stopped, shaft stuck by hand, controller pressure disagreeing with the gauge) or on two different checks that both see it; a finding several causes share, or a diagnosis by elimination, is only a best guess (not declared) unless a second check agrees. **Ed (decisive)** declares at the first supported finding. Both are written in the DSL of `troubleshoot/v2/tree.py` (`tree_E()`), from the STRUCTURE of the guides, not from any rate: ControlsHub 3.2 (a box that does not respond: air handler, controller status, 24 VAC, signal, shaft by hand), 4.2 (a box reading the wrong airflow: reading against setpoint, controller pressure against a gauge, static at the inlet, K-factor, maximum setpoint), 5.2 and 9.2 (airflow at the diffuser; zone schedule and setpoint), the PNNL VAV maintenance checklist, top-down narrowing from the air handler when several rooms complain (the Smart Buildings Academy webinar and the experienced building engineer's own order), and the two real calls replayed in Addendum 38 (zone schedule and setpoint first; a heat stage before the contactors). Its root splits on measured airflow against setpoint (delivery faults against room-side faults), reads the air handler first and once when two or more complaints share it (a `preamble`), uses an air-handler reading already known from an earlier complaint without repeating it, and has separate branches for the three data tiers (an unavailable check sends it down the physical path through the DSL's `na` branch).
- `tree_L1`, `tree_L2`: two trees written **blind** by two independent expert authors: `Explore`-type subagents (no memory index, no repository context) given only `expert_trees/BRIEF.md` (the situation, the 14 causes, the 29 usable checks (the catalogue's 30 minus asking an occupant, who is never present) with their minutes by tier, the DSL, and a persona line: L1 a senior controls-first service technician, L2 a senior hands-on mechanical technician). They were NOT told about the tool, any detection rate, any prior frequency, or any result. Each transcript shows one tool use (the Read of the brief). The trees are frozen exactly as returned (`expert_trees/tree_L1.json` sha256 prefix cd8ef4c2, 66 nodes; `tree_L2.json` 6f984c1e, 59 nodes). They are language models writing as experienced technicians, not human experts. They differ in style: L1 reads the control system first and only walks to condemn a part; L2 treats the control system as triage and confirms with its own meter.
- `fast_parent`: the flat checklist of Addendum 30 (an air handler with two or more complaints first, then each complaint by cheapest-reading-first), to show how much the branching adds over flat. `no_check`: the prior's best guess with zero checks, declared for none (the floor).
- Frontier arms (the tool only, Addendum 30's primary cell only): `min_rate` 0.003, 0.01, 0.04, 0.08 at `tau` 0.95, and `tau` 0.90 at `min_rate` 0.02, to see whether a faster setting of the tool matches a tree's speed while keeping an accuracy lead.

**The same cases, outcomes, times and stopping rules: what that means here, and the one difference that cannot be removed.**
- Same visits: every arm works the same drawn visits (event streams `[30, cell, 0, i]` for the 18 base cells, exactly Addendum 30/41's; `[43-world, 100 + k, 0, i]` for stress worlds).
- Same check outcomes: answers come from one table, a fixed function of (visit, complaint, check, target) or (visit, air handler, check), whoever asks and in whatever order (`TableOracle`'s rule). A tree and the tool asking the same question get the same answer.
- Same times: every minute is the tool's own accounting (access, doing, waiting, fallback minutes for missing data, where the technician is, shared air-handler readings), applied to the trees by the same accountant (`Session` as accountant, as in the stand-ins), and checked by an independent accountant (gate G4).
- Same limit and scoring: at most 12 checks per complaint; a complaint is scored correct if the arm's best guess is exactly the true first-order cause (a box feeder must name the right box); **declared** = the arm commits to the diagnosis (the tool: the leading cause reaches `tau` AND a real observation informed it; a tree: a leaf with `sure` true, or the real-feeder procedure naming a box); **declared and wrong** is the confident-wrong rate; **not declared** is the abstention rate (the tool: unresolved; a tree: an unsure or escalate leaf).
- **The difference that cannot be removed:** the tool stops on a posterior threshold and a value-per-minute rule; a tree stops where its branch ends. I did not give the tree a belief to stop on (that would make it the tool). The frontier arms show how the tool's own stopping knobs move it relative to the trees.

**Worlds.**
- *Base (18 cells):* Addendum 30's shared-cause rate (0.01 / 0.03 / 0.09) x drawings and map faults (M0 / M1 / M2) x reliability (R0 as assumed / R1 = every response probability 0.25 toward a coin flip), 1000 visits each.
- *Stress (26 worlds, from the primary cell: f 0.03, M0, R0; one factor at a time, plus one combined):* **cause frequencies** (the world's true local-cause mix differs from the tool's prior; the trees have none): controls-heavy, mechanical-heavy, zone-side-heavy (each x4 on its causes), flat (every cause equally likely), rare-causes-heavy (other and unlisted x8, capacity x4), drawings poor (30% of boxes wrong); **sensor noise**: control-system reads 0.3 and 0.6 toward a coin flip, field measurements 0.3, the air handler's control-system static sensor stuck at normal in 25% of visits; **check reliability**: every check 0.5 toward a coin flip, false alarms x3, hit rates 40% weaker, checks sharper than assumed (0.5 toward certainty), every check deterministic (a ceiling); **model mismatch**: the world's response table jittered (logit-normal, sigma 0.5 and 1.0, two table seeds each), 10% of (check, cause) signatures lost or invented (two seeds), an economizer fault neither system knows about (the world of Addendum 37 without its checks, f 0.09), **settings the tool is given wrongly**: it believes upstream faults are 10x commoner or 10x rarer than they are, or rates the drawings poor when 5% are wrong; **combined bad day**: reliability R1, 10% air-handler map faults, 15% wrong boxes, zone-side-heavy causes, control-system noise 0.3, jittered table (sigma 0.5). Each has its own event stream and 1000 visits.
- *Tiers:* T3-noattr (control-system access + point map, no documented design data; **the primary tier**, the user's common case), T3 (full data), T2 (no control-system access: the physical order).

**Metrics.** Per arm, world, tier, visit kind (single complaint, several unrelated causes, shared cause on one air handler) and true cause: mean and median minutes per visit, checks per visit, accuracy (visit-level mean of the share of complaints whose best guess is exactly right, as in every earlier addendum; and complaint-level shares), declared-and-right, declared-and-wrong, not-declared; on single-complaint visits the median minutes by true cause. Comparisons are paired on the same visits with Addendum 21's labels (5% minutes, 0.02 accuracy, 0.02 safety, 95% paired bootstrap intervals, 1000 resamples).

**A MATERIAL advantage of A over B (registered; the bars are three times the project's "meaningful" minutes bar and 2.5 times its accuracy bar, because the question is commercial, not statistical):** (a) at least 15% fewer minutes, interval excluding 0, with A's accuracy no more than 2 points below B's; or (b) accuracy at least 5 points higher, interval excluding 0, with A's minutes no more than 5% above B's; or (c) declared-and-wrong at least 3 points lower, interval excluding 0, with A's minutes no more than 5% above B's and accuracy no more than 2 points below. Anything else is not material, including a trade (slower and more accurate), which the value model below prices.

**Verdict 1 (is the tree already close?).** At T3-noattr over the 18 base cells, the number of cells where the tool has a material advantage over tree E: at most 5 = **ALREADY CLOSE**; at least 12 = **MATERIAL GAP**; otherwise **MIXED**. The same count against all four trees at once (material over every tree) is reported beside it. **Verdict 2 (does it survive stress?)** At T3-noattr over the 26 stress worlds: **REVERSES** if tree E is materially better than the tool in at least a third of the worlds; else **SURVIVES** if the tool is materially better than E in at least two thirds; else **ERODES** if in at least a third; else **DOES NOT SURVIVE**. **Verdict 3 (value).** A visit costs its minutes + C_wrong x (complaints declared and wrong: a return trip, a wrong part) + C_undeclared x (complaints not declared: the technician carries on alone). C_wrong and C_undeclared are ASSUMPTIONS, not measurements, so the verdict is read over a registered box (C_wrong 60 to 240 minutes, C_undeclared 0 to 60): the tool is **AHEAD** if it is cheaper than the tree by more than 5% of the tree's cost, with the interval excluding 0, at all four corners; **BEHIND** if dearer at all four; else **DEPENDS ON THE COSTS**; the break-even C_wrong at each C_undeclared is reported. The registered combination is **MATERIAL AND ROBUST** (Verdict 1 = MATERIAL GAP and Verdict 2 = SURVIVES), **NOT MATERIAL** (ALREADY CLOSE), **MATERIAL ONLY UNDER THE ASSUMED WORLD** (MATERIAL GAP, not SURVIVES), or **PARTIAL**. A dollar translation (minutes per visit x an assumed loaded technician cost of $60, $100 or $150 per hour) is an illustration, labelled as such.

**Predictions (mine, strict, written before any tree was run; T3-noattr, cell means over the 18 base cells unless stated; E = `tree_E`, the confirming form).**
- **P1.** E takes at least 10% fewer minutes than the tool.
- **P2.** The tool's accuracy is at least 3 points above E's.
- **P3.** E declares at least twice as many complaints as the tool.
- **P4.** E's declared-and-wrong rate is at least twice the tool's.
- **P5.** The tree captures most of the flat-to-adaptive accuracy gap and is still faster than flat: E's accuracy minus the flat checklist's is at least half of the tool's minus the flat checklist's, and E takes at most 80% of the flat checklist's minutes.
- **P6.** Tree design matters: the four trees' accuracies span at least 5 points.
- **P7.** Verdict 1 against E is not MATERIAL GAP (the tool's edge is mostly a trade, which the material test does not count, or is absent).
- **P8.** The tool's accuracy lead over E is smaller in the two sigma-1.0 jitter worlds (mean) than in the primary base cell.
- **P9.** In the economizer world (a cause neither system knows), the tool's declared-and-wrong rate is below E's.
- **P10.** The value verdict against E is DEPENDS ON THE COSTS in at least 10 of the 18 base cells (there is no cost-free winner).
- **P11.** The tool at `min_rate` 0.08 (primary cell, frontier arm) takes no more than 110% of E's minutes and is more accurate than E.

**Gates (checked before any table is read; a failure is NO RESULT, and no gate is redefined after seeing a result).** G1: the neutral world is exactly the existing generator and answer table (events and tables equal `world2.draw_event` and `degrade_all`; the economizer draw equals Addendum 37's; every mix is a proper distribution). G2: the answer table is order-independent, equals Addendum 41's `TableOracle` in the neutral world, and a stuck sensor is a property of the visit. G3: every tree validates, runs to the end on random answers at every tier, and never spends more than 12 checks on a complaint. G4: the minutes every arm reports (tool, routine, all four trees) equal an independent accountant's total from the checks it actually did, on visits of three worlds at all three tiers (nobody gets a free or a doubled step). G5: the tool and routine reproduce Addendum 41's stored tau-0.95 arrays exactly (minutes, checks, accuracy, declared-and-wrong) in the 18 cells at T3-noattr and T3. G6: the first 50 visits of every base cell match Addendum 30's stored kinds. G7: a second run (one worker against several) of three worlds reproduces exactly.

**What I had seen before registering this (disclosure).** (1) Tree E was written by me with full knowledge of the catalogue (including its placeholder rates) and of the tool; it is NOT blind, and its design may favour or handicap the tool in ways I cannot see; that is why L1 and L2 exist. (2) I had run the tool, its routine and the flat checklist on 60 primary-cell visits for timing (T3-noattr: tool 58.5 min / 80% accuracy / 27% declared; flat 95 min / 50%) and I know Addenda 41-42's tool numbers (63 min, 79%, 31% declared at T3-noattr). (3) During plumbing I looked at the leaf inventory of tree E and four of its traces on primary-cell visits (no aggregate of any tree was printed, and no tree had been run on a visit set). (4) The unit tests found one accounting gap before any result: the real-feeder procedure could exceed the 12-check limit; it was capped. (5) The L1 and L2 trees were frozen before they were run on any visit.

**Limitations named up front.** (1) **Home field.** The world is the tool's own model family with placeholder rates; the trees' authors reason from physical sense, and the world answers by the tool's table. The base-world comparison is the most favourable setting for the tool; the stress worlds show how fast the edge erodes; none of it is evidence about real buildings or technicians. (2) **The trees are not an optimum.** "Strong" here means written from the guides and from independent authors, and the four disagree with each other (P6); no tree was tuned on simulated data, so a tuned tree could do better. A tree that is the tool's own decisions printed out (an upper bound on any branching procedure) was not built. (3) **No symptom information.** Real experts use the complaint story (hot or cold, "cold in the morning", whether air comes out); the simulated complaints carry none for either system, which is a handicap for both and probably larger for the trees' ordering. (4) **Declared means different things** in the tool (a posterior threshold) and a tree (a structural rule); the cost model prices both the same way but the equivalence is by construction. (5) The cost model's two numbers are assumptions; the dollar translation is an illustration. (6) The two blind authors are language models; one read of one brief each; no repeated sampling of the same author. (7) Multi-complaint handling is the tool's and the trees' own (a preamble and a memory of air-handler readings for the trees); neither learns anything else across complaints. (8) Answers are independent given the cause, as in every earlier addendum; real errors correlate.

**What would follow.** If the tool is materially ahead and robust: the adaptive machinery earns its complexity, and the next step is measuring real check reliability (the pilot's case records). If the tree is close and the stress worlds erode the lead: the cheaper path is a good tree (with the tool's few real wins, such as the shared-cause shortcut and the stopping rule, folded into it), and the tool's remaining case rests on the value model and on situations the tree cannot express. If a tree materially beats the tool in stress worlds: the tool's model-dependence is its liability, and robustness (not more accuracy) is the thing to work on. Nothing in this addendum changes the tool, the catalogue, the trees' files or any default.

**Implementation, written and tested BEFORE the run, then frozen:** `troubleshoot/v2/tree.py` (the DSL, validator, machine, tree E), `troubleshoot/v2/study_tree.py` (worlds, stress oracle, arms, comparisons, verdicts, value model, predictions, gates), `troubleshoot/v2/study_tree_report.py`, `scripts/94_tree_vs_adaptive.py`, `tests/test_troubleshoot_tree.py`, `expert_trees/` (BRIEF.md, tree_L1.json, tree_L2.json, the authors' notes); outputs in `results/docmismatch/troubleshoot_tree/`, with the sha256 of every file hashed into `FROZEN.sha256` before the run.

### Amendment 1 to Addendum 43 (dated 2026-10-12; written AFTER a 40-visit plumbing smoke run and BEFORE any full run) and Deviation 1

**What the smoke run showed, and what I did about it.** The plumbing smoke (2 base cells, 2 stress worlds, 40 visits, all arms) printed aggregates for the first time: tool about 69 min / 66% accuracy / 1.2% declared-and-wrong; E 60 min / 46% / 16.7%; E-decisive 56 / 46% / 22.6%; L1 42 / 42% / 26.8%; L2 60 / 40% / 14.9%; flat checklist 64 / 34% / 55%. To see whether a tree was failing from a logic bug or from the world, I ran trees E and L1 on a separate DEV stream (event stream idx 950, never used by the evaluation; 1500 visits; primary-cell world; T3-noattr). No tree was edited as a result. The largest single source of declared-wrong complaints in both is a low-static complaint read as "fan off" (89 of E's 393 declared-wrong complaints, 121 of L1's 535): in the catalogue's table the fan check is abnormal in 20% of low-static complaints, a confusion the tool's model contains and no blind author could know. Next: causes no check at this tier can name (capacity_design, other: a tree escalates, the tool guesses from its prior), and the real-feeder search's false alarms (seven candidate boxes, a 10% false-alarm check each). **Reading: part of the base-world gap is the tool's knowledge of the world's confusion table (home field), not adaptivity.**

**Amendment.** (1) Two more trees, I1 and I2, written by two further `Explore`-type authors with the same two personas, whose brief (`expert_trees/BRIEF_INFORMED.md`) adds what the tool knows: the service-history base rates (from the dev stream idx 950, 3000 visits), the complete response table of every reading check, the act-and-watch rates and the upstream-fault rates. They answer "does a tree that knows what the tool knows keep up?", which separates knowledge from adaptivity. (2) The trees are now `BLIND_TREES` (E, E-decisive, L1, L2) and `INFORMED_TREES` (I1, I2). (3) Verdicts 1 and 2 are also computed against every informed tree, every blind tree and all trees; the headline verdict stays against E as registered; the verdict relevant to adaptivity is against the informed trees. (4) Predictions P12 to P14 are added: **P12** the better informed tree is at least 5 points more accurate than E; **P13** the tool is still at least 3 points more accurate than the better informed tree; **P14** the better informed tree's declared-and-wrong rate is at most half of E's. (5) No registered threshold, gate, world or P1-P11 prediction is changed; E, E-decisive, L1 and L2 are untouched.

**Deviation 1 (at the user's instruction: "I just want numbers, you don't need to run like 1000 trials").** The first full run uses **300 visits per world** instead of 1000 (same event streams, so the first 300 visits of every registered stream; intervals are wider; the 18 + 26 worlds, three tiers, arms, bars and gates are unchanged; gate G5 compares the tool's arrays with the first 300 entries of Addendum 41's stored arrays), and runs **without the informed trees if they are not ready** (they were still being written).
